Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


Our Freedom 


ee a century and a half of American his- 
tory the Fourth of July has been the heyday of 
orators. So much so that the phrase ‘‘ Fourth-of-July 
oratory’’ has come to signify bombast and demagogic 
speech and particularly the excesses of patriotism. 

Although in the past year we have had a maximum of 
noise, perhaps, the shouting has not fallen on deaf ears. 
In a radio address President Roosevelt has said: ‘‘It is, 
I believe, true that the events of the past ten months 
have caused a greater interest in government, the prob- 
lems of government and the purpose of government 
than in any similar period in our history.”’ 

Our grandparents did not know very much about 
their government at Washington, and seldom heard 
from it. When the Civil War was fought there were 
people living in this country who never knew of it until 
it was over. In comparatively recent times there were 
homes for which the arrival of an R. F. D. route was 
the first link with the country as a whole. Slowly, 
through the income tax, through the activities of 
county agents in rural areas, through conscription and 
the Liberty Loan drives in wartime, through federal 
questionnaires and regulations and prohibitions and 
services, one class of people after another came within 
reach of the long arm of the nation, came to know that 
we are all parts of one great whole. 

Now the spread of that knowledge has become in- 
finitely more rapid. You drop into the lunch wagon at 
the crossroads and see an N. R. A. sign showing that 
the man in the checkered apron is one of a national or- 
ganization and is serving his hot dogs under a code. 
In the deep forest you hear the sound of the C. C. C. 
axes. Dwellers in slums must move out because the 
government has financed new building. Farmers must 
agree to grow less, clerks must stop work at closing 
time, shopkeepers must not sell below cost. 

Whether you like or dislike the encroachment of 
government into a hundred departments of life that 
were formerly quite private, it does make our period 
exciting. Some feel that we are losing our freedom. 
Others believe that we are gaining new forms of free- 
dom. Clearly it is a time for vigilance. We shall do 
well to curb our old bad habit of excess in oratory 
and look coolly at what is going on. 

At a meeting in London Albert Einstein spoke, not 
as an American but as a citizen of the whole world. He 
said: ‘‘If we want to resist the powers which threaten to 
suppress intellectual and individual freedom we must 
keep clearly before us what is at stake and what we owe 
to that freedom which our ancestors won for us after 
hard struggles. Without such freedom there would 
have been no Shakespeare, no Goethe, Newton, Fara- 
day, Pasteur or Lister. There would be no comfortable 
houses for the mass of the people, no railways or wite- 
less, no protection against epidemics, no cheap books, 
no culture, no enjoyment of art for all. There would be 
no machines to relieve people from the arduous labor 


2 


.generation ago, is beginning to catch up. And in the 


needed for the production of the essential necessities of 
life. It is only men who are free who create the inven- 
tions and intellectual works which to us moderns make 
life worth while.”’ 

In those words lies a glimpse of what freedom has al- 
ready given us. We still have that freedom. And free- 
dom has still more to give. 

Not freedom to gouge your fellows, to exploit child 
labor, to cheat and steal and kill, not the freedom of 
‘tooth and claw.’’ This is not freedom. It is slavery. 
It was not for this our ancestors fought. 

There is danger in so vast an experiment as this 
country is now trying. Mistakes will be made, have 
been made. Wild steps will have to be retraced. Efforts 
begun will have to be abandoned. New expedients not 
yet thought of will have to be tested. But we must not 
doubt the purpose or lose sight of it, and it is this: 

Freedom for all our people to work, to earn, to eat 
and be clothed and sheltered, to learn and play and be 
healthy, to invent and discover and improve the means 
of living, to develop to the full their own personal 
powers and to share fairly in the good oe by the 
powers of the whole community. 


Forest Values 


IN THE old days the crude law of the man with the 

ax was ‘“‘cut out and get out.’’ More than ninety- 
three per cent of the privately owned forests east of the 
Rockies was cut over. Great portions of these lands 
were left bare, without new growth and unfit for any 
other use. West of the mountains the tendency has been 
in the same direction, though the damage has not gone 
so far. Now the slow march of conservation, begun a 


new code of the lumbermen there is an almost revolu- 
tionary clause. E. A. Sherman, of the government forest 
service, points out that ‘‘for the first time the industry 
as a whole has publicly committed itself to a policy of 
conservation and sustained yield of its basic resource, 
tke forest. Henceforth forest destruction is to be recog- 
nized by the industry as an unfair practice.”’ 

Every lumberman well knows that it is to his own 
interest to keep the forests alive, but as in so many other 
industries, the individual has had to be ruthless in the 
face of unchecked competition. Now the code gives 
him the chance to cut wisely and with decent regard for 
the future. And the public interest is involved for 
forests, as Mr. Sherman says, ‘‘ serve to ameliorate the 
climate. They hold the soil on hills and mountainsides 
and along streams. They modify extremes of run-off 
and protect the sources of water for domestic use, ir- 
tigation, power and navigation. They furnish a habi- 
tat for multitudinous species of birds, game animals, 
fur bearers and other wild life.’’ These are their 
services also.

Attractive wives whose husbands seem always 
to be attentive ... stunning young enchantresses 
ever able to captivate men... mature women 
whose many friends look forward to their pres- 
ence... what is their secret? Such women, you 
will almost invariably find, give as much attention 
to their breath as they do to their hair, their 
skin, their teeth, and their clothes. Never would 
they be guilty of halitosis (unpleasant breath). 
Conscious always of the possibility of offending 
others, they regularly take this simple and sensible 
precaution: Listerine, the safe antiseptic with the 


pleasant taste. Lambert Pharmacal Co.,St.Louis,Mo.

Rural Homes 


MRS. FRANKLIN D. ROOSEVELT 


‘KI RECEIVED an invitation the other day 
from some farm women in one of our mid- 
western states asking me to come and visit in their 
“poor homes.’’ The implication was that I per- 
haps did not understand what hardships the farm 
women of this country were going through. 

Perhaps many people throughout the country 
do not Paden what rural life means for the 
women in the farm homes. 

I was brought up and spent six months of every 
year in a farm community in New York State and 
I have been on farms in different parts of this 
country and I think I can give a picture which will 
perhaps not be absolutely accurate for every 
section but which will have some elements com- 
mon to different parts of the country. 

In New York State the well-to-do farmer who 
has a good orchard or a fine dairy or grows some 
specialty for which he has a steady market lives 
fairly comfortably. His house has many modern 
improvements and he usually has running water 
in the house, a bathroom and electric light. His 
barn is often equipped with electricity before his 
house is, the water is pumped by electricity and 
he probably has a furnace, sometimes an oil 
furnace. Much of his farm machinery is of the 
modern labor-saving type and a washing machine 
will often be found in the kitchen, as well as an 
electric iron and other conveniences for the house- 
wife. It is from this type of farm that the master 
farmers are picked. Every state which has any 
master farmers will know, I feel sure, what I 
mean when I say that at the annual dinner at 
Cornell when the awards are made, I always have 
a sense of great pride in our agriculture and the 
fine men and women who make a go of it. 


Sie THE other side of the picture, you will find 
the farmer on some marginal land who cannot 

A F carter 
produce enough to feed his family or to nourish his 
few scrawny head of cattle, whose house has few 
conveniences, whose barn is much in need of re- 
pair, whose children are unkempt and unwashed 
and miserably dressed, who tills an unproductive 
soil and who tries to eke out a miserable living by 
working part of the year on roads or at any- 
thing he can get to do in the neighborhood. His 
wife is old before her time, she has no medical 
care, her work is hard and unending and her life 
drab beyond description. This type of farmer 
either lives on a rented farm or carries a mortgage 
on his property so big that he usually loses it in 
hard times. 

Of course one of the dangers of well-to-do 
farmers is that they will buy additional land when 
farm products are selling high and then, when 
prices go down, they too are left with heavy mort- 
gages which sometimes will entail the loss of a 
very good farm. I think the past years, however, 
have taught many farmers that it is better to 1n- 
crease productivity of the soil and not to buy 
much land which they cannot pay for in cash. 

Nowadays farming has become more of a part- 
nership than it used to be. This has come about 
in places where women have become an asset in 


the earning power of the farm. I know of one 
farm in New York State where the woman runs 
the apple orchard entirely; I know of others 
where the chicken and egg business and sometimes 
even the vegetables and all fresh produce as well as 
canned foods are part of the woman's profit from 
the farm and are sold either at the stand on the 
road or to customers that she can find in the 
neighboring district. 

But at best, when the farmer is intelligent and 
enterprising, there are certain things that make 
the business of farming difficult and the woman's 
lot is far from easy. 


yore THE west, farming is done on a larger scale 
than in the east and usually a farm has but one 
big crop—wheat, cotton or cattle, as the case may 
be—so prosperity hangs entirely on the success of 
that particular crop and the price obtained for it. 
In the east where farms are smaller one great 
difficulty is frequently the lack of codperation 
among farmers and the old individualistic spirit 
which will make every farmer along a given road 
put his own produce out for sale and sell it in com- 
petition with every other farmer along that same 
road instead of combining both on selling and on 
the necessary purchases which every one of them 
must make. 

Where farmers have formed codperatives you 
can almost always see improvement in the neigh- 
borhood. But the rugged individualism we hear 
so much vaunted is exemplified in many of our 
farmers in the eastern states especially, and its 
drawbacks may be studied especially as you get 
away from the main arteries of traffic. 

The farmer has been expected to work success- 
fully with his hands and at the same time be a 
clever and successful business man seeking markets 
for his produce, watching prices to buy when 
things were low and knowing how to hold his 
products until the price was high. Sometimes | 
think that when we developed this complicated fi- 
nancial and industrial civilization we forgot what 
a tremendous added burden we were putting on 
the farmers. We certainly did not put enough 
emphasis in our rural schools on the type of edu- 
cation which our countrymen should have if they 
were successfully going to carry on the most 
difficult business we have in the country today. 
There are successful farmers but they are nearly 
always large-scale farmers and men with some 
capital, who have been able to try an occasional 
experiment, who have been able to take advantage 
of all the education available, and who know 
what the government, both state and national, 
can do for them. Frequently the men and women 
who need the help that the government offers are 
at a loss to find out how to get it. 

I look forward hopefully to the revival of handi- 
craft work which is coming gradually in many 
rural districts because I feel that it will add much 
interest to community life in the development of 
artistic appreciation and pleasure in handicraft 
projects. With the radio and the automobile and 
the telephone the loneliness which was one of the 


Pine 


ILLUSTRATOR : 
ee ALAN CRANE 


hardships of farm life can be removed. If we can 
make farming sufficiently profitable to afford 
security, if we can bring rural life into contact 
with the outside world and supply that element 
of adventure which everyone must have at times 
to keep life from becoming too dull a routine, 
then I believe more and more people will turn 
with pleasure to rural life. 

If we can make the rural school of the future a 
really useful community center as well, we shall 
be on the road toward making rural life really pay 
dividends not only in money but in other types of 
satisfactions now almost forgotten because the 
hardships are usually uppermost in our minds. 

Anyone who has lived ih the country knows 
that there is nothing comparable to the feel of the 
early hours of the morning, or to the view of a 
sunset over a familiar landscape, and these are joys 
that nourish the soul. The contacts of simple 
living give one the real values in life, but people 
must have economic security. We are largely a 
country still of open spaces, and rural homes: let 
us give careful thought to every measure which 
may affect these homes. But the success of these 
measures depends on the thought and the work of 
rural people themselves who must in turn learn to 
understand the needs not only of their own com- 
munities but of the urban communities, as the 
prosperity of both is closely tied together.

JULY 1934 


YOUNG Mrs. Matthew Farrell’s suspicion that 
€Y she was not going to have a good time over this 
fateful week-end became a definite conviction just before 
leaving home. Quite early in the morning Matthew's 
tortured anticipation began to wear her down. 

For the fifth time he asked her jerkily, ‘Did you put 
in both the bathing suits?. And the citronella? And that 
inner tube I bought for you? I don’t want to be fishing 
you out water-logged all the time.” 

“All packed,’’ said Mrs. Farrell briefly. She sank 
down upon a suitcase. “‘I think I’m not going,’’ she 
said hollowly. “‘All night I had premonitions. I 
dreamed of white mice. I can’t swim and if I go I'll 
drown. And you'd rather I’d stay home than go and 
drown, wouldn’t you?”’ . 

‘‘No,”” said Matthew unthinkingly. The very young 
Mrs. Farrell's lips quivered. Rather hastily he added, 
“Anyway I’m going to teach you to swim this time.” 


“You can’t,’’ said Binks gloomily. “‘No one can. 
People have tried for years. But there’s something in 
my legs or some place. So I think I won’t go.”’ 

Matthew, not listening, opened out on his bureau one 
of those wonderful leather things girls give men for 
Christmas. He fitted his razor into the razor compart- 
ment and his toothbrush into the toothbrush compart- 
ment. 

Binks watched him moodily. 

Wouldn’t you think, she inquired silently, that an 
advertising man, after years of studying human psy- 
chology, would know better than to flaunt before his 
bride of only eight months a present out of his girl-in- 
fested past? 

“T bet she could swim,’’ muttered Binks darkly. 

‘“Who?”’ inquired Matthew vaguely. 

“The girl who gave you that leather thing. I bet she 
was a regular Eleanor Holm.”’ 

“*Sylvia,”” murmured Matthew zippering the thing 
shut. “‘Name’s Sylvia. Anyone,’’ he added testily, 
‘can learn to swim. What you need is—’’ 

‘Don’t say it,’’ Binks warned him. ‘‘The last time 
anyone said that to me something in me begged me to 
scream. And if you’re going down to Dune Highlands 
just to swim and see a man about a soup I think I won’t 


go 

pee REITERATION of this statement began to 
take on some of the attention-getting character of 

a slogan. As a member of the advertising firm of Mercer 

and Farrell, Matthew was sensitive to slogans. 

He looked upon her grimly. 

‘‘T suppose your being grown up enough to be married 
and all,”’ he said, ‘‘makes people expect too much of 
you. They ought to remember you are only twenty 
years old. You couldn’t, I guess, be expected to realize 
the importance of getting to know Tyler Howard.”’ 

Binks sighed. ‘‘I don’t believe Tyler Howard exists. 
He's just a voice sending out word he isn’t interested 
and how can he tell he isn’t interested if he doesn’t 
let you show him the memorandum?” 

‘““Now,”’ said Matthew witheringly, ‘‘we are getting 


She saw Sylvia smile and close her 


arn still more closely around his neck 


somewhere. After this week-end with the Gerards at 
Dune Highlands we'll have met the man, eaten with 
him, swum with him—we'll know him and he can’t re- 
fuse, the next time I try to get into his office, to see me. 
All you have to do is keep mum and be decorative—"’ 

“Say that again,"’ said Mrs. Farrell hungrily. *‘The 
part about being decorative.”’ 

But Matthew, his jaw set, his eyes fevered with dog- 
ged ambition, was pacing the rug. 

“Tf just once—just one single time,’’ he muttered, 
‘Tyler Howard reads that memorandum on his entire 
line of soups I'll have his advertising. And then the 
depression will be over and it will be 1928 again.”’ 

“T wish,”’ Binks said wist- 
fully, ‘I'd been married to you 
in 1928. Maybe I'd have been a 
well-kept bride, lolling around 
being worshiped like Madame

wouldn't have had to try things

“T told you,’’ snapped Matthew, ‘it was part of my

well as a general test of the fundamental salability of a

““You couldn’t have,’’ admitted Binks. ‘‘Not even

If I'd been married to you ten years, I couldn't be

‘“We ought to be leaving in about ten minutes,”’ said 
Matthew. 


ers GLOW of an August sun lighted the path of 
their ancient ferry to Dune Highlands. Matthew 
relaxed in its warmth and held Binks’ hand and she felt 
wonderfully cheered. 

But not for long. Fred and Laura Gerard, with a 
tall sun-gold girl she had never seen before, met them at 
the wharf. 

‘‘T wonder,”’ mused Binks, suddenly depressed, *‘why 
people who are not tanned always feel inferior to people 
who are.” 

The tall girl, who wore a bathing suit under a beach 
robe, was pumping Matt’s hand fervently. 

“It’s wonderful seeing you again,’’ she said devour- 
ing him whole with her gray-green eyes and her flashing 
smile. 

“*Great,’’ agreed Matthew. He remembered Binks. 
““M'wife,”’ he said. ‘Sylvia Karnan.”’ 

“You cute little thing,’’ said Sylvia shaking hands 
and Binks felt at once like a six-year-old girl who tooka 
four-year-old size in everything. A pale little girl too, 
of a shade resembling the under side of a mackerel. In 
her mind she was seeing Matt stowing his razor into a 
leather case given him by a girl named Sylvia. More 
than ever she knew she was not going to have a good 
time. 

‘Tyler Howard,”’ Fred was telling Matt as they 
walked toward the cottage, “‘isn’t here yet. He wasn’t 
sure what time he'd arrive so we're not to meet him. 
And I did what you said, Matt—didn’t mention any 
business angle—"’ °

A Sense 
of Security 


uA AS WE cast about for an explanation of some of the 
(VY puzzling things that our children do we are con- 
fronted with the fact that every child has certain innate 
cravings, or urges, that greatly affect his conduct. One 
of these basic urges—drives, or trends, the psychologist 
calls them—is the child's desire to be physically com- 
fortable; not hungry nor fatigued not in pain, neither 
too hot nor too cold. Another is his craving for recog- 
nition by his associates and for respectable standing in 
the group to which he belongs. Another one yet is the 
wish for achievement, success and mastery, that builds 
up and gratifies his ego 

There is still another of these fundamental urges that 
is one of the most potent of them all. This is the crav- 
ing for a sense of security; for the assurance of being 
loved and desired by the rest of the family; for the free- 
dom from anxiety for the future as well as the present 
that goes with the confidence that things are all right 
and quite as they should be. Lacking this sense of 
security, any child is bound to be wretched, no matter 
what other ‘good things he may possess. 

These urges are not confined to children. We all 
know that men will endure fatigue and hardship, risk 
danger and death, in order to be secure and happy in 
their homes. They will make great sacrifices in a to 
purchase the economic security for themselves and their 
loved ones that life insurance gives. Confronted with 
the destruction of this sense of security in their homes 
through the loss of employment, the sweeping away of 
accumulated savings or the betrayal of their love and 
trust, we have seen some men lose their reason, while 
others have actually taken their own lives. 


‘J NOW WHEN a child is deprived of this sense of 
security he does not become insane, nor does he take 
ba own life, except in rare instances. Instead, he is prone 
to experience a strange impulsive craving to commit 
some form of wrong-doing, which he and his parents 
never connect with the emotional shock he has suffered. 
This conduct is particularly puzzling because he 
seems to derive little or no satisfaction from it. He may 
even admit that it is making trouble for himself as well 
as for his parents; yet he is driven to continue it just so 
long as he suffers from the lack of security that brought 
it about. 

Here for instance is a girl who suddenly began to 
steal, bringing grief and humiliation to parents whom 
she loved and nothing but trouble to herself. No cause 
could be found for this pilfering, which frequently con- 
cerned articles for which she had no use. Sympathetic 
study finally traced the cause to her recent discovery 
that she was not the actual daughter of her supposed 
parents, but an adopted child—a discovery, by the way, 
always fraught with grave emotional peril unless dealt 
with in the wisest manner. 

And here is a boy, previously quite tractable, who 
suddenly began to be mischievous at school and un- 
manageable at home. It required patience and keen 
powers of deduction to relate this misconduct to the 
development of serious friction between the boy's 
parents. This had recently come to the stage at which 
they were openly discussing a legal separation. 

These two children were suffering from damage, 
either done or threatened, to their secure status in the 
home. Yet it required experience with the devious ways 
that these frustrated urges take, to discover what was 
wrong. Boarding-school teachers recognize and dread 
the conduct disorders presented by children from homes 
broken by divorce and hence offering no security. 

But even in the home where the child is assured of 
love and affection, he may suffer from a feeling of econo- 
mic insecurity through ‘hearing financial worries dis- 
cussed. Children take things literally and can paint 
very gloomy pictures indeed when we thoughtlessly 
furnish them the pigments. The little daughter of a 
clergyman of comfortable means said to her school- 
teacher just before Christmas: ‘‘I shall be thankful if I 
get a doll’s comb in the toe of my stocking, we have so 
many poor people in our church to be taken care of.” 

Her fear was groundless, as a wealth of presents on 
Christmas morning showed; yet it was the result of 


9 


FRANK HOWARD 
RICHARDSON, M.D. 


ILLUSTRATOR: 
EDWIN A:GEORGI 


what she had heard discussed at the table. Finances 
should be discussed freely; for if there is need for 
economy, the children ought to help in the saving. But 
the need should not be exaggerated; and there should 
never be an emotional tone of worry and apprehension. 
Children face financial reverses with remarkable poise 
if they do not see their parents overwhelmed by them. 

A family discussion of the failure of the authorities to 
punish crime has given rise to a feeling of personal in- 
security in many sensitive children. The moving pic- 
ture, with its glorification of the gangster and the 
racketeer, has enhanced this feeling and a more careful 
selection of the films our children see is most desirable. 
Sometimes a frank explanation of the small percentage 
that unpunished crimes bear to the vast number that 
are promptly detected and eventually punished will do 
much toward reestablishing the sense of security that 
comes from confidence in society's ability to give pro- 
tection through the forces of law and order. 


THE THOUGHTFUL child finds the threat of 
f Y impending war, made more vivid by the newsreel 
with its close-ups of rioting crowds and demonstrations 
threatening existing governments, a most disturbing 


threat to his sense of security, 


War used to be a romantic happening, remote in time 
and place, with gaudy uniforms and gayly marching 
bands, which affected the soldier alone. The World 
War changed all this and showed what a terrifying un- 
romantic dirty business war has become, involving 
civilians as well as soldiers, women and children as w ell 
as men. And we have been willing to have screen and 
fiction keep this conception before our children with the 
laudable hope that it may discourage future war-mak- 
ing. Yet we are constantly being warned that another 
war is imminent; and many a child shivers with dread 
over the thought of the horrors he has seen so vividly 
portrayed. 

A new factor that threatens the sense of security of 
our older boys and girls today is the uncertainty of their 
economic future. College and professional school train- 
ing, once taken for granted, has in many cases become 
impossible. And even those young people whose 
parents can afford such training see little prospect of 
earning a living at the end of their period of study. For 
vast numbers of them this means an indefinite postpone- 
ment of marriage. 

How much of the lowering of youth's moral stand- 
ards, how much of the nomadism of adolescent hoboes, 
how much of the restlessness and inability to settle 
down to any sort of work is due.to this dread of an 
economically insecure future, it would be difficulr to 
say. We do know that unémployed leisure has a dev- 
astating effect; yet society, has as yet evolved no ade- 
quate plan for utilizing the time of its idle young people. 


&K\, WHETHER or not home study, or the development 

\ hobbies, or such*voluntéer social work as Boy or 

Girl Scouting, or settlement teaching, is anything more 
than cold comfort for a hopeless situation, can be argued 
either.way. Yet nothing more constructive seems to 
have been suggested forus to do until the hoped-for re- 
turn.of betrér times restores the sense of a secure future. 

How can we parents combat this frustration of a 
fundamental urge that the psychologists tell us is re- 
sponsible for so much misconduct in our children? Not 
by cloistering them, even if we could do this; for they 
would succumb to the first attack as soon as our shelter- 
ing influence.was withdrawn. We can meet the situa- 
tion in no other way. than by building up such a strong 
sense of security in the home that it will ward off and 
deflect all these outside adverse forces. 

Nothing can take the place of the home where real 
love is the dominant force—love between parents and 
love for each child, not silly and affected but deep and 
strong. Give the child the sense of security that ema- 
nates from this as a center and threats of the sort we have 
been describing are powerless. After all, nothing can 
take the place of the genuine fine American home as a 
builder of sound conduct in America’s boys and girls, 
through the creation of an unassailable sense of security.

CHARLIE CHAPLIN and 
Mickey Mouse are the only 
two universal characters that 
have ever existed. The greatest 
kings and conquerors, gods and 
devils, have by comparison been 
local celebrities. Mickey's do- 
inain is today even more exten- 
sive than Chaplin's. Charlie's 
mustache, hat, pants, shoes and 
cane belong to western civiliza- 
tion and make him a foreigner 
in some regions. Mickey Mouse 
is not a foreigner in any part of 
the world. 

Mickey’s public extends to 
and beyond the frontiers of 
civilization. He has for ex- 
amplé.an Eskimo following in 
Alaska. Douglas Fairbanks on 
his world tour endeared himself 
to cannibals and head-hunters 
by showing them movies of 
Mickey Mouse. Tribes of Kaf- 
firs in South Africa recently 
declined to accept any cakes of 
soap which did not bear the 
image of Mickey Mouse, just as 
formerly they declined to accept 
coins which did not bear the 
image of Queen Victoria. Tribes 
of connoisseurs in the big cities 
have taken up Mickey Mouse 
and lorgnettes are focused on 
original drawings of him on the 
walls of fashionable galleries. 


MICKEY has received 
(VY greater honors abroad than 
at home. He was recently created 
a citizen of France at a carnival 
in Granville. His fifth bitthday, 
on September twenty- -eighth 
last, was almost unnoticed 
in America, but was the occa- 
sion of impressive column-long 
editorials in newspapers in Eng- 
land. Mickey's greatest tri- 
umph in Germany was Hitler's 
denunciation of young Nordics 
who were wearing Mickey 
Mouse emblems instead of the 
swastika. Since Stalin ordered 
the young people to laugh and 
be gay, Mickey Mouse films 
have been smuggled into Russia 
in considerable numbers, al- 
though there is no authorized 
distributing agency there. There 
are theaters in Sweden and in 
India which run seven” or 
eight Mickey Mouse epics one 
after the other as their main pro 
grams. The animated cartoon 
is the life of the movie business 
in Jepan where the feature pic 
ture is often cut to pieces be- 
cause of the prejudice of the 
Orientals against scenes where 
lovers are shown frying in the 
deep fat of passion. 

The little hero is billed as 
Miki Kuchi in Japan, Michel 
Souris in France, Miguel Raton- 
cito in Spain and Micky Maus 
in Germany. The number of ad- 
missions to theaters where 
Mickey Mouse played Jast year 
is calculated at four hundred 
and sixty-eight million. King 


George, Queen Mary, Mussolini and President 
Roosevelt are among his fans. More than six 
hundred manufactured articles ranging from 
doormats to jewels bear his portrait. The Inger- 
soll factory turned out more than a million 
Mickey Mouse watches in eight months but 
was unable to keep up with thedemand, Mickey, 
like Chaplin, was discovered by the mob before 
he was discovered 
by the art world. 
It was another 
case in which the 
masses gave a les-

intelligentsia.

who is a little bitter toward

Mickey Mouse exhibit among 
the great moderns. The little star of the animated car-

hands of the dilettantes. After escaping hundreds of 
other dreadful perils, he is now in the most desperate 
plight of his career. 

The creator of Mickey Mouse is Walt Disney, a small 
wiry bright-eyed and eager-faced chap of thirty-two 
years, a little bewildered but wholly unspoiled by his 
sudden rise to fame. Disney's own personal fame in fact 
is not so great. He basks to a certain extent in reflected 
glory, but only a small percentage of the Mickey Mouse 
fans have any interest in Disney. Mickey was discovered 
on his first appearance more than five years ago; Disney 
is just beginning to be discovered. He has tremendous 
prestige with the film colony, although he is not w idely 


Walt Disney is the creator of Mickey Mouse-—vvorld celebrity and honorary French citizen 


tion for 


known there personally. He seldom attends Hollywood 
parties and premiéres, but in the last two years he has 
gone Hollywood to the extent of taking up polo, a game 
in which he has an official rating of zero. 

Will Rogers and other stars praised him to the skies 
and Chaplin did a Chaplin walk in his honor at a din- 
ner tendered to Disney last fall by the Hollywood 
Writers’ Club. Disney, who is as shy as Chaplin him- 
self, was quite overwhelmed. 

An example of Walt’s high standing in Hollywood 
occurred when he visited the studio where Rasputin was 
being made. The Barrymores are not ordinarily given to 
effusiveness toward strangers, but when Ethel was asked 
whether Disney could come on the set she said, ‘‘I’ll go 
home if he doesn’t.’’ She introduced him to John who 
threw both arms around him and then demanded that a 
group photograph be taken of the three Barrymores and 
the creator of Mickey Mouse. On being told no camera 
was available for taking still pictures, John exclaimed, 

‘There are thousands of them around every time an 
Iowa politician comes here. Do you mean to tell me 
there is no camera when Disney visits us?’’ Barrymore 
clamored about this until a still camera was found and 
the Barrymore-Disney group taken. 

The only part of Disney's sudden renown which dis- 
turbs him is the art appreciation. He is apprehensive 
that his studio will become art-conscious. Even now a 
few of Disney's able Cartoonists worry over their work 
and ask themselves, ‘‘Is this art?’’ When the publicity 
department receives a magazine or newspaper article dis- 
cussing the dynamics, the rhythm, the fluidity of line, 
the symbolism and the expressionism of Mickey Mouse, 
Disney orders the publication taken away so that no one 
in the studio can read it. 


DISNEY is a product of the system of child rearing 

which, although it has developed more than its share 
of American notables, is seldom recommended in modern 
treatises on education. This is the system which regards 
a boy as an economic asset who should at least pay for 
his keep and, if possible, pay dividends. Elias Disney, 
the father of Walt, had been reared on this plan and he 
believed in it for the sake both of the child and of the 
family. The philosophy of it is summed up in one of 
Frank Sullivan's simplified adages, “All work and no 
play makes jack.’’ First as a chore boy on a farm in 
Marceline, Missouri, and later as a new sboy in Kansas 
City, Walt carried on the old Disney tradition. His 


Walt Disney and his staff study live penguins as part of the prepara- 


new and antarctic Silly Symphony, Peculiar Penguins 


father had been a building contractor for 
twenty years in Chicago, but moved to the 
farm when Walt was an infant. 

The chore boy in Disney still manifests 
himself both in Mickey Mouse and in the 
Silly Symphonies. One of his earliest farm 
assignments was that of feeding the pigs. 
He had hardly outgrown infancy when he 
became an expert milkman, and the cow 
has always been a leading Disney charac- 
ter. Both Walt and his brothers fed their 
kittens by shooting streams of milk directly 
from cow to kitten. Twenty years later 
this became one of the Mickey Mouse gags. 
Flying streams of milk figured in the early 
Disney work as custard pies figuied in the 
glorious Mack Sennett i The censors 
of Ohio and some other states held that 
Disney's milking scenes were improper and 
that the sight of a cow's udder was indeli- 
cate. Disney. met the issue by putting a 
brassiére on the cow. 

The family moved from the farm to Kan- 
sas City when Walt was almost eight years 
old. From the age of nine to fifteen he de- 
livered newspapers, getting up every morn- 
ing at three-thirty, finishing about six and 
then selling papers on a corner. He re- 
peated this routine after school in the 
afternoon. At times he floundered through 
three feet of snow with his papers and in 
bitter cold weather he warmed himself 
now and then in hallways of apartment 
houses. His earnings went into the family 
funds. When he wanted pocket money he 
had to take on extra tasks. Responsibility 
and hard work at an early age gave him 
his drive and industry; his talents for fun 
and pictures were inborn and insuppressible. 

Disney began to draw almost as soon as 
he began to walk. He was an old hand 
with crayons before he was of school age. 
His first art patron was a veterinarian who 
lived near the Disney farm in Missouri. 
When Walt was seven the old horse doctor

Abraham Lincoln and Charlie Chaplin. In 
the fourth grade he recited the Gettysburg

five years. This was tempting fate, for the 
hardened Lincoln actor usually spends his 
whole life playing Lincoln both on and off 
the stage. The plight of the professional 
Lincoln was summed up by Gene Buck who 
said, ‘That guy will never be happy until 
he is assassinated.’ 


LUCKILY for Disney, he soon became 
¢V a better Chaplin than a Lincoln. He 
perfected himself in the art of springing a 
cane into the air with his feet and catching 
it nonchalantly. Sneaking out of the bed- 
room window he would attend amateur 
nights where he won several small cash 
prizes and became known as the second 
best Chaplin in Kansas City. He still be- 
lieves that he was jobbed, as the winner of 
the first prizes broke the rules by departing 
from pantomime and singing songs. Ama- 
teur nights in Kansas City had a great 
influence on the career of Disney. 

Chaplin was a kind of godfather to 
Mickey Mouse. It is now and always has 
been the aim of Disney to graft the psy- 
chology of Chaplin upon Mickey. The two 
universal characters have something in 
common in their approach to their prob- 
blems. They have the same blend of hero 
and coward, nitwit and genius, mug and 
gentleman. The emotional subtleties of 
Chaplin, his repose, wistfulness and pathos 
are not for the animated cartoon; the action 
is too rapid for effects which must be 
gradually developed. Laughs can be pro- 
duced in the fraction of a second; tears 
require time. Disney has, however, suc- 
ceeded in introducing occasional heart- 
throbs. Once for example when Mickey 
Mouse was elk-hunting, he shot his dog

THE TELEGRAM came during lunch. 

“Oh dear,’’ said Mrs. Mandeville. Not that she 
was particularly surprised. One might have known it 
of course. For had there ever been a time within the 
memory of the household when Miss Piper, with con- 
summate nonchalance, heralded merely by an announce- 
ment of the hour of her arrival, hadn't managed to drop 
into their midst when her presence there was least to be 
desired? 

Miss Piper was a sort of cousin. No one seemed able to 
establish the relationship more specifically than that. 
When, some twenty-five years before, Mr. Mandeville 
had brought his fiancée (aggressively virginal i in white) 
to Mandeville Hall, he had said, ‘‘ You must know the 
whole place, Charlotte, every inch of it—’’ and he had 
shown her the gardens and the stables, the cellars, 
pantries, linen closets—everything. “And that,”’ he 
had said at last, indicating a small curiously chaotic

‘* Miss Piper?’’ Mrs. Mandeville had repeated alertly. 

And Mr. Mandeville had said, ‘* She’s a sort of cousin.” 

Miss Piper’s next visit had neatly coincided with 
their return from the honeymoon. 

While, with the elder Mr. and Mrs. Mandeville, they 
were moving in stately procession across the hall to 
their first dinner as man and wife under the family roof- 
tree, Miss Piper had come spanking down the beautiful 
curved stairway, wearing a dilapidated gown of red 
velvet and an air of cheerful expectancy. She had eaten 
a great deal of food with frank enthusiasm, and stared a 
great deal at the bride; then after gulping down her 
coffee in the library she had seemed sud enly to lose all 
interest in the household and had gone off for a walk 
across the meadows in the moonlight, munching a

In due time Cyril had been born; the elder Mr. Mande- 
ville had died; Margaret had been born; Elinor had been 
born. And all through the years Miss Piper had been 
arriving and departing with airy ease, without a shred 
of explanation. Sometimes she stayed three days, oc- 
casionally three months. No one knew where she came 
from or where she went to. Once as she was leaving she 
had said, ‘Could anybody lend me a floppy hat? I'm 
going to Nyasaland.” Once when he was sixteen Cyril 
had come across a picture in a magazine captioned, 
‘Lands Giant Tuna,”’ and there, wearing a mackintosh, 
with a dead fish at her feet, had been Miss Piper. And 
once they had seen her in a newsreel, marching in a 
parade (in another country) with a banner that said, 
‘Protection for Our Pets.”’ 


FOR SOME time Mrs. Mandeville had been rather 
bitter about these erratic intrusions. “‘ But why?" 
“Why should she be free to enter 
our home in this extraordinary fashion?” 

No one had been able to advance a coherent reason; 
everyone had repeatedly agreed that something should 
be done. Miss Piper must be spoken to. The very next 
time. But somehow or other she never had been spoken 
to.

AT A QUARTER to three Cyril returned from 
yb nee and was immediately closeted with his parents

Piper arrived upon the scene. 

As usual she was sitting chummily next to Hunter

she shot to her feet, thrust two fingers into her mouth 
and let out upon the calm autumn air a long thin curling 
whistle. 

Almost instantaneously, almost miraculously, the 
dogs came. Round the corner of the stables, across the 
gravel yard: one after the other—Fanchon, Passenger, 
Berkley, Colonel. Mrs. Mandeville, hearing them, felt, 
as she always felt, a vague sense of irritation. It was 
really absurd the way the dogs carried on. Even Lady 
Shayne, the new cocker puppy, who had never set eyes 
on Miss Piper before, seemed to be under the impression 
that she was greeting a lifelong friend. 

When, half an hour later, summoned by the gong, she 
came down into the drawing-room, she found only Mrs. 
Mandeville at the tea table.

hostess vigorously. ‘‘What's up?’’ Mrs. Mandeville 
pinched her lips together slightly. It was of course in-

MISS PIPER got up early and ran around the big 
meadow three times. And after that she rushed 
down to breakfast. 

Mrs. Mandeville was just beginning, and Mr. Mande- 
ville just ending, the meal; Margaret and Elinor, two 
pale-haired delicate-featured girls in dark jerseys and 
pleated skirts, were eating porridge; and Cyril was 
standing at the serving table helping himself to bacon— 
a rather harried-looking young man of twenty-two, 
with the thin-lipped mouth of one parent and the 
pinched nostrils of the other. 

“You know,’’ Miss Piper announced affably, ‘‘I 
never see you all together without thinking how beauti- 
fully well-bred you look. Like Russian wolfhounds or 
something. One feels you must have been flawlessly 
sired and damed for generations—"’ 

After breakfast Margaret, Elinor and Cyril, at Mrs. 
Mandeville’s suggestion, changed into riding clothes 
and went cantering off; at ten o'clock Hunter left for 
town; at a quarter to eleven he returned; and a few 
minutes later Miss Piper ambled into the library. 

‘‘Morning,”’ she said. 

Miss Rosalie Ross, sitting wary and alert in a capa- 
cious leather chair, said, ‘Good morning.”’ 

“Oh, you needn't be guarded with me. I'm only Miss 
Piper. A sort of cousin."’ 

She surveyed Miss Ross with a calm eye. She was 
decorative, undeniably; with a taut fragility of person, 
with a small seductive face unexpectedly strengthened 
by the decisive line of the jaw. 

“T suppose,’ Miss Piper told her, ‘‘you realize that 
the whole household is rocked with emotion. People 
are lying down with damp handkerchiefs on their 
brows. Young sisters are rushed off on horses. Fathers 
are pacing floors and mothers beating their bosoms. 
Probably the servants are huddled in the cellar, weep- 
ing. Let’s go outside,’’ she suggested. ‘“‘If you're fated 
to be the future chatelaine of the place you'd better have 
a look round.”’ 


ON THE terrace in the wind and sunshine they 
met three of the dogs. 

For a minute or two they were too wildly occupied 
with adoring Miss Piper to investigate the new figure; 
then they began sniffing about her. Suddenly they came 
to a decision: Colonel planted his forepaws on her 
shoulders and butted her cap off; Berkley, something of 
a sentimentalist, kissed her throat; and Lady Shayne, 
seizing a glove out of her lap, tore over to the edge of 
the terrace at a terrific rate, turned round, tore back 
again and, quivering with excitement, presented the 
glove to its owner. 

Miss Piper looking down approvingly said, ‘‘All 
right. That'll do. Come along,’ and with the dogs 
careering about them they struck off across the green 
slopes of the garden toward the river. i 

Miss Piper sat down on the bank; then she lay 
out flat on her back, with her hands locked behind her 
head. 

‘Of course,’ she remarked, ‘‘I'm rather for it myself. 
New blood, you know. A healthy upheaval. All that 
sort of thing. But perhaps you'd rather have the 
money.” 

“The money?” 

“Of course. If you stick your ground the distraught 
parents will try to buy you off.” 

Miss Rosalie Ross stiffened abruptly. ‘‘Well—of all 
the—"’ 

‘Outrageous impertinence,”’ said Miss Piper. ‘‘What 
do they take me for? A despicable gold-digger? An un- 
principled trull? Me—or I, rather—I who would give 
my very life to make Cyril happy.’’ She paused. ‘Well, 
if you don’t want money, what do you want? Cyril?”’ 

“Yes.”’ 

Miss Piper mulled this over. ‘‘You don't mean—you 
don't by any chance love him, do you?”’ 

Miss Rosalie Ross, still sitting erect, said, ‘‘I am very 
fond of him.”’ 

“Good gracious! Are you really?’ Miss Piper ap- 
peared quite impressed. ‘I mean—confidentially, you 
know, between ourselves—Cyril’s a nice boy of course, 
but I've always found him so excruciatingly dull.”’ 


There was another pause; then suddenly Miss Rosalie 
Ross spoke. 

“See here,’’ she said, her voice curt, ‘‘what are you 
trying to do? Whose side are you on really, theirs or 
ours?” 

“Alas,’’ Miss Piper deplored, ‘‘not theirs or ours, but 
theirs or yours. He’s been recaptured, you know. He 
practically belongs to them now."’ 

“Because they've bullied him! He didn’t before! Not 
two days ago—”’ 

For several moments Miss Rosalie Ross regarded Miss 
Piper in silence; then, ‘‘This is the first decent break 
I've ever had. And I'm going to fight for it! I’m nota 
fool—I know what I'm landing into with a family like 
this—but it'll ke a sight better than—"’ 

Miss Piper said brightly, ‘Enough! 
You're Tired of It All!”’ 

“‘Exactly,"’ said Miss Rosalie Ross. 

“Why?” Miss Piper asked after a while. ‘‘Aren’t 
you any good?” 

“Tdon’t know." She lifted her head in sudden anger. 
“Yes, 1am! But what's the use of being good when you 
never get a chance to prove it?”’ 

“Quite,"’ said Miss Piper; then, ‘‘What are you in? 
What do you do?" 

“Treasure Trove. Chorus work and understudy for 
Hermione Nettle.’ 

“Good gracious!’’ Miss Piper seemed arrested. ‘‘That 
woman!"’ 

“Healthy as the devil,’’ Miss Rosalie Ross pro-

life. And never will. Never, never.” 

Miss Piper said, ‘But she absolutely butchers it. 
Anyone can see that. My song, you know."

year. In the Pyrenees. I always bear fruit in the Pyre-

Miss Rosalie Ross had become all at once animated.

Cyril in lieu of what you really want. You're falling

“Exactly,”’ affirmed Miss Rosalie Ross. She suddenly

a chance, even now, to make good—"’ 

Miss Piper looked dreamily into space. ‘‘Ah,’’ Miss 
Piper said again, ‘‘I see.'’ Then she got up. ‘Well, come 
along. We'd better go back.” 

“Of course,’ Miss Piper continued, ‘‘they won't let 
me in on the fray. But you'll be all right, won't you? 
Hold your ground, any way you want to, but don’t let 
anyone persuade you to do anything definite until 
Friday morning.” 

‘Friday morning?’ Miss Rosalie Ross said blankly. 


Miss Piper had bitten largely into an apple and was 
now staring down at it with a thoughtful eye. ‘‘How 
degrading,’’ she said. ‘I believe I’ve eaten a worm.” 


Pn | afternoon Miss Piper, thoroughly in dis- 
grace (‘We can’t understand it!’ Mrs. Mande-

In due time she drew up in front of a leading hotel. 

A uniformed attendant opened the door of the car and 
the dogs promptly fell forth, wildly excited. ‘I’m 
afraid, Madam—"’ the attendant began; but Miss Piper 
swept him majestically out of the way, strode through

mione Nettle. By appointment.”’

Miss Hermione Nettle cried: 
Immediately! Take them away!’

ANTOINETTE TAFT, lovely, high-spirited, is de- 

lighted with her job, society editor of the Morning 
Call in San Francisco. Every night when she goes home 
to the shabby comfortable apartment, her adoring 
family gather round to hear the details of the day's 
work—her elder sister Brenda; her brothers, Cliff and 
Bruce; and Aunt Meg. Frequently they are joined by 
Barney Kerr, whose advice Tony seeks when invited toa 
party by Bessie Cutter, whose lavish salary, given by 
the managing editor, is not justified by her work at the 
Call. Barney proposes to Tony, much to her distress. 
‘*He isn’t in love with me,”’ she tells Brenda, ‘‘and I’m 
not in love with him. I know the real thing when I 
meet it, Bendy. One day in a newspaper office I met a 
man—something flashed between us that was nearer 
love than anything Barney's ever made me feel!"’ 

One day, on an assignment, she meets Ruth Bellam 
and is astonished and thrilled to find that her husband, 
Larry, is the handsome editor of the Journal whom she 
had met one day in her search for a job. Ruth, ten years 
older than her husband, melancholy over the death of 
her seven-year-old son, responds to Tony's zest for life; 
tells her, ‘‘ You've been good for me, my dear.”’ 

Tony scoops an engagement story; expansive under 
the approval of Greenwood, the city editor, she says: 
‘Here's something that may interest you: Lawrence 
Bellamy is going over to the Examiner with the Jour- 
nal. I know him and his wife quite well. I saw him 
today and he told me.’’ Greenwood headlines what she 
has told him about Bellamy, and ‘‘Larry’s frantic,”’ 
Ruth phones. ‘‘We both know that it wasn't you. 
Can you find out who gave it to the Call?’’ Tony is 
heartsick over her careless indiscretion, and over a call- 
down from the assistant manager about the methods 
she'd used in getting her scoop. Finally she goes to 
Larry's office and sobs out her unhappiness. He is kind, 
absolves her of any blame; they have dinner together. 
Tony’s spirit soars once more. 

The Bellamys take her to Santa Barbara for her vaca- 
tion; it is a glorious unforgettable week. Monday, at 
dawn, Tony and Larry start for San Francisco by motor; 


a truck crowds them off the road; the car overturns. 
After a moment of sickening horror Tony finds herself 
suddenly safe and tight against Larry's heart—'‘ Tony, 
my darling, my darling—you’re all right?”’ 

Larry becomes the business and financial editor of the 
Call. Whenever he encounters Tony, he is pleasant but 
studiedly casual; Tony, unable to bear it, asks him 
what's wrong. “‘ Perhaps there’s something I ought to 
say about all this,’’ Larry tells her. Tony, emotionally 
shaken, bursts into tears. 


IV 


THE burst of tears ended almost as suddenly as it 

had begun and Tony felt ashamed of herself, felt 
that the situation had suddenly gone flat. What was 
she doing up here in Larry’s office making a ridiculous 
scene, she who despised Mae’s obvious methods with 
men and pitied poor Bessie? 

Sniffling and gulping, not looking at him, she fum- 
bled for her handkerchief, blew her nose and wiped her 
eyes. . 

“IT don’t know why I did that,’ She apologized, 
swallowing. 

Larry was sitting facing her, his big hands on the 
arms of his chair. The expression of his face was com- 
pletely non-committal; his fine gray eyes were narrowed 
and fixed on space. Tony’s heart sank as she looked at 
him; he was not going to help her out. 

‘You knew I felt that way,”’ she said sullenly. 

It was all like a dream. The sense of wearing a new 
dress and a smart new hat, the odor of gardemias, the 
setting of the cheaply furnished temporary office, and 
Larry sitting opposite her with a troubled and stubborn 
look on his face. The girl had a desperate sudden feeling 
of suffocation; a desperate need to awaken, to be away 
from here, somewhere else, somewhere alone in the 
dark. 

‘* However, if you don’t understand me, Larry,’’ she

“The bad news for Miss Taft

began with broken dignity. And with hands that

gloves. 

“If I did,’’ Larry said suddenly, ‘‘ then what?”’ 

For a second she stared at him, paling. Then dully, 
hopelessly she looked away. 

““T don't know,”’ she said. 

“*Exactly,’’ Larry added briefly. There was silence 
again. 

“Don’t think I'm glad about it,’’ Tony said standing

all this—’’ 

After a pause he said a little unnaturally in an every-

Tony was fitting on the soft new white gloves. 
‘*And you'll give my message to Ruth?”’ she said trying 
for an ordinary tone. 

“*T will indeed.’’ 

“T'll telephone her and make a tea date.” Tony 
walked to the door, looked back, nodded a good-by 
and went out, closing the door carefully behind her. 


BACK AT her desk in the city room again Tony 

found herself inclined to talk aloud. From her 
bag she tumbled out on her desk several odds and ends 
of paper ies which social notes were scratched. 
Bendy’s wedding was over now and she must get down 
to business. 

“Oh, you fool!’’ she said under her breath. She 
ranged the little notes before her without seeing them; 
slipped a sheet of yellow paper into her typewriter. 
‘What of it—what of it—what of it—"’ she said fiercely. 
‘“What do you care what he thinks? He knows I’m 
crazy about him. What of it? I wish I hadn't gone 
up there! I could have sent a note. Darling Bendy— 
she looked so sweet. I wonder how it would feel to 
be married to a man who made you feel completely 
crazy, nutty, the way I feel now. Going off with him 
inacar to Monterey—oh, I wish I hadn't gone up to 
his office! What did he say? Did he say anything at 
all? No, he didn’t. He just sat there and was sorry 
for me.”’ 

She brought her mind to her notes: the bridesmaids 
for the di Milano wedding had been chesen. Mrs. 
James Lee Fairchild and her daughter Miss Janet were 
leaving for the east, where Miss Janet would be placed 
ina girls’ school. Mrs. J. Kane Kelly was entertaining 
for her niece Miss Barbara Fairchild. 

Tony felt broken and sick; her head ached. She could 
not seem to keep her mind on what she was doing. At 
six o'clock she walked home. The walk seemed nothing 
tonight. The nervous energy that drove her would 
have carried her on for miles without fatigue or even 
consciousness. Reaching her own room she changed 
from her: wedding garments jerkily, spasmodically; 
sometimes hurrying as if driven, sometime: stopping 
short to fall into restless and feverish thought. There 
was a telegram; blood rushed into her face and her fin- 
gers trembled as she opened it. It was only a few words

of love and gratitude from San Jose, signed ‘‘ Brenda 
and Alvin Atwater.” 

“They don’t care who knows they’re bride and 
groom,”’ Tony thought. ‘‘Oh, Larry—Larry!’’ She 
put her hands to her head and squeezed her palms 
against her temples. 

In the sitting-room Aunt Meg, Aunt Sally and her 
daughter Violet, still in their wedding regalia, were 
upon the inexhaustible subject of Brenda’s marriage. 
Tony sat down, handing the telegram to her aunt. 

“See that? She couldn’t wait to sign her new name.”’ 

“The place seems perfectly forlorn without her,” 
Miss Bruce said tremulously. ‘‘It’s just the time she 
usually came home every night, so sweet and amiable 
always, and so interested!’’ 


THE TELEPHONE rang and Tony sprang to it. 

Her heart was beating so hard and her senses were 
in such confusion that it was difficult for a moment to 
identify the speaker at the other end of the line. 
Ruth—it was Ruth—

We got home yesterday.”

‘That was Mrs. Bellamy.’’ 

““You could have gone to dinner, Tony.’ 

“T know, Aunt Meg. But I really didn’t want to.” 
She began to straighten the books in the cases; every 
muscle in her body seemed inclined to jerk and tear; it 
seemed impossible to go on quietly stacking books; it 
seemed impossible to speak quietly rather than to 
shriek. The fever in her soul was like a pain, more 
dreadful than any pain she had ever known. 

Tony stood by the window looking out. The city 
was mantled in fog now. It was moving softly, stealth- 
ily in; she could telephone Larry. About what? About 
anything. ‘‘Larry, I made an engagement with Ruth 
for tomorrow and now I have to break it, and she 
doesn’t answer her telephone.’’ But she would answer 
her telephone of course. Chevalier was there to attest 
that Miss Taft had not telephoned; Ruth would say 
that he had been there all afternoon. 

Oh, was anything ever so dreary as twilight ona cold 
windy foggy September afternoon! Brenda wouldn't 
think so, though. She and her Alvin had an apartment 
of four rooms waiting: little gas stove, little fireplace, 
cheap little white curtains that Brenda had made, cheap 
little china set—and Ruth’s lovely silver. The bare 
little sitting-room would be lighted tonight with 
Bruce’s lamp, furnished with Tony’s and Clifford's 
gift: the two fat chairs and the davenport in ribbed 
dark green corduroy, the narrow long table whose 
black walnut surface was so cleverly nicked and stained, 
the “‘extra’’ chair with its tavern back and molded seat 
in dark fruitwood. 


TONY WONDERED tonight how much real 
ecstasy and passion had gone to Brenda’s marrying. 
Love was there of course, but what sort of love? She 
had been tremendously pleased at Alvin’s adoration; 
she had been maternal and amused; very sure of 
herself. All her phrases for Alvin were motherly. 
That was one way of loving; perhaps the most satis- 
fying of all, to Brenda. But there were other ways. 
There was the way of a woman of twenty-three and a 
man nearly ten years older; a way that included admira- 
tion, all the respectful homage that a girl loves to give 
a man, and this tearing agony of physical need too. 
Brenda had been the first person to discover the charm 


“Is it who I think it is, Tony?” Brenda said, very low 


of Alvin; but everyone adored Larry. There was not a 
man in the office who had not felt the stimulus and in- 
spiration of his coming. He was forceful, dynamic, an 
instantly impressive and memorable personality. Alvin 
had taken his completely happy wife to four rather 
ordinary rooms in Monterey; Larry—ah, what wouldn’t 
he do—what couldn't he do for the woman he loved! 

While she thought her odd restless disconnected 
thoughts, Tony had been staring out of the window. 
Now without being exactly conscious of what she did 
she went into the kitchen, propping the swinging door 
open so that she could go on with the general conversa- 
tion.

After a while the Cravens were going, with many

Everything seemed very quiet and lonesome without 
Brenda, but as she lay awake that night looking wide- 
eyed at the angle of moonshine that came down the 
shaft and kept the room bright, Tony was not thinking 
of her sister. Her body seemed on springs; there was no 
sleep anywhere; there was no lying still. 

Toward morniag she fell into uneasy dream-ridden 
slumber; Ruth was a small gray mule, Ruth had always 
been a small gray mule, it seemed, inexplicably married 
to Larry. It was all quite natural and taken for granted 
in the dream. They were all going to China on a large 
ship with oo presses crashing and crashing 
punctually on the deck— 

Aunt Meggy waked her at ten. Wasn’t there a very 
important club meeting to settle the question of clean 


vegetable markets, that she had to attend at eleven?

fore the apologetic pleasant voice had stopped. ‘‘No, 
no coffee, Aunt Meg. I'm lunching with Ruth; I'll 
make it up then!"’ 

She was putting on her wedding garments with all 
possible speed as she spoke: dark blue suit, white frill,

She walked two brisk blocks in morning fog, got ona

laid out yellow paper, murmured to her associates.

SOME ARE born to tule, 

as some to be ruled. Mrs. 

John Sampson Taylor belonged 

to the first group and had no- 

tably and enjoyably achieved 

her destiny. She had all the attributes of a natural 

despot: she was quick, perceptive and decisive; she had 

energy and persistence and most important of all she 

knew when to give way. Asa child she had ruled the 

nursery in which she had been the eldest, and now at 
seventy she was the family oracle. 

Yet she was aware of her place in the general scheme 

of things without being complacent about it—for she 

was a woman of many and diverse interests. Mrs. 


Evelyn ‘was conscious of a warm 


Taylor had passed a part of her youth in Paris and had 
studied with one of the obscure painters of her period. 
Her studies had borne no fruit on canvas but that ob- 
scure artist who had been driven by poverty to give 
lessons to an idle and fashionable young American had 
not remained obscure; and that period had long since 
been recognized as belonging to the great days. At 
twenty Janet Nason had been no fool and if her talents 
were dynastic rather than creative she had even then the 
touchstone that distinguishes fine gold from pinchbeck. 
She had recognized almost at their true value the gifts of 
her sardonic and bitter teacher and her days under his 
tutelage had given her for all her life a respect for 
achievements outside her own orbit. It had given her 


as well a lively and abiding interest in what the cata- 
logues call the graphic arts—a taste that her family bore 
with but did not share. 

So when her favorite nephew wrote her a long and 
confused letter saying he wanted “‘your advice or I 
ought to say suggestions as advice should never be 
asked in my opinion unless Iam going to take it. That I 
cannot say I will do,”’ she replied succinctly, and more 
grammatically, informing him that she would be in 
New York on the following Thursday and would be 
glad to see him at four o'clock in her apartment. She 
was quite honest in her willingness to advise and help 
him (synonymous terms) but she was equally pleased to 
be able to attend a private sale of some prints in which 
she was interested. So James found her poring over a 
bright trophy which lay on the table. He gazed at it 
indifferently while she praised it. 

“People are always talking about how times change 
and how things are different nowadays. I must say I 
can’t see it. Just look at this! And she was Monet's 
mistress but I can’t see that anyone ever stopped buying 
her pictures on that account or thought much about it 
either.”’ 


Nae got up and looked at the picture. ‘It 
seemed to be some sort of nursery scene with a rosy 
baby leaving its bath for the arms of a mother who was 
as rosy and benign as the baby. Innocent and felicitous 
and indescribably winning. 

“Who was the French hussy?’’ said James, fasci- 
nated by the contrast of the lady’s free life and her 
domestic drawing. 

“She was Mary Cassatt,’’ said Mrs. Taylor, ‘‘and she 
was an American, ignorant boy. What did you want 
to see me about? Don’t tell me you're in love.” 


rush of feeling as she turned toward him 


James started. ‘‘How did you know?” 

Mrs. Taylor looked pleased. *‘Oh, I’m psychic,’’ she 
admitted modestly. ‘‘ You know I always know things 
before people tell me.”’ 

This was true. Years of shrewd observation had taught 
the genial despot that people will always tell you more 
than you can ever learn by question and that an atten- 
tive ear will rarely go unused. 

““Ah,”’ said his aunt, ‘‘if it isn’t one thing it’s an- 
other. But I did think you’d had enough. Still, people 
never learn. I never learn that! Come, tell me about it. 
I hope at least she’s not so disagreeable as Louisa.”’ 


ab ae looked pained, but before he could speak 
his aunt answered him. 

“You needn't looked so shocked. I’ve never held 
with this idea of speaking no ill of the dead. It would 
be a lot more to the point to spare the living, it seems to 
me. And you can’t deny Louisa was a disagreeable 
headstrong woman.” 

‘“‘Perhaps she was, Aunt Jenny, but I’ve come to

“You're pretty young still—"’

**And how old’s your girl?’’ asked Mrs. Taylor. 

““That’s it, exactly. You see she’s only twenty-two 
and I think this time everything would be different. 
I’m older and I know a good deal more about how im- 
portant it is for a man to be master in his own house- 
hold. And Evelyn is young and—’’ he hesitated. 

*““Docile?”’ 

“*N-no, I wouldn’t say docile, exactly—” 

““Well, what seems to be the trouble?”’ 


“Rich people don’t have any fun” 


“I don’t know,” said James dejectedly. ‘‘She seems 
to like me but I can’t pin her down to anything. And 
then we quarrel a good deal. Not quarrel exactly but 
disagree and argue and never get things settled.” 

““Oh,”’ said Mrs. Taylor again. She was silent a mo- 
ment. Then, ‘Have you told her about Louisa?”’ 

“Yes. I told her I hadn’t finished college because I 
got married my senior year, and that Louisa’s family 
were furious with her for marrying me and you were 
furious with me for marrying her and that we had gone 
around the world on some money Louisa had and then 
when we came home you gave us a house in Cambridge. 
And I told her Louisa was terribly attractive and I was 
jealous and we both acted very foolishly. I told her 
about Louisa asking me to deed her the house and 
then selling it; and how I hated the car she drove 
and the way she was always getting arrested for 
speeding.”’ 

“*You seem to have covered the ground. Did she 
gather that your wife was an obstinate creature? Did 
you tell her Louisa killed herself by her own headstrong 
foolishness?”’ 

“But, Aunt Janet, she didn’t.” 

“Yes she did, James. I always thought that. I don’t 
doubt she had the right of way, just as the court said, 
but if she hadn’t been so bound and determined not to 
yield an inch of her rights I don’t believe the car would 
ever have gone over the bank. She was killed by her 
own character. What did your girl—what’s her 
name?—say?”’ 

‘She didn’t say anything except she was sorry I’d 
had such an unhappy marriage and she thought such a 
painful experience must make the idea of a happy one

“And you said?”

He looked at her, not like the quiet and self-contained

history. 

Mrs. Taylor looked unconvinced but as James en- 
treated her with another appealing glance she nodded. 
“Tl write her a note,’’ she promised and, such was her 
power, a quarter of an hour later he went off contented.

&N A TEACHER of swimming 

must have three qualifications: 
experience, intelligence and trust- 
worthiness of character. The need 
for experience is obvious. Intelli- 
gence is essential because he must 
vary his teaching methods accord- 
ing to the pupils’ needs. Character 
is necessary because the instructor 
must handle his pupil with the 
most painstaking care; he must be 
both gentle and firm. Many people 
who have the last two requisites 
lack experience; this article is 
meant to help them. 

This recipe, prepared from my 
experience as a professional in- 
structor, is for teaching the most 
efficient method of swimming so 
far devised, the one which gets the 
swimmer the greatest distance in 
the shortest time with the least 
effort, the American crawl stroke. 
No one could learn the whole 
stroke at once; here one simple feat 
is added to another until the total 
sum is the crawl. Both children 
and adults can be taught from this 
recipe; you need not know how to 
swim in order to use it (the in- 
structor doesn’t have to get into 
the water unless he prefers to); in 
fact, from it you can teach youtself 
to swim. 

The most important fundamentals 
of teaching the crawl are: (1) The 
pupil must stay in water no more 
than shoulder-deep. (2) The pupil 
must learn to relax; this will be the 
greatest asset. 


EVERY city and large town 
=> has its municipal pools of salt 
or fresh water where the beginner 
may practice; and no small commun- 
ity is so far away from some lake, 
river or shore as not to make the 
trip worth while for both teacher 
and pupil. In general, twenty 
minutes area good length for each 
lesson; but make them longer or 
shorter as seems best at the time. 
Don't hurry past any step before 
the pupil has learned what it 
teaches; it is a good idea to recheck 
the various steps now and then, in 
order to master all preceding ones. 
And don’t force the pupil; maneuver 
him into the mental state of want- 
ing to learn and you'll both fare 
happily. 

Step 1. Ducxinc. The pupil 
holds his breath and submerges his 
face in a basin or tub of cool or 
lukewarm water. He does not 
hold his nose; to keep water from 
entering he can expel air slowly 
but steadily through it. He should 
learn to keep his face under for ten 
or fifteen seconds at least. This 
step may be done at home before 
going to the pool. 

Step. 2 ExHatinc UNpeR Water. 
The pupil submerges his face and 
while it is under exhales vigorously 
through his nose. He must not of 
course inhale water. This too may 
be done at home. 

Step 3. Taxtnc Hotp of THE 
Water. The pupil walks back and 
forth in waist-deep water and as- 
sists himself by using his hands as 
oars. He presses the water with 
the palms, not the backs, of his 
hands, and takes long sweeping

strokes, moving his hands either 
simultaneously or alternately. His 
wrists are straight and his hands 
very slightly cupped; it is very im- 
portant that he keep his fingers 
pressed together. This exercise 
helps the pupil get used to the 
water and teaches him to grip the 
water with his hands. It should be 
repeated at intervals until step to. 

Step 4. Ducxinc. The pupil 
holds his breath and ducks under 
water in the pool. He may hold on 
to the railing or overflow trough. 

Step 5. Openinc Eyres UNper 
Water. The pupil ducks under 
water, holding one hand several 
inches from his face. While he is 
under he opens his eyes and looks 
at his submerged hand. Learning 
to keep his eyes open under water 
will heighten his confidence im 
mensely. 

Step6. Exuatinc UNper Water. 
The pupil submerges his face in the 
pool and then exhales vigorously, 
as in step 2. This should be re- 
peated at intervals until step 17. 

Step 7. Front Froat Hotpinc 
Rat. The pupil stands in chest- 
deep water facing the pool wall. 
He grasps the pool rail or trough 
with both hands, then takes a 
deep breath, puts his face in the 
water, lies down, straightens his 
body and relaxes. He has only to 
remain in this position for a few 
seconds and he will be floating at 
the surface. He must not support 
himself on the rail. He must zor 
try to float. If he will relax and 
simply allow himself to float, he 
will do so. 

Step 8. Learninc tHe Kick. 
For this the pupil must be in the 
swimming position, floating face- 
down in the water. There are two 
easy ways for him to maintain this 
position: he can rest his hands on 
the bottom at a spot where the 
water is as deep as the length of his 
arm, or he can use his arms as a 
bracket, gripping the rail or trough 
with his left hand and bracing his 
right hand against the wall about 
eighteen inches under water and 
directly beneath his left. For this 
exercise he keeps his face out of 
water so he can breathe. 


&.N WHEN HE is in position he 

beats his feet alternately down 
into the water about twelve inches 
and returns them to the surface. His 
knees are straight, his toes are 
pointedand slightly pigeon-toedand 
he kicks from his hips rather than 
from his knees. He kicks inthe tempo 
of a fast run, and he kicks rhythmi- 
cally—the exact speed is not im- 
portant; it is very important that 
the kick be rhythmic, and that 
each foot kick as hard as the other. 
He does not push the water with 
the soles of his feet. He gets all his 
power from the pressure of his in- 
steps and the tops of his toes 
against the water on the downward 
beats. The up-beats do nothing 
more than return the feet to posi- 
tion for more down-beats.

be in a horizontal position in the 
water. The crawl kick has two 
functions, keeping the feet near the
