ILLUSTRATOR: 
NORMAN 
PRICE 


AT THE out- 
break of the Re- 
bellion Grey Seadrift 
had remained loyal to 
England and had 
joined His Majesty's 
army. Later Lieuten- 
ant Seadrift was a 
member of George the 
Third’s mission to 
Germany to hire 
troops. This did not 
prove easy for in spite 
of the cupidity of 
most of the German 
princelings, they were 
reluctant to sell their 
subjects. Some said 
this was due to the op- 
position of Baron von Lessing prompted by his Ameri- 
can wife and her sister. 

Grey was appointed to spy upon the Lessings but un- 
fortunately for his duty he at once fell in love with the 
charming baroness. As the months passed although 
she did not encourage his love she did not send him 
away. Finally the first regiments sailed for North 
America under the command of General von Riedesel. 
In spite of Grey’s vigilance the baroness, a close friend 
of von Riedesel’s wife, slipped away when she and the 
general's children followed him. Grey sailed at once 


for Canada only to find that the baroness had remained | 


in England. 

By the summer of 1777 the English army under Gen- 
eral Burgoyne was ready to march south to meet Gen- 
eral Howe who was supposed to be moving toward 
Albany from New York. Lieutenant Seadrift was 
present at the capture of Ticonderoga but the pleasure 
of his first action was somewhat dimmed by his desire 
to return to the baroness who had finally arrived in 
Canada a few days before the campaign began. After the 
retreat of the American troops, General Burgoyne called 
a staff meeting to try to discover how his plans had be- 
come known to the Americans and why so many Ger- 
mans and Canadians had deserted. Nevertheless no one 
was able to offer an explanation and the meeting broke 
up with Burgoyne’s decision to go to Albany by 
Skenesborough and Fort Anne rather than by Lake 
George. 

III 


THE DRY crackle of rebel rifle fire broke out ahead 
in the dense woods, followed by solid volleys from 
General Fraser's leading battalions of light troops. 

As I forced my startled horse forward to come up 
with my baron, I rode near to some of General Fraser's 
troops who, entangled in thick brush and swamps, were 
floundering there in something like a panic while the 
rebel rifle bullets whistled among the trees. 


Copyright, 1934, by Elsie M. Chambers 


ee ae 


23 


“Herr Lieutenant, do you really think you care for me” 


Sweating British officers, trying to check the flight of 
their bewildered men, bawled hoarsely at me that they 
were being outflanked and that I should find General 
Riedesel and report the critical situation of General 
Fraser's entire command. 

I shouted back to them that I would do so; and even 
as I wheeled my nag to spur out into the main road, I 
could see scores of dead or wounded redcoats lying in 
the swamp and could hear the rebel riflemen cheering. 

Before I reached General Riedesel I passed more than 
a score of our poor Brunswickers lying hurt or stone 


‘dead under the heavy belt of musket smoke. I saw Cleve 


cheerily conducting a bewildered German battalion 
toward the right. 

When the baron noticed me he said calmly: “‘I know 
that the British advance is outflanked. We are remedy- 
ing that very nicely. Remain near me, if you please, 
Mr. Seadrift.”” 

Indeed his diversion in Fraser’s favor already had be- 


gun and its effects very quickly became apparent. Rebel 
cheering ceased; nothing now came from their riflemen 
except a distant dropping shot here and there. 

“They fight their rear-guard action very fiercely,” 
said the baron. ‘You are right, Seadrift; these people 
are not cowards.” 

That night we made a most miserable camp. St. 
Luc’s savages got drunk with rum and prowled about 
our outer pickets so fiercely and persistently that the 
general gave orders to shoot any Indian approaching a 
German outpost. 

That night more than a dozen furtive Canadians de- 
serted with their arms and accoutrements—God help 
them!—for Ido not doubt that the Hurons got them be- 
fore they reached the nearest rebel outposts. Two more 
Brunswick soldiers also went to the enemy—or, more 
likely, to their German Gort. 

At daylight our jagers resumed their pursuit of the 
retreating light infantry of General St. Clair; and I saw 


24 


the rebel prisoners we had taken—a sorry ragged starved- 
looking set. And again it gave me a strange sense of 
satisfaction to see how defiantly they regarded us—none 
among them seeming afraid or even disheartened, but 
bearing themselves in their rags and blood like very 
men. 

About noon a terrible commotion occurred some- 
where ahead of us. A British regiment came into view 
presently in full retreat, but retiring in some sort of 
order, and their rear ranks blazing away at an unseen 
enemy which we could neither discover nor even hear. 

We marched forward, however, to the sound of 
musketry; but the enemy had gone off after driving this 
British battalion away from Fort Anne; and had burned 
the fort and set fire to the forest. 


HERE IN this fearful and smoking forest were a 

few clearings, roads, rough farmhouses and fenced 
fields. And I am very sorry to say our Germans began 
marauding and plundering and brutalizing these remote 
and unhappy people. 

General Johnny was in a fine rage when he heard of 
this and sent Sir Francis Clerke to our baron with in- 
stant representations. Our good baron, who thought 
plundering a legitimate part of war, was both irritated 
and mortitied at the message. But he issued strict or- 
ders; and after several Hessians and Brunswickers had 
been caught marauding and had been severely whipped, 
things went better. But the mischief had been done; 
every man, woman and child in the eritire northern 
region began to fear and detest the very sight of a Ger- 
man or British uniform; and the excesses and brutality 
of our jigers and grenadiers promised to prove a bad 
business for this army. 

I cannot properly describe to you the shaggy wilder- 
ness in which our regiments, artillery and wagons had 
now become inextricably entangled. The enemy ax-men 
had felled huge trees on both sides of the roads so that 
they lay across lengthwise, their vast limbs and branches 
intertwined in an almost impassable barrier. 

Our wretched sappers and pioneers were obliged to 
bridge half a hundred swamps and creeks; one corduroy 
road alone was two miles long and ran through a morass. 
Our horses began to give out; scores of army wagons 
broke down; General Johnny warned all officers to re- 
duce their baggage; and many among the line officers 
retained only a tent and a knapsack. And yet there 
were nearly four hundred women and children in the 
army, plodding along afoot, or a-horse, or in wagons. 

It took us twenty days to march from Skene’s house 
south to Fort Edward—which was at a rate of about a 
mile a day, artillery and wagons bumping, jerking, 


The column was done for; a iter day for the British army 


scarcely seeming to move at all between dawn and dark. 

There was little firing during our painful march and 
no real fighting; but every day rebel scouts and rebel 
Indians, slinking on our laboring flanks, took a few 
murderous shots at us. 

Our flour and salt beef, out of England, were nearly 
gone. We had news out of Canada, however, couriers 
and runners arriving and departing almost daily. But 
the news they fetched told us nothing about our other 
two armies, or whether General Howe was coming up 
from New York to meet us, or whether Colonel St. 
Leger had as yet stormed Fort Stanwix and was possibly 
already marching east along the Mohawk to join us. 

There was a man who called himself ‘‘Colonel" 
Skene of Skenesborough—which is now Whitehall— 
who joined General Johnny’s staff after our fierce little 
fight with Colonel Warner's light troops. 

He was the most talkative man I ever met, one of 
those gentlemen who know everything and everybody 
and are generously prepared to give good advice to God 
himself. 

His vast acreage was a property including all that 
wilderness we had traversed, and dotted with settle- 
ments where his frontier tenantry were trying to make 
clearings, sow grain and till a few stump-ridden fields. 

This Skene saw in his fatuous brain as vast broad 
acres yellow with phantom wheat and corn, teeming 
with ghostly sheep and cattle! He saw, in his mind's 
eye, an increasing and prosperous population, devoted 
to him as overlord and loyal to their King. 

And this vain deluded incredibly stupid man—this 
“Colonel"’ Skene of Skenesborough!—so cocksure, so 
plausible, so ingratiating, was received by our General 
Johnny, admitted to intimacy, listened to, believed and 
his halt-crazed advice not only seriously considered in 
council, but followed! 

And this—when that honest wise brave experienced 
soldier, General Baron Riedesel, was pie from 
British councils of war! 


THE FIRST time I saw Skene of Skenesborough, he 

and General Johnny, Sir Francis Clerke and my Lord 
Balcarres were at table, laughing, singing, cracking bot- 
tle after bottle of claret and champagne; and the hand- 
some Mrs. Lightways sat beside our General Johnny 
with one fair hand dropped over his gilded shoulder. 

Well, I did my sane at headquarters, was treated 
handsomely, even affectionately, by these gentlemen in 
their merry cups and received a few languishing looks 
from Mrs. Lightways. 

Then back again to our German camp where our wor- 
ried baron sat fighting mosquitoes and poring over the 
unreliable maps furnished us, 
silently cursing every quarter of 
an inch which represented some 
agonizing mile along our end- 
less road. 

There is an express come in 
from Three Rivers with Ger- 
man mail,” said he lifting his 
anxious perplexed eyes. ‘My 
wife torments me in several let- 
ters to permit her to join me; 
and God knows I want her; 
but, Seadrift, I can’t let her join 
me in such a situation as this!”’ 

He took the letter which 
General Johnny had scribbled 
at table and had handed to me 
to transmit. 

“There's mail for you also, 
Herr Lieutenant,’’ said Riedesel, 
motioning toward the table. 

I seated myself on a camp- 
chair and broke the seal of a 
thick letter—the second mis- 
sive that ever I had received 
from the loveliest lady in the 
world: 


‘My gay and engaging Friend, 
‘I promised you a letter. I 
perform my promise. I fulfill 
all prescribed forms and formal 
courtesies by inquiring: 
**First—Are you in health? 
“*Second—Are you alive? 
‘“*Third—Are you victorious? 
“Believing that you have not 
completely forgotten your ex- 
tremely timid correspondent, I 
venture to give you a brief 
history of myself since I said 
farewell to you at Chambly 
Fort. 
“IT traveled to Three Rivers 
with Madame von Riedesel, 


where I left her nicely lodged. I then continued on to 
La Chine where I found a lodging prepared for me. 
‘However, I languish and am restless and impatient 
to get on to Albany; and can scarce endure to await 
General Burgoyne’s permission to join his army and 
travel thither—as Madame von Riedesel expects to do. 
‘She writes to me that her husband has promised to 
let her join him as soon as the Americans are in retreat 
from Fort Edward; and that then it will be an easy mat- 
ter to take boat and drop down the Hudson to Albany 
where General Howe is to meet General Burgoyne and 
end the war by the.end of August. 
‘So you may expect to see me at no distant date. 
“Well, sir, here ends my promised letter; and I hope 
it pleases. For, although you may not quite believe it, 
I really am desirous of pleasing you, Herr Lieutenant. 
‘Therefore, sir, believe me to be your obliged and 
faithful friend who perhaps one day may sign her name 
to so rash and tender a confession of friendship, but who 
now merely bids you to think of her not unkindly. 
‘Good night, He-r Lieutenant!”’ 


OUR GERMAN division left the ruins of Fort Anne 

and lay for a while near Castleton, watching for an 
enemy who never appeared; and early in August Gen- 
eral Johnny called our whole contingent back to the 
main army. 


Their drums struck up accompanied by the cracking of rebel rifles; the prenadiers burst into song 


Baron Riedesel confided to me that he had dispatched 
Captain Willoe of the brigade staff to go to Madame 
Riedesel in Canada and conduct her, her children and 
servants, and the Baroness von Lessing to his head- 
quarters. 

‘Tam sorry I did it,’ said he, ‘“‘but I thought, when 
we arrived at Forts Anne and Edward, that we were at 
least on the edge of civilization.”’ 


uk ames he was still talking to me, General Johnny, 
followed by General Phillips, General Fraser and 
Colonel Baum, came in with Sir Francis Clerke in attend- 
ance and followed by that chattering jackass ‘‘ Colonel’ 
Skene. 

After salutes and bows all around, our orderlies laid a 
cloth on headquarters table and there was a smoking 
pot-pie of wild pigeon, cold venison and several dozen 
of claret. 

“*General,’’ said General Johnny to Riedesel, ‘‘your 
dragoons ought to be mounted!”’ 

“I know that,”’ said Riedesel with a shrug; ‘‘but I 
cannot mount them on the wild deer of these forests.’’ 

There was a general laugh. 

General Johnny laid his hand on the shoulder of 
“Colonel’’ Skene: 

“‘Sir,’’ said he to the baron, *‘our military intelligence 
officer, who has lived long in these regions and who 


knows the temper of the settlers better than anybody 
else, assures me that the entire region beyond Fort 
Miller is loyal to His British Majesty; that the peasantry 
only await our appearance among ’em to rise and join 
us; and, best of all, that these honest settlers are ready 
and eagg¢ to furnish us with fresh provisions; and, more 
than that, that they will fetch us enough horses to 
mount your regiment of dragoons!” 

There ensued a sort of charmed and happy silence, 
broken dryly by the baron: ‘‘And where, sir, is this 
Arcadian Utopia and its celestial inhabitants who are 
going to do all these things for us?”’ 

Then that empty-headed Philip Skene let loose like a 
squirrel: “‘The rebels are badly scared! Believe me, 
gentlemen, all they dare hope for is a little plunder from 
our abandoned camps—a bone here—a rag there—for 
they've scarce a pair o’ breeches or a crumb o’ bread 
among ‘em! All we have to do is to march out to Fort 
Miller. They all know me; I can furnish you five hun- 
dred recruits in a day! I can furnish cattle, horses, for- 
age, provisions. 

‘*All you need do is to send Colonel Baum with the 
dragoons to Bennington where the rebels have a vast 
depot of supplies; and I pledge you my honor, every 
dragoon will return on horseback driving before him 
enough fat cattle to supply the entire army with beef on 
this campaign!”

from sheer lack of wind and

Johnny, beaming effulgently

I alone heard our baron’s 
guttural but smothered: 
“Herr je! Gott bewabr uns!” 

Toward evening I was 
standing before my tent, 
sorting letters which had 
just arrived by Quebec cou- 
rier and sealing in a single 
packet such as should be 
carried forward to General 
Riedesel who, with Lieuten-

escorted by Colonel Baum’s 
dismounted cavalry, had 
pushed on toward Fort Miller 
—wherever that might be. 
The plan was General 
Johnny's; Riedesel dissent- 
ing; Skene urging. So my 
baron started. 


JADED couriers con- 

tinued to arrive with 
certain news that Colonel 
St. Leger had invested Fort 
Stanwix and that the main 
rebel army was at Stillwater 
near Albany. 

German and British in- 
fantry were still marching 
out of Fort Edward toward 
Fort Miller when I sealed 
the packet for German head- 
quarters and sent the ex- 
press galloping off to my 
baron; and with it, a dirty 
noisy body of Canadian 
Indians went off yelling and 
flourishing hatchet and gun, 
followed by the Canadian 
battalion, now shrunk to 
half its numbers by incessant 
desertion. 

Just then Lord Petersham 
rode up. I gave him the 
mail sack for General Bur- 
goyne. Some of St. Luc’s 
savages, loping along, 
paused to stare at Peter- 
sham’s brilliant scarlet uni- 
form; and one of them even 
had the impudence to finger 
his polished silver spurs. 

“Get out, you greasy 
dog!’’ said Petersham kick- 
ing at him with stirruped boot. 

St. Luc, in his gaudy buckskins and fringes, heard him 
and shouted, in fury, *‘The commander-in-chief shall 
hear of this! I'll have you to know, Monsieur, that I 
and my Indians are to be respected and not insulted in 
this manner!”’ 

‘‘Be pleased, Monsieur, to go to the devil,”’ said I, 
“and take your savages with you!”’ 


eho a THAT he pulled at his belted hatchet and I 
took my horse pistol by the barrel and started 
toward him with every determination to knock his 
painted face in; but our German chaplain, Brudenel, 
seized me, and Monsieur Langlade grabbed St. Luc and 
fairly dragged him away among his excited savages 
who immediately resumed their trail, yelling and caper- 
ing their derision and defiance. 

Petersham said to me: ‘‘We are certain to hear of 
this, Mr. Seadrift.”’ 

‘‘These Frenchmen and their savages will abandon us 
at our first reverse anyway,”’ said I. **Let them go now, 
if they choose.”’ 

“Yes,” said he uneasily, ‘“‘but General Johnny has 
been at some pains to placate them; and if St. Luc runs 
to him with loud and Gallic cries, you and I may be sent 
home in disgrace.”’ 

That wasn’t a pleasant prospect. But I very soon 


26 


forgot the matter, being burdened with more impor- 
tant business incident to my baron’s recent departure 
toward Fort Miller. 

But now that empty-headed ass Skene came to inter- 
rupt me and inquire for his own private mail. 

Captain George Pausch, the competent Hessian gun- 
ner, particularly disliked Skene; and when the latter 
went galloping off with his mail, broke out into gut- 
tural curses. 

“Ach,” said he, ‘‘that dummer Kerl will be the ruin of 
the army yet!"’ 


FREEMAN of our staff came upand, overhearing us, 
said with a hearty oath: ‘The trouble with this 
army is that we have been underestimating the ability of 
the rebel officers and the courage of the Continentals.”’ 
‘* Always and ever since I drew sword,”’ I replied, ‘‘in 
the cause of King and Government, I have protested 
against the contemptuous attitude of British officers and 
soldiers toward my misguided American countrymen. 
But it does no good. And sometimes, gentlemen, I al- 
most wish I believed what such honest folk as Generals 
Greene and Washington believe—that my King really is 
a tyrant, and that British government is a failure! So 
exasperated do I become sometimes by the silly talk I 
hear—"’ 

“Oh, stop your prattle,’’ said Freeman. ‘‘I have 
plenty of trouble myself with those thirty wagons.”’ 

General Johnny had thirty wagons for his own per- 
sonal baggage; and these, with the enormous train of 
supplies, battery after battery of artillery, herds of 
cattle, horses, oxen, and more than four hundred women, 
were becoming a terrific strain on the resources and 
movements of this army. 

Pausch remarked bitterly that the plan was to cut 
down the artillery but not General Johnny's thirty 
wagons. 

As I turned away and entered the marquee to 
superintend the packing up of my baron’s more personal 
baggage, one of General Phillips’ orderlies rode up to 
inform me that some ladies had just arrived in one chaise 
and a big caléche and were inquiring for Baron Riedesel 
and for me. 

You may believe that I left the marquee in a hurry 
and at the lintel almost stumbled over Baroness von 
Riedesel and her children. The little ones cried out joy- 
ously at sight of me and ran and clung to my arms and 
legs and sword so that I could scarce make out to kiss 
the kind plump hand the baroness extended to me. 

Then, looking anxiously around, I caught sight of my 
beautiful Baroness Lessing, lovely beyond words. 

That was a heavenly moment! I called a courier and 
sent him galloping to Fort Miller to find my baron. 


WE SEATED ourselves on the trunk of a felled 

maple tree facing the river. Delicate tints of sunset 
illumined her face; she sat motionless, her narrow 
clasped hands resting on her knee. 

‘Are you truly rejoiced to see me, Mr. Seadrift?’’ she 
asked smilingly. 

‘* Life is a very miserable affair when you are absent!” 
I said so earnestly that she seemed a trifle disconcerted. 

“I hoped you had been cured by this time of any 
ardent intent toward me.”’ ; 

‘I am long since beyond the aid of any physician 
save one, Madam.”’ 

“Do you think your case so desperate? Wait, my 
friend, until you encounter the next and newest de- 
moiselle whose pretty but mindless features stir your 
inflammable fancy to romantic flame!—and I don’t 
mean Bettina Follaire, either,’’ she added somewhat 
hastily as a handsome fresh-faced girl walked past. 

“It is of real love I speak, Madam.” 

‘*Oh, what is ‘real love,’ after all?’’ said she with gay 
impatience. 

“* Madam, you speak like one disillusioned. But that 
cannot be, because all know you are happily married.”’ 

‘Do you also believe it, sir?”’ 

‘*I—have been sorry to suppose so.”’ 

“Oh. And, supposing so, you have dared tell me that 
you are in love with me?” 

‘“T can’t help being in love with you, Madam.’ 

‘But you can help making love to me, can’t you?” 

“No,” said I sulkily, ‘I can’t.” 

Just then we caught sight of some ladies walking with 
General Phillips. Two of them were handsome—one, 
on the arm of Lord Balcarres, particularly so; and 
caught my eye again—for she was that same fresh-faced 
damsel who had just passed. 

“Who are they?’ lasked curiously—already guessing. 

‘The taller is Captain Foy’s wife. The other mi—’’ 
I thought she was going to say ‘‘minx,’’ but she said 
“‘person’’—and went on coolly—'‘the other person is 
Mrs. Foy’s younger sister, Bettina Follaire. And the 
three others, with Sir Francis Clerke and Major Acland, 
are Mrs. Harnage, Mrs. Reynall and Major Acland’s 
lady. . . . Will you be pleased to show me my tent?”’ 


We rose and walked toward the tent which stood next 
to the Baroness Riedesel’s quarters. 

The Baroness von Lessing demurely gave me my 
congé with a low-murmured thanks; and I went over to 
General Burgoyne’s headquarters, all a-tremble with 
adoration. 

The general-in-chief, it appeared, was supping pri- 
vately with the lively Mrs. Lightways. So I continued 
on to the tent of his aids-de-camp, where I discovered 
them also at supper and engaged in excited conversa- 
tion. 

They bade me welcome, making room for me to sup 
with them, and I seated myself and listened in some sur- 
prise to what they were saying. 

It seemed that General Johnny had just come back 
from the advanced corps beyond Fort Miller and was to 
return this same night in order to continue in touch with 
Fraser's light troops and Indians who had crossed the 
river to Saratoga. 

‘We are packing up,”’ said Sir Francis Clerke to me. 
“‘Breymann has marched over the Batten Kill; and it 
seems that Colonel Baum, with the dragoons, has been 
in contact with a body of rebel riflemen somewhere near 
a mill of some sort, on the Bennington road. He has 
sent back word that he needs more troops and General 


Johnny intends to order Breymann to reinforce him if 


necessary. We're all going on tonight. By the way, 
your baron is on his way here to meet his wife and 
children.”’ 

It had grown so dusky in the tent that candles were 
lighted. But I remained only long enough to taste a 
glass of wine; then made my adieux and walked back 
toward German headquarters where the Baroness 
Riedesel and the children anxiously awaited the baron’s 
return. 


>! WAITED upon Madame Riedesel; the children 
were abed and asleep; our plump baroness was eating 
sausage. 

“Ach, dear Herr Lieutenant,’ said the pretty blue- 
eyed lady, ‘‘do you really believe that my husband will 
arrive tonight?” 

“Yes,” said I, ‘‘General Burgoyne’s staff officers say 
he is on his way. But it is a long ride, Madam, and he 
may not arrive until very late. You should lie down and 
try to sleep a little.”’ 

Toward ten o'clock she finished her supper and con- 
sented to lie down on her husband's camp bed; and I 
went out into the still star-spangled night. And, pass- 
ing the unlighted tent of the Baroness von Lessing, saw 
her standing there 

When she noticed me she merely gazed at me without 
any greeting; but suffered her hands to remain in mine 
as I bent and covered them with kisses. 

‘Herr Lieutenant,’’ said she, ‘‘do you think that you 
really do care for me? I mean gently and generously?” 
“In every way, Madam, as God listens to me—"’ 

“IT beg you not to be perpetually kissing me. I need 
counsel, not kisses, Herr Lieutenant. . . . Very well, 
you may keep your sword arm around me if you think it 
necessary. . . . I have to tell you that I've just re- 
ceived a very strange letter from General Burgoyne. He 
writes me that he has received from Colonel Faucitt in 
Germany a letter which troubles him; and that this let- 
ter concerns me.” 


A CHILL crept through me. I had quite forgotten 
ate this charming woman was considered danger- 
ous; and had proved to be so; and that I had been detailed 
to watch her behavior if she came to the army and to ar- 
rest her if she attempted to stir up discontent among the 
German troops or incite them to desert to the enemy. 

I said with an effort: ‘‘Madam, your sympathy with 
the rebel cause is known to Colonel Faucitt. It is known 
in England also. Naturally your actions are watched. 
But personal opinion is one thing and personal conduct 
another. And I cannot believe that because in Germany 
vou and your husband bitterly opposed the hiring of 
German troops, you now would attempt to sow discon- 
tent and mutiny among them here in America.”’ 

“And if by any unfortunate chance my innocent be- 
havior should displease General Burgoyne? Or you?” 

‘*Whatever your opinions,’ said I, “‘you would be 
mad to meddle. You would be stupid! And God knows 
you are not that.” 

She gave me a shyly humorous look: ‘‘God knows, 
sir, that I have all I can do to defend my person and my 
virtue without attempting to seduce an entire German 
army to misconduct!" 

She stepped nearer and laid her hands against my breast. 

‘*Moreover,’’ said she, ‘‘I have a very desperate 
business to occupy me—which is to keep you faithful 
to the romantic passion which you say you have con- 
ceived for me. And not permit some other woman to 
ensnare you.”’ 

‘*Madam, I love you madly—”’ 

Her uncontrolled laughter interrupted and exasperated 


me, and the next instant I had her in my arms and 
covered her face with kisses. 

Fragrant, flushed, dumb, she endured the embrace as 
though hopeless of avoiding the tender storm which her 
mockery had evoked. 

God knows what my broken words and sighs were 
telling her or what she replied; but of a sudden the 
drumming of heart and pulse was stilled as the heavy 
gallop of horses dominated their tumult. 

She sprang back into her tent, as I, torn from my 
mad dream, released her and turned, dully confused, to 
be blinded by the red glare of torches in my eyes and 
found myself surrounded by a whirlwind of Hessian 
horsemen. 


I SAW Baron Riedesel pull in his big sweating 

battle horse, swing his bulky body to earth, hold 
out his arms toa plump white barefoot figure at the door 
of his own tent. 

“Thou, Seadrift!’’ called a familiar voice from a 
spangled saddle—and I recognized Ensign Count von 
Guilder, astride a madly dancing horse. 

‘From where do you arrive, Kurt?"’ I asked. 

‘From the Mculin de Sainte Croix where our dragoons 
are in noisy touch with the enemy! Our baron goes 
back there in the morning. Have you heard what Brey- 
mann is doing this side Batten Kill?”’ 

I had not heard. Sir Francis Clerke touched my el- 
bow: ‘‘ Better go to bed, Seadrift, for I think Baum is 
making heavy weather of it out Bennington way. 
We'll all be in the saddle again by sunrise.”’ 

I slept fitfully but waked before daybreak and got up. 
A driving mist dampened the air. It was Friday, the 
fifteenth of August. 

When at leng-h I was dressed, and had swallowed a 
cup of coffee, I went out. 

My orderly whispered that an express had just ridden 
in from Colonel Baum and that the officer of the guard 
had taken the packet to General Burgoyne. 

Even as he sdoke I saw Sir Francis Clerke coming 
toward me. 

‘‘Where is General Riedesele’’ he whispered. 

**Asleep.”’ 

*““Well,’’ said he, ‘‘you must awaken him. General 
Burgoyne wishes him to order Breymann to join Colonel 
Baum.” 

‘Very well,”’ said I, ‘‘come on.”’ 

At the door of the marquee I saw the chasseur, old 
Rockel, fussing about and sent him in to call the baron. 

He came to the tent door in his nightshirt, blinking, 
troubled, squinting at us through the candlelight. 

‘Read it,’’ said he to me bluntly. 

The note from General Johnny said that Breymann 
was to be ordered to Bennington in consequence of good 
news received f-om Baum. 

‘Good news!”’ grunted my honest baron. *‘ Strange 
orders for good news, gentlemen.’’ And, to Sir Francis: 
“T thank you, Herr First Adjutant. Have the goodness 
to make acceptable my compliments to General Bur- 
goyne, and say to him that his orders shall be obeyed!"" 

It was raining hard when George Pausch rode out 
with his Hessian guns, ammunition and wagons. 

‘*No,"’ said he leaning from his wet saddle to shake 
my hand, ‘I am not going to Breymann. He moves 
with his guns from the Batten Kill."’ 

As the artillery jolted by, General von Riedesel in 
full uniform came out of his tent and took our salutes 
impassively. 

“*Damnable weather,"’ he grunted. Then, gutturally, 
in my ear: “‘Let General Burgoyne give his own orders. 
Isent a Brunswick staff captain to him so that it might 
be plain that the orders go directly from him to Brey- 
mann. . . .. What a cursed idea! And plenty of British 
light troops for the job. . . . I wash my hands of the 
whole business. I am ordered, but never consulted. Or, 
if consulted, I am bullied and overruled!’’ 


&N\ON THAT gloomy Friday, under sagging skies 
FA through a heavy rain, the rear guard, train, 
baggage, beef herd and troops of our strange army 
marched for the Batten Kill. 

I don't exactly know when General Johnny departed; 
he and his staff were already gone before our head- 
quarters moved cumbrously out over a flooded road 
nearly hub-deep in mud. 

That distressing and ponderous German march to the 
Batten Kill was a heart-breaking and miserable affair. 
Lieutenant Willoe and I were detailed to assist the 
ladies; and so, through the driving rain, I sometimes 
had an opportunity to ride beside the rickety old chaise 
of my inamorata. 

A war party of savages in their rain-bedewed paint 
kept pace with us; and though St. Luc had gone off 
with the bulk of the Indians, I did not like to see these 
ferocious warriors loping along like wolves. 

Twice we had to slash at them with our riding whips 
to drive them away from our [CONTINUED ON PAGE 36]

be achieved by tempering the sunlight 
with Venetian blinds and crisp organdie 
curtains at both French doors and windows.

tan, the room pictured at the right is 
washable, and that unusual and refreshing 
virtue is found also in the attractive stick

*“WHAT CAUSES frost in my refrigerator and why 
must it be removed?”’ 
That is the first question most women—three out of 
every four—ask about mechanical refrigerators after a 
little experience with them. With warm weather ap- 


proaching and bringing thicker blankets of ‘‘icebox 
snow’’ this phenomenon willplague you more and more. 
There just isn't any way to avoid it. We can tell you 
what causes it, how to remove it and how to save money 
in doing so, but not even the engineers can stop it. 
What does cause frost in mechanical refrigerators? 
Exactly the same thing which causes a film to form ona 
cold doorknob if you blow on it on a winter morning. 
The ice-cube trays and the compartment in which they 
are lodged are very cold, like your doorknob, and the 
air in the refrigerator is comparatively warm and moist. 
When it strikes the metal surfaces its moisture becomes 
frozen and adheres. Layer after layer it piles up. 


TO UNDERSTAND this subject of frost let’s fol- 

low a breath of air into your refrigerator. As you 
open the door, air rushes into the food storage compart- 
ment, which is of course much cooler than your kitchen. 
Being warm, it does what all warm air does—rises. It is 
then that it comes into contact with the cold surface of 
the ice-cube compartment. The moisture in the breath 
of air—or some of it—clings to the cold metal surface 
and stays on as frost. 

The air, now chilled, does what all cold air does— 
falls. It drops toward the lowest shelf, displacing the 
slightly warmer air there. This air in turn rises, comes 
into contact with the metal surfaces, leaves more of its 
moisture and falls. Thus a constant circulation is set up. 

Sooner or later of course in an unopened refrigerator, 
all the moisture would be drawn from the air, but every 
time you open the door you admit more moisture. This 
may be moisture in foods, or on the dishes themselves, or 
moisture in the air; humidity, we call that. So you 
can see that the more use you make of your refrigerator, 
the more warm air and moisture you admit; which is to 
say that the more circulation you set up, the more frost 
vou induce. A merry-go-round indeed, for the refrigera- 
tor is of no good unless it is used. 

One more thought about this movement of air. 
Don't crowd the storage compartment, for you will 
hamper the circulation. It is the hampering of the cir- 
culation rather than the bulk of the foods you put in the 
refrigerator which raises the temperature, for most 
mechanical refrigerators are so geared up that the 
warmer the things you put into them are, the harder 
the refrigerators work to keep them cool. It is for this 
reason that warm water, put in an ice-cube tray, will 
often freeze more rapidly than cold water. 


NOW LET us assume that when we first opened the 
door and admitted our breath of air to the refrigera- 
tor the ice-cube compartment was free of frost. The 
metal surfaces were able, quickly and efficiently, to 
draw the moisture from the air. As layer after layer of 
frost accumulates, the efficiency of the metal in accumu- 
lating moisture goes down. 
At first the layer of frost is, let us say, like a sheet; 


ALTHEA 


LEPPER 
Household Engineer 


ILLUSTRATOR: 
ETHEL COBHAM 


but soon it becomes like a blanket, warm and clinging. 
The circulating air cannot touch the metal, but comes 
into contact only with frost, a poor conductor of heat. 

The accumulation of frost causes the temperature to 
rise in both the ice-tray compartment and the box itself. 
It reduces the efficiency of the machine. Perhaps you 
have noticed, when there is a frost blanket in the re- 
frigerator, how sluggishly it responds even when you 
set the cold control for lower temperatures. 


SUCH AN effective insulator is frost that when it 
reaches a thickness of one inch, the temperature of 
the entire refrigerator is raised about ten degrees—suf- 
ficient to make it incapable of preserving some foods. 
A temperature of fifty degrees in the refrigerator is 
recognized as the ‘‘health line’’ and above this it should 
not go. Most mechanical refrigerators are normally 
regulated to read sixteen degrees in the chilling unit and 
about forty-four degrees in the food compartment. 
Thus, if frost is permitted to accumulate to a thickness 
of an inch, the temperature in your refrigerator probably 
is fifty-four degrees—or four degrees above the maximum 
to arrest the growth of organisms causing spoilage. 
Remember too, your refrigerator is set to operate in 
kitchen temperatures of seventy to seventy-five degrees. 
For every degree rise beyond that normal—in summer or 
during the time you are cooking dinner—the tempera- 
ture inside the average box will rise one-quarter degree. 
Despite insulation, mechanical refrigerators cannot be 
made entirely insusceptible to outside temperatures. 
Just how often, then, should the refrigerator be de- 
frosted? Half an inch of frost should be about the maxi- 
mum allowed. Beyond that, the efficiency of the ma- 


You can cut your fuel bill twenty 
per cent by intelligent use of the re- 
frigerator; defrosting at the proper 
time may account for half this saving. 


Awoman opens her refrigerator on 
an average of forty-eight times a day. 


With many mechanical refrigera- 
tors continuous operation saves fuel. 


chine will be impaired, the food will be in danger of 
spoilage and there will be danger of odor; for a thick 
frost blanket readily collects and distributes food odors. 

Engineers who have made laboratory tests, with’al- 
lowance for seasonal temperature, have discovered that 
an empty refrigerator, opened only six times a day, ac- 
cumulates, on its chilling unit, a frost deposit ot a 
thirty -second of an inch every twenty-four hours. 
And since you open your refrigerator on an average of 
forty-eight times a day (one manufacturer learned that 
from questionnaires to more than one hundred thousand 
women), and since you place warm moist dishes and 
foodstuffs in it, your chilling unit will accumulate at 
least as much as those in the test boxes used by the en- 
gineers. By simple arithmetic you should defrost at 
least every sixteen days. Better make it every two weeks 
in summer. 


THE ACTUAL method of defrosting depends upon 

the kind of refrigerator you have. Most of those 
now being sold are equipped with controls which per- 
mit automatic defrosting. When the controls are set at 
the defrosting point, the flow of operating fuel—elec- 
tricity or gas or kerosene—is reduced and the box warms 
up enough within eight or ten hours to melt the frost. 

In models without automatic defrosting controls, it 
is always good practice to turn off the fuel, remove the 
ice cubes and refill the trays with hot water until the 
frost melts off into a pan placed beneath the ice com- 
partment. Never chip the frost off. You run the risk 
of damaging the chilling unit. 

Engineers have estimated that fuel consumption may 
be decreased as much as twenty per cent by careful and 
intelligent use of the machine. That is more than two 
months’ bills in a year! Defrosting at the proper time 
may account for half this saving by relieving the chill- 
ing unit of the extra work it must do when it attempts 
to overcome the insulating effect of a thick frost blanket. 
The other half may beachieved by observing simplerules. 


&_.N KEEP THE food covered to prevent excessive 
moisture from forming extra frost. 

Arrange foods you use most near the front. This will 
enable you to close the door quickly and will tend to 
reduce the fuel consumption. Try to eliminate some of 
the average forty-eight openings a day. When you are 
making a cake, remember to take the milk, the butter 
and the eggs out of the refrigerator at the same time. 

Place the containers in such a way that there can be 
circulation of air. 

If you have a gas refrigerator keep it going all the 
time. Continuous operation of this kind of mechanical 
refrigerator is an important factor in fuel saving, 
paradoxically enough. With many electric refrigerators 
too, it is false economy to shut off the fuel for a period 
of even a few days, for the quantity required to rechill 
the box and refreeze the water in the ice-cube trays 
often amounts to more than the fuel required for con- 
tinuous operation. Some manufacturers have equipped 
their controls with a special ‘‘vacation’’ temperature 
setting which will hold the box at a reasonable tem- 
perature and allow it to consumeonly a minimum of fuel.

GRACE TABOR 


NE THING invariably reminds 

me that a pool of one kind or 

another is probably the greatest 
asset a garden can have. That is my 
own pool! ripe and quite near 
the French window leading out to the 
garden, it meets my gaze whenever I 
glance up and its surface is always 
significant and lovely. 

Every garden needs a body of water 
to round it to completion. The size 
is not important except that it should 
be in scale with the proportions of the 
garden generally. The form is impor- 
tant, because the form has much to do 
with one aspect of a pool that is of 
great value. This is the contemplative 
influence—that still reflective calm 
which reacts so favorably on human 
restlessness and nerves. 

A circular pool embodies in the 
highest degree all the properties that 
contribute to this contemplative ef- 
fect. Its uninterrupted shape is, next 
to the sphere itself, the most perfect 
form that can be conceived. It is the 
one shape that will fit perfectly into 
every garden and into every human 
consciousness without friction. 

In a small garden and in intimate re- 
lation to the dwelling, the need to 
choose form carefully is perhaps more 
pressing than in a wider area —just as 
the need throughout the small garden 
for perfection in plan, proportion and 
excellence of detail is greater than in 
the large garden, where perspective 
will in general diminish the effect of 
errors that are not too flagrant. Close 
to the average house the type of pool 
needs to be in accord with its sophisti- 
cated eavironment—its construction 
exact, architectural, frankly man- 
made. Remember always that you 
cannot create a convincing impression 
thata pooloranything else is ‘‘natural’”’ 
when its surroundings are manifestly 
not in a state of nature. The thing to 
do is to design and execute the feature 
with such care that it introduces ex- 
cellence and beauty expressive of hu- 
man consciousness and imagination. 


HH YOUR pool shall be made 
depends upon you and your 
garden of course. There are several 
ways of making pools simply, cheaply 
and successfully. The best, because it 
is permanent and because it allows of 
different rim treatment, is the con- 
crete pool worked directly on the 
earth over a reinforcement of chicken 
wire. I have never known a pool made 
in this way to crack and I do not see 
how such a pool can crack—unless 
there has been some fault in the mixture 
of cement or a gap between spreading 
it so that uneven drying has occurred. 

It is imperative to spread each coat 
in its entirety at one time, without in- 


M* DEAR Miss Tabor: Last spring we had a garden mirror 
made by the directions given in your article in the Com- 
PANION, and it has been greatly admired. I am sending you a 
photograph of it. The motto around the edge of the pool is: 


So, since the universe began 
And till it shall be ended, 

The soul of nature, soul of man, 
And soul of God are blended 


I wrote the motto off free-hand as quickly as possible before the 
cement could get dry. I didn’t have any special tools, just a little 
wooden skewer and a slightly larger stick with well-rounded 
point. A small tool that the gardener called a cold chisel was use- 


ful to make a straight edge. 


terruption or intermission. And it is 
important to use enough cement to in- 
sure a wall as strong as it can be made. 
This can be done by following the di- 
rections for proportioning the mixture 
furnished by every cement company. 

Where a cement pool is too much of 
an undertaking it is still possible to 
construct a very good pool with tar 
roofing paper and cement. I can’t 
promise that such a pool will be per- 
manent though it may last many years 
if its seams are well cemented and the 
entire interior thickly painted with 
waterproofing, and if no plants are 
grown in earth directly on the bottom 
of it or in the earth beside it. 

One such pool that I know of—a 
large and lovely naturalistic one at the 
edge of a woods—was hopelessly in- 
jured by the roots of plants penetrat- 
ing tiny crevices and opening these 
into wide gaps through which of 
course the water escaped. Marginal 
planting of grass to bring the turf 
right to the water's edge will do no 
harm, but such vigorous growth as 
reeds, bulrushes or mints are taboo. 

Whether you choose the circular or 
another form let me urge you to have 
no corners, no angles either outside or 
inside a pool. Hollow out the ex- 
cavation with rounded sides always— 


Epitu W. Tait 


do not cut straight down and then 
across. Such an interior provides unde- 
sirable catch-spaces for litter and 
rotting leaves, making it hard to clean 
out, and offers fatal resistance to the 
expansion of water when freezing 
occurs. The form that best meets wide 
temperature variations is the rounded 
saucer-shape, as shallow or as deep as 
desired. This offers practically no re- 
sistance to freezing of either the sur- 
rounding ground or the water. 

There is no horticultural purpose in 
such a pool as I am here urging for 
every garden. It is the water for the 
water's sake and not for the sake of 
growing water lilies or other aquatic 
plants. Ifa water lily seems desirable to 
complete the picture, choose one of the 
miniature forms for arry pool less than 
twelve to fifteen feet in diameter; for it 
is important to have twice as much 
open water surface as of lily pads. 

By all means grow a water lily (the 
miniature type of which I have spoken 
can be grown in a tub of water); but 
keep your garden pool—one garden 
pool—open to the skies! 


NSTRUCTIONS for a concrete cir- 

cular pool are given in Loose-leaf 
Gardener Leaflet, Garden Pool Con- 
struction, sent for 3 cents postage.

Be sure the ham is Swift’s Premium. If it is a center slice, simply fry or broil it. 
(Martha Logan suggests serving it as illustrated with watercress and tomato 
slices spread with horse-radish and dotted with butter, broiled, and sprinkled 
with chopped green pepper.) If it is a whole or half ham, bake it this easy way: 
Place in a roaster; add 2 cups of water and cover the roaster. Bake in a slow oven 
(325°), allowing 21 minutes a lb. for a large whole ham; 25 minutes a |b. for 
smaller (up to 12 lb.) hams or half hams. When ham is done, remove from oven. 
Lift offrind. Score surface and dot with cloves; rub with mixture of 14 cup brown 
sugar and 1 tbsp. flour. Brown, uncovered, for 20 minutes in a hot oven (400°),

QUESTIONS 
Club Women Ask 


By ANNA STEESE RICHARDSON 


Director of the Good Citizenship Bureau 


pap a tiene are now in the 
making and many inquiries come 
to me from inexperienced club workers. 
Here are a few typical questions. 


I— What does the term President’s Day 
mean? 

President’s Day may be celebrated 
in honor of a club’s outgoing and in- 
coming presidents, or in honor of all 
the club’s presidents present and past; 
or it may be given by one club for the 
presidents of other clubs in the city or 
community. It may be marked by a 
program of speeches and music, fol- 
lowed by an informal tea; or it may 
start with a breakfast at twelve noon, 
or a luncheon at one o'clock followed 
by a program, with the president of 
the hostess club presiding. 


I1—What is meant by Reciprocity Day? 

Reciprocity Day seems to have vari- 
ous meanings also. In some com- 
munities it is marked by a special 
program in which a subject of common 
interest to a number of local clubs is 
discussed. A representative from each 
club is on the program. For this type 
of meeting each club receives in advance 
a complete outline of the different 
phases of the topic to be discussed. 
The opportunity to become informed 
on the subject makes for a lively dis- 
cussion and a successful meeting. 

Again, the reciprocity committee 
may arrange for an exchange of pro- 
grams with other clubs, thus: The 
Thursday Literary Club may have 
given an especially good program on 
American poetry while the Friday 
Morning Club may have had a success- 
ful program on early American novel- 


ists. The exchange of these outlines, 
with perhaps the original papers, will 
prove mutually agreeable. 

Finally, the term may mean a more 
personal exchange. The Women’s 
Club of Sunnyside has presented a fine 
musical program including vocal and 
instrumental numbers, or a delightful 
concert by its choral group. The 
Riverside Little Theater Group has 
presented a skit, a smart comedy or a 
pageant. So the musicians of Sunny- 
side travel to Riverside for a single 
performance and the Riverside players 
in turn present their skit or comedy in 
Sunnyside. 


III—How can we arouse interest in 
international affairs, the World Court 
and the League of Nations in our town 
where there is great bitterness among for- 
eign-born groups? 

Start by creating better feeling among 
the school-children from your various 
foreign-born colonies who will carry 
the peace message home to their par- 
ents. Here is a chance for parent- 
teacher associations and ieee of 
other women’s organizations to organ- 
ize ‘‘Better Neighbors’ gatherings. 
Present programs of folk-dancing and 
singing. Hold exhibitions of food and 
handicraft prepared by women of vari- 
ous nationalities. Have game nights 
in church parlors, community houses 
and school gymnasiums, at which men 
and women of various nationalities 
learn to play together. It can be done. 
It has been done. Start among children 
in schools. Let them carry the peace 
message home. The Good Citizenship 
Bureau will supply a list of peace plays 
and pageants easily presented.

Have a Good Vacation 


F you have been grinding 

away, month after month, 
you need a special tonic. It is 
the world-famous health builder, 
the blending of sunshine, fresh 
air, change of scene, rest and 
diversion—a vacation. 


Plan to enjoy a totally different 
kind of a life for a short time. 
New ideas, new scenes, new 
people afford recreation. And 
recreation is necessary to health 
and good spirits. Joy, pleasure 
and laughter invigorate mind 
and body. They help to tone 
up the entire system. 


What would you like to do in 
order to have a complete change? 
Motor, hike, or take a trip by 
rail or steamer? Will you go 
deep into the woods near a lake 


ora mountain? Or sun yourself 
on the beach at a summer resort? 
Active sports or quiet leisure, 


or both? 


But while you are happily plan- 
ning your vacation and thinking 
of the good times and the rest 
you will have, keep in mind that 
people are more likely to be hurt 
or to hurt themselves when in 
strange surroundings than when 
in familiar ones. Don’t let your 
vacation be spoiled by a need- 
less mishap. You can guard 
against most accidents. 


Metropolitan Life Insurance 
Company has prepared a book- 
let “First Aid” which tells you 
what to do in event of accidents. 
Send for your copy, read it and 
take it away with you. 


At Ocean, Lake or River 


In the Woods 


case, see a doctor. 


Anywhere 


The Metropolitan’s free booklet, “First Aid’ tells what to 
do and how to do it—at home as well as when you are away 
—in event of broken bones, burns, sprains, poisoning, ap- 
parent drowning, fire, wounds, electric shock, bites, sunburn, 
sunstroke and common accidents of various kinds. 


let “First Aid.” 


Name 


VACATION “DO’S” AND “DON’TS” 


Know how to resuscitate in cases of apparent drowning. 

Do not go in swimming when overheated, or within two hours after eating. 
Never go in bathing alone at any time, even if you area strong swimmer. 
Do not dive unless you are sure of the depth. 


Don’t drink from wayside springs, streams or strange wells, unless the 
water is boiled, in order to avoid intestinal or other disorders. 
If you come in contact with poison ivy or poison sumac, wash exposed 
part in at least five rinsings of soap and water. In a serious 


Break a burned match before dropping it, to be sure that the 
flame is extinguished. Never leave a fire or embers burning. 


In case of fire caused by gasoline or kerosene, smother flames 
with sand or dirt, or with blankets, coats or other heavy 
woolen articles. Never use water. 

Never throw away a lighted cigarette or cigar. Grind it out. 
Get a “First Aid” booklet and keep a First Aid kit at hand.

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


Love and the Lieutenant 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 26] 


baggage wagons; and de Langlade, in 
his wet deerskins, came back, furious, 
but got no satisfaction from us. 

‘Keep your dirty savages in hand,”’ 
said I, “‘or we'll do it for you! Now 
run to the commander-in-chief, if 
you choose, and tell him we don’t like 
you or your filthy savages!"’ 

He did go. And, to our surprise, 
Lord Petersham came back to sum- 
mon us forward to General Johnny. 

General Johnny, riding beside the 
chaise of Mrs. Lightways, was as gay 
and debonair as usual. 

“Young gentlemen,”’ said he, ‘‘we 
have no more than eighty Indian 
scouts left. Is it the part of wisdom 
to insult them? You know, Seadrift, 
I am told that St. Luc quitted us be- 
cause you used him very roughly.” 

I hung my head. 

““Maybe you don’t like Indians,”’ 
continued General Johnny, ‘‘but is 
that a reason for driving them out of 
our service? And I am free to inform 
you, young gentlemen, that the 
enemy's Indians—the Loup d’Orange 
—are beginning to annoy our out- 
posts. ...I pray you, be a little 
more discreet in airing your personal 
and petty prejudices, and show me a 
little more consideration.” 


NA WE RETIRED much chagrined 
Pan mortified by the just rebuke. 

This was not entirely an unin- 
habited wilderness we traversed. 
There were a few clearings in the vast 
and overpowering forests—a few 
hovels of logs—even two or three 
small clapboarded houses, dimly seen 
through the falling rain, where some 
wretched settler and his wife and 
ragged children peered out at the 
marching troops. 

And nowhere were any cattle, 
horses or provisions to be discovered 
by our foragers, and Cleve told me in 
a nervous whisper that the general-in- 
chief was surprised and worried— 
after all the optimistic promises of 
this man Skene—to discover that the 
settlers, for the greater part, had gone 
off to the rebel lines, driving away 
their sheep and cattle and taking every 
scrap of provision with them. 

Later I saw Freeman who told me 
that our Indians were getting out of 
hand, plundering and terrorizing the 
wretched folk in solitary and remote 
farms; that not a single recruit had 
come in to our lines; and that General 
Johnny smelled treachery somewhere 
but had not yet nosed out the source 
of the stench. 

‘*He wishes to see you,”’ said Free- 
man. ‘‘It seems that Ottawa Indians 
have been seen wearing necklaces of 
German gold coins and that two 
Canadian deserters have been caught 
with German money in their pockets.”’ 

“What do I know about these 
things?”’ said I irritably; but spurred 
my jaded horse forward through the 
rain and came finally upon partly 
cleared country where General John- 
ny’s marquee loomed up. 

What a wet and nasty mess was this 
Batten Kill camp, the ground shin- 
deep in mud; tents, baggage, uniforms 
saturated, and the forlorn trees drip- 
ping under somber skies. 

The officer of the guard conducted 
me to General Johnny’s marquee; and 
there sat the gay general eating a cold 
pigeon pastry and drinking claret with 
Mrs. Lightways. 

I bowed to them with perfect polite- 


ness and a wooden countenance; and 
Mrs. Lightways rose, curtsied to me 
and glided off through a canvas corri- 
dor toward another tent. 

When finally we were alone: ‘‘ Mr. 
Seadrift,’’ said General Burgoyne, 
“T’ve lost practically all of my Indians. 
This is serious. Also the Canadian 
battalion is so reduced by desertion 
that it is become negligible. This is 
more serious still. Most serious of all, 
however, is the increasing discontent 
among our German allies—the per- 
petual friction between General Riede- 
sel’s command and officers and soldiers 
of the British division. Have you, sir, 
any suspicion concerning the origin of 
any treasonable efforts among the 
Hessian or Brunswick troops?”’ 

“T, sir!’’ said I, startled. ‘‘No, sir, 
I have no suspicions that the troops 
are being tampered with.”’ 

“Let me,”’ said he, “‘represent for 
your consideration several unpleasant 
events of the past. In the first place, 
when I returned from England and a 
secret conference with His Majesty 
and ministers concerning this cam- 
paign, I had reason to believe that no- 
body excepting the King, his ministers 
and myself knew what we had planned. 
But I had not been in Canada a week 
before I learned that every private and 
secret detail of our plan was not only 
generally known in Canada, but 
equally well known to General Wash- 
ington and his army. 

‘Following that, every move we 
made found the enemy alert and pre- 
pared. Our Indians deserted us; our 
Canadians are dwindling; our Ger- 
mans are slow and seem unwilling. 
Every single scout, forest runner, ex- 
press or messenger of any description 
that I have sent south to General 
Howe has been captured and hanged!”’ 


ISTARED at him in undisguised 
astonishment. 

“Every one,’ he repeated. ‘‘It 
would seem that at least a single 
messenger might have got through. 
None has. And if Generals Howe and 
Clinton in New York ever even hear of 
them it will be because the rebel 
newspapers publish the dispatches of 
mine which these unhappy messengers 
carried. I don’t know whether Howe 
has started from New York to come 
up to meet me at Albany. I don't 
know that he knows Iam here at all.”’ 

Sir,’ said I hotly, ‘‘will you be 
pleased to send me to New Yorke” 

“Thank you, Mr. Seadrift,’’ said 
he with his winning smile, “‘but I 
have men for that business whom I 
can better spare than I can your good 
self." Then his handsome features 
darkened and he gazed at me intently. 

“Have you any reason to suspect,” 
said he, ‘‘that the Baroness von Less- 
ing could possibly be a source of mis- 
chief in this army?” 

My face burned scarlet: 
God, sir, no!’’ I blurted out. 

‘*God forbid I should suspect a 
lady,’’ he continued. *‘God forbid I 
mention it within General Riedesel’s 
hearing if I did suspect her. Why does 
she request of me a flag to Albany? 
Why does she choose such a time to 
visit relatives among the rebels of the 
Mohawk Valley? And one of them a 
fighting colonel of a fighting Con- 
tinental regiment? And once a Ger- 
man baron?” 

Miserable misgiving, jealous anger,

Where there’s a whine 


there’s a warning 


HEN BABY LAUGHTER turns to 

whining—and your simplest rep- 
rimand meets a storm of tears—don’t get 
irritated, mother. Get worried! 


For Nature often takes this way of 
saying: “Mother, this child isn’t well!” 


Don’t be misled, Mother 


There can be several causes for a child’s 
complainings. It can be sour stomach, or 
flatulence, or acid stomach. In babies, 
you can often suspect it is colic due to 
gas. Then—it may be Nature’s warning 
of a coming cold. Many, many times it is 
constipation—for child specialists warn 
us that this disorder affects 90% of all 
children—even those who are seemingly 
“regular.” 


In each of these cases, your wisest 
course is to give your child a laxative. 
But, mother, make very, very sure it is a 
child’s laxative. Little systems can be 
painfully upset by many laxatives made 
for grown-ups, 


Give Fletcher’s Castoria 


Fletcher’s Castoria is a safe, gentle laxa- 
tive—made especially for children, from 
babyhood to 11 years. Unlike castor oil 
and other harsh laxatives, it does not be- 
come an irritant in the bowels in order to 
increase their action. It gently stimulates. 


Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


And it contains NO NARCOTICS, does not 
disturb digestion. 


Children love it, for it has no nasty, 

“medicine” taste—they take it willingly. 
Get a bottle today. The family size is 
most economical. The signature Chas. I. 
Fletcher is always right on the carton. 
For the many occasions, mother, when chil- 
dren need a laxative—use Chas. H. Fletcher’s 
Castoria. From babyhood on, it is your best 
“first-aid’’ for colic due to gas, for diarrhoea 
due to improper diet, for sour stomach, flat- 
ulence, acid stomach. And every mother 
knows that a laxative is any doctor’s Ww 
first advice for treating a cold.

alarm, despair strove together in my

‘““Have you found in her behavior

‘‘I am eased to hear you say so. I

WHEN SHE saw me she made 
me a friendly gesture: ‘‘Come 
and sup with us!"’ she cried gayly. 

I bowed to her with stiff politeness 
and regretted that my duties carried 
me elsewhere. 

Lord, what a very flame of jealousy 
raged in my silly noddle; and Kurt’s 
complacent smirk set my blood a-boil. 

“Please, Herr Lieutenant,’’ she re- 
peated sweetly; but the triumphant 
mien of von Guilder was too much for 
me and I gave her a black look and a 
jetky bow and stalked off in a rage. 

My baron was very tired. What 
orders and communications he had 
for his troops and for general head- 
quarters I wrote out at his weary dic- 
tation; and he went to bed presently, 
leaving me to carry the portfolio to 
my tent and finish. 

Before I in my turn went to bed I 
had a tiresome search in my dispatch 
box and military chest for some 
memorandums, returns, copies of 
orders in duplicate already entered in 
headquarters orderly book, and several 
sets of military cipher on tissue, still 
under discussion for adaptation. 

I concluded that in the confusion 
they must have been deposited in 
General Riedesel’s tent; and had not 
the heact to disturb him at that hour 
for he really had seemed ill. 

So I extinguished my candle and lay 
down; and in spite of a jealous heart- 
ache and dark design against this 
smirking rival of mine, I fell asleep. 
"It was still dark when the rays of a 
lantern dazzled me awake. 

Von Geismar, Riedesel’s staff ma- 
jor, was shaking my shoulder: ‘‘Get 
up,’’ he repeated; ‘‘Kurt von Guilder 
has gone to the enemy with a parcel of 
German and Canadian deserters!”’ 

I floundered to my feet, amazed. 

“He’s a spy,’ said the major 
hoarsely. ‘‘Colonel Faucitt suspected 
him. We've had him under observa- 
tion for months, but he’s been too 
smart for us. Tonight he was sur- 
prised beyond our outer posts, deliver- 
ing a packet to the enemy pickets.”’ 

““Where?”’ 

‘Beyond San Coick Mill. He has a 
dozen German, Provincial and Cana- 
dian deserters with him. He’s takena 
sack of headquarters documents. Our 
chasseurs surprised him in his treason 
but he and his deserters got away to 
the bush. Baum sends word that they 
can be surrounded by a light party 
operating between Owl Creek and 
Little White Creek. The general 
wishes you to take the Provincial 


battalion and try to drive Guilder and 
his deserters toward Breymann, who is 
marching on Bennington.” 

I was dressed, booted and cloaked 
by this time; an orderly had my horse; 
and as I hurried out into the darkness 
of early morning I saw two mounted 
officers of the Provincial riflemen wait- 
ing for me with about eighty men. 

General Riedesel came out of his 
tent with Cleve who carried a lantern. 
He looked ill and tired and very grim. 

He told me that Guilder had taken 
important military papers; that there 
was no slightest doubt he was and al- 
ways had been an intelligence officer 
in Washington’s army; that, like 
Baron Frederick von Weissenfels, he 
had shed his title and become an 
American farmer until the rebellion 
broke out, when he volunteered im- 
mediately and was sent back to Ger- 
many to enlist in, spy and report on 
the troops destined for America. 

“He is a rebel officer and is trying to 
do his duty. But catch him if you can, 
Seadrift, and let this army see what 
Washington's army has so often looked 
upon—a military execution of an 
enemy intelligence officer taken in his 
devilish line of duty!”’ 

As I touched my horse with my 
spurs, Lord Petersham came picking 
his way in, carrying a letter for me to 
give to Colonel Baum at Sancoik, or 
to Breymann—which was an order for 
him to send back all captured cattle, 
wheat and flour. 

““My lord,’’ said I, perplexed and 
exasperated, ‘‘am I to endeavor to 
overtake and arrest Ensign von 
Guilder and his deserters on Owl 
Creek, or am I to go to Colonel Baum 
with this dispatch?”’ 

“You go to Sancoix Mill first,”’ 
said he; and I saw General Riedesel 
bite his lip in the lantern light. 

I looked angrily again at. the ad- 
dressed letter: ‘‘Are all these places 
different—Sancoix, Sancoick, Ste. 
Croix, Saint Coick?—or is it defective 
education and bad spelling that seem 
to send me to four destinations at the 
same time?”’ 


> a ana laughed at my ill 
humor: ‘The two reasons for all 
this advance is to give a jealousy to 
General Arnold and to mount the Rie- 
desel dragoons at Bennington,"’ said 
he. ‘This army has other work to 
do than to chase German deserters." 

I saw my honest baron turn pale 
with rage; but Petersham coolly 
saluted him and turned his horse 
toward general headquarters. 

I looked at my general, too mad to 
speak, 

After a furious silence a very thun- 
der of profanity rolled from his out- 
raged lips: 

‘Everything planned collapses! 
Guilder escapes to the enemy with our 
papers and his deserters! Baum is 
checked; Breymann is trapped in the 
mud! So be it! These are none of my 
orders! Nevertheless, Herr Lieuten- 
ant, get on with that cursed letter!”’ 

I saluted, wheeled my nag and rode 
away into the blackness. 

At daylight on Saturday, the six- 
teenth, I piloted the weary detach- 
ment under the retired officer, Captain 
Wayland, to the lower Ow] Kill at its 
confluence with the Hoosic. 

Here a company of swarthy furtive 
Canadians joined us, sullenly_report-

Love and the Lieutenant 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 38] 


ing contact with Breymann, but pre- 
tending no knowledge of Colonel 
Baum’s advance beyond Little White 
Creek; and I had no doubt they were 
skulkers and were lying to me; but 
could do nothing more than set these 
hang-dog fellows to beating up the 
bush for von Guilder’s party. 

If they saw the renegades I do not 
know. Scouting ahead of our Pro- 
vircials they may have met them and 
treacherously assisted at their de- 
sertion. Anyway, some among them 
stole my letter as my poor nag stumbled 
and fell, and I sprawled, partly stunned, 
in an unexpected slough. 


WHEN I finally caught my horse 

and got into my saddle again, I 
heard distant firing. Wayland, leading 
his own gnat-tortured horse, camecurs- 
ing and stumbling through the brush 
to inform me that the Canadians had 
just met some Indians and these had 
suddenly turned and fired on our ad- 
vance guard from a wooded ridge. 

There was no use trying to ride on 
in this mess of bog and brier and 
thicket. Wayland was afoot; and I, 
dismounting and seizing my horse by 
the head, floundered across the soft 
black bottom-land; across the Owl 
Kill which, swollen by rain, swirled 
dark and foamy above our waists; and, 
scrambling up the slippery bank, came 
upon two score or more of our wet and 
weary Provincials under Lieutenant 
Graham, aligned in the open woods. 

Here, to our mortification, was no 
trace either of the Canadians or of the 
strange Indians they had met with and 
joined in the murderous attack on our 
advance guard. 

Neither of course was there any sign 
at all of Guilder and his deserters. 

Graham, a retired officer of the 
Indian Department, told us that he 
had caught a glimpse of the hostile 
Indians and that he took them for 
Loup d’Orange, scouts of the enemy. 

I cannot describe my shame and 
fury at being so treacherously tricked. 
But worse was to come for, calling up 
by bugle-horn, and assembling the 
loiterers of Wayland’s dawdling Pro- 
vincials who had lagged behind in the 
swamp and underbrush below, we had 
the mortification to discover that 
about thirty of them had deserted us! 

About this time I noticed a few 
wretched houses and presently espied 
the mill of San Coick just north of 
where the roads to the Batten Kill and 
to Bennington fork. 

And down the Bennington road 
came galloping that incomparable ass, 
‘Colonel’? Skene. He had several 
mounted German dragoons and a 
dozen wagons at his crazy heels and 
when he saw me he pulled up and 
yelled and flourished his cocked hat. 

“All's well!’ he shouted in his 
high-pitched gabbling voice. ‘* Baum 
is intrenching on the Waloomsac and 
his artillery are in position!”’ 

‘‘Where are you going?”’ I shouted. 

“To hurry up Breymann!’’ he 
bawled. “‘We've got ‘em now! All 
the country is rallying to our aid! 
Loyalists are swarming in to the 
colors! You'll know ‘em by the white 
badges in their hats. Bennington will 
be ours by noon!” 

With that he flourished his laced 
hat, clapped spurs to his horse and 
was off with his wagons, and _ his 
clumsy dragoons plunged after him. 


I gazed after him with deep aversion; 
then, signaling to Captain Wayland, 
continued on into the Bennington 
road which was merely a river of 
liquid mud. 

In a short time Captain Wayland’s 
battalion—what was left of it—had 
become so utterly exhausted that he 
was obliged to halt his men. 

I asked him sarcastically whether 
he considered them as light troops, 
and suggested that they unsling their 
packs.and leave them in the woods 
under guard. 

They did unsling them, but only to 
rummage for food. 

Presently a sudden and dazzling sun 
broke through the drizzle and beat 
down upon the earth so fiercely that 
all the landscape steamed. 

It must have been about the noon 
hour, and many soldiers lay asleep in 
the sun, when suddenly I heard a 
musket go off. Instantly our bugle- 
horn blew the alarm; and our stupe- 
fied fellows scrambled to their feet. 

Enemy Indians were plainly visible 
now, just across the Waloomsac, 
seeming very impudent and not afraid 


_ of revealing themselves; and even 


shaking their rifles and hatchets at us. 

They seemed to be Loup d’Orange 
scouts of the enemy; but the range was 
too great for our muskets; and their 
flashing rifles dropped only spent bul- 
lets into the water below us. 

Wayland, who was coolly examin- 
ing the country about us through a 
small field telescope, handed it to me. 

“*Sir,’’ said he quietly, ‘‘I fear we 
are in something of a pickle. The 
enemy seem to be in force ahead of us.”’ 

I took a good look through his glass 
at the hostile Indians; then turned the 
telescope northward. A large body of 
enemy militia was plainly visible, 
halted along the eastern bank of the 
river commanding the Bennington 
road. 

“Sir,”’ said Wayland, ‘‘they seem 
to be mustering along the right flank 
and rear of Colonel Baum’s command. 
T hope he knows it, because we cannot 
go forward to inform him until Brey- 
mann’s advance arrives.”’ 

It was perfectly true. There seemed 
to be a full regiment of rebel militia 
on the river. We had remaining to us 
between fifty and sixty Provincials. 
And the truth was that I secretly dis- 
trusted even these. 

*Sir,”’ said I to Wayland, ‘‘some- 
body should ride back and try to 
hasten Breymann. I think I had bet- 
ter try to find him.”’ 


SO I CLIMBED into my saddle 

once more and started back at a 
stiff trot toward San Coick Mill. And 
when [ came near San Coick bridge I 
saw a mounted man in military hat and 
cloak ride out across the other bridge 
and turn south toward Albany. 

Supposing him to be a staff officer 
from Breymann, bewildered by the 
two bridges, I spurred to overtake 
him. And came suddenly upon him at 
the forks of the lower road. 

And, as I galloped up beside him 
and he heard my horse and looked 
around, an astounding and terrible en- 
counter occurred. For I was staring 
into the strained and blanched features 
of the Baroness von Lessing. 

The next instant she had struck her 
horse with her spurs, but I caught him 
by the head, nearly throwing her from 


the saddle and clinging with difficulty 
to my own. 

“What are you doing here?”’ said I 
hoarsely. 

Her eyes blazed in her white face: 
“Take your hands from my bridle!”’ 
she gasped; and spurred her frantic 
horse again so that I was nearly 
dragged from my own nag to keep my 
hold unbroken. 

“Not until you show me by what 
authority you are here, and wearing a 
soldier’s uniform and cloak!”’ 

“Tam going to Albany!”’ she said. 

“Are you in your right mind, 
Madam? By whose leave do you travel 
alone in enemy country?” 

“By my own leave!”’ 

“Do you know that enemy savages 
are close at hand and watching this 
route? And enemy militia guarding 
it?”’ 

“‘Tam not afraid,’ said she. ‘‘ After 
all, it is my risk. Let me go.” 

But I now had collected my senses, 
anda most horrid suspicion was poison- 
ing my mind. 

““Madam,”’ said I, ‘‘listen to me. 
This army so far has lost nearly a 
thousand men by desertion. God for- 
bid I should suspect you of secretly 
fomenting trouble. But I am detailed 
to observe your behavior and to re- 
port it if suspicious. And,”’ said I, red 
as fire but staring her straight in the 
face, ‘‘were I a million times more in 
love with you than I am, I would 
carry out my orders.”’ 


THE COLOR came slowly back 
into her face; the white surprise 
and desperation faded. Then the fa- 
miliar raillery glimmered in her eyes. 
“Herr Lieutenant,’’ said she, ‘‘I 
have perhaps behaved like a spoiled 
child. I want to go to Albany. They 
would not give me a flag. So, when 
you rode out of camp, I borrowed your 
spare uniform and your led-horse.”’ 

I looked at her and at her nag in 
startled astonishment. It was true. 
I recognized my second-best clothing 
and my other horse. 

“*Madam,”’ said I, ‘‘yours is the 
maddest escapade I ever heard of! But 
I thank God it is nothing worse. I do 
not believe that you have been in 
treasonable communication with the 
enemy, or that you have secretly 
stirred up discontent among the Ger- 
man troops. Nevertheless, there have 
been clever rebel spies in our army, and 
last night your agreeable acquaint- 
ance, Ensign von Guilder, left camp 
secretly with a parcel of German, 
British and Provincial deserters.’’ 

If her astonishment was feigned she 
must have been an actress unsur- 
passed. I was at ease to believe her 
amazement genuine. 

‘““Now,”’ said I gently but gravely, 
“‘you will understand why I cannot 
permit you to go forward in this un- 
authorized reckless and utterly im- 
proper fashion.”’ 

‘“Do you suppose I also shall be 
suspected and imprisoned—perhaps 
even fetched before a military court?”’ 

‘Certainly you have risked all these 
penalties by your mad indiscretions,”’ 
said I. “‘I don’t know what General 
Burgoyne may think of this business.”’ 

“‘Ts it necessary to tell him?’’ she 
asked, naive and charming as a whee- 
dling child who begs one not to tell. 

“‘No,”’ said I, ‘‘there seems to be no
