[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 4o] 


need to worry him with a matter 
which, I am convinced, is no worse 
than the rash inconsidered impulsive 
behavior of a perverse, spirited and 
spoiled child.’’ 

She hung her head. ‘‘The Herr 
Lieutenant is very severe.” 

I drew a deep breath of relief. 

‘Ride close to me today,”’ said I, 
“and I will try to account for you, if 
questioned, and strive to take you 
back to camp under cover of darkness. 
Come, we should be pushing on.”’ 

We moved off at a walk, then spurred 
to a canter; and so approached the 
San Coick Mill where I discovered, to 
my vast relief, about eighty grenadiers 
of Breymann’s advance. 

Their tired and muddy officer, Cap- 
tain von Gleisenberg, said that Brey- 
mann, with chasseurs and grenadiers 
of the main body, was coming up very 
slowly. 

I suppose, if they noticed her at all, 
they mistook my culprit baroness for 
some fledgling staff youngster, in her 
long riding cloak, for the brim of her 
cocked hat shadowed her eyes and the 
high cloak-collar touched her ears. 

“T am going to ride to Colonel 
Baum,” said I, ‘‘and inform him that 
Breymann’s advance is at Saint Coix— 
or whatever they call this place.”’ 

All the officers waved their hands 
after returning my salute. 

Out of the corner of my eye I had 
noticed with amusement and relief the 
mute and correct salute of my naughty 
baroness; and, as we turned our horses 
and cantered north, I laughed and ex- 
pressed my approval. 

“You see, dear schoolmaster,”’ said 
she, ‘‘I try to make amends for play- 
ing truant.” 

I drew up beside her and passed one 
arm around her neck. ‘‘I forgive you, 
dear pupil,’’ said I and kissed her 
mouth. 

In a little while we rode up to our 
outposts who told me that Captain 
Wayland had marched, following the 
enemy Indians and militia, who 
seemed to be retreating. 


SO WE galloped across the bridge 

over Little White Creek and con- 
tinued northward at a smart pace until 
we overtook Wayland’s company 
which was already entering Baum’s 
lines. 

I asked for Baum; their officers 
pointed to a wooded hill on the crest 
of which I could see a log intrench- 
ment and the muzzle of a_three- 
pounder. 

So up the hill we climbed and there 
discovered Colonel Baum. I explained 
to him the position of Breymann and 
the probable hour of his arrival. 

Baum told me in his dull heavy 
way that there seemed to be only 
militia in front of him, but there were 
a great number of them. 

Spangenberg came up and we shook 
hands; and he led me forward where I 
could overlook the country below and 
pointed out the position of our de- 
tachments and those visible of the 
enemy. 

From this wooded hill the view was 
superb. Range on range of forested 
hills cut by river valleys stretched 
away to the huge ramparts of the 
Green Mountains. 

Here on the hill were posted our 
dismounted German dragoons and 
Fraser’s sharpshooters, behind log 
breastworks. . 


Beyond the river underneath us, 
southeast of the bridge, a Loyalist 
battalion lay behind a breastwork 
made of fence rails stuffed with flax. 
A few log cabins stood near by, one of 
them crowded with poor frightened 
camp women attached to Baum’s bat- 
talions. A company of our untrust- 
worthy Canadians held the bridge, 
supported by another three-pounder 
and by British and German infantry. 

Our treacherous Indians lay hidden 
along a sort of tableland to the left of 
the hill upon which we stood. 


WHILE Spangenberg and I talked 

together, Baumcameupand called 
our attention to numerous small and 
widely scattered groups of men who 
had suddenly appeared in the clearings 
and fields below, and were advancing 
toward our lines. 

Through a glass we could see them 
very plainly; groups of little companies 
of half a dozen to a score, all moving 
confidently toward us in plain sight— 
hundreds and hundreds of them com- 
ing in from every direction and all 
making straight for the bridge below 
and for our hill. 

They appeared to be country folk, 
in shirt and breeches, wearing no uni- 
form and seeming to have no officers. 

‘They all seem to be wearing white 
cockades in their hats,’’ said I. 

“That,’’ said Baum, ‘“‘is what 
Colonel Skene told them to wear so 
that we should know they are loyal’ 
folk come to join us and help us.”’ 

‘To me,”’ said Spangenberg bluntly, 
“they look like rebels who are trying 
to filter in by deceiving us.”’ 

At that moment, several officers in 
Continental uniform rode out within 
cannon range. 

“Give those fellows a shot, Spang- 
enberg,’’ said Baum. 

Bang! Our little three-pounder 
buried itself in smoke. 

Then suddenly and to our horror and 
Baum’s stupid astonishment, the sup- 
posed Tory farmers with the white 
cockades turned and poured a volley 
of musketry into the flanks and rear of 
our scattered detachments at the foot 
of our hill. 

A rattle of musketry broke out on 
the plateau where our cowardly 
Indians were hidden, and they ap- 
peared, fleeing before the white cock- 
ades. The Canadians also were 
stampeding in disorderly flight. 

A terrific roar of musketry and rifle 
fire arose from the bridge where our 
Loyalist battalion fought, enveloped 
by enemy light infantry; and we saw 
the flax-stuffed intrenchments carried 
by storm and the Loyalists rushing 
out of it, floundering across the river 
and toiling up our hill to seek pro- 
tection behind our log breastworks. 

Through the choking smoke I saw 
dense masses of enemy infantry climb- 
ing our hill and other battalions cross- 
ing the bridge. 

“Get onto your horse,”’ said I to 
the baroness, ‘‘and ride over to that 
barked hemlock tree and wait for me.”’ 

Scarcely was she arrived there when 
all about me the roar of musketry re- 
doubled; the entire hill-front was a 
mass of flame and smoke; dragoons 
were falling by dozens; yelling cheer- 
ing infantry of Stark’s command en- 
veloped us. 

Baum, mounted, flourishing his 
twelve-pound broadsword, bellowed

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


Love and the Lieutenant 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 2] 


encouragement to his dragoons; but 
they were being shot down by scores 
all around him. 

Suddenly came an_ earth-shaking 
crash and a sheet of fire. The reserve 
ammunition wagon had exploded. 

In the horrible flame-lit powder- 
gloom I saw Colonel Baum trying to 
control his horse. Then a bullet hit 
him. 

In the horrible confusion that fol- 
lowed I forced my horse through to 
the white-scarred hemlock where 
my baroness sat her horse, craning 
her neck like a curious and fearless 
child. 

“Come,” said I quietly. 

She turned her horse obediently, 
trottirg with me to the southern 
brow of the hill. 

From here the spectacle was becom- 
ing tragic. In the smoke and sultry 
rays of a fierce August sun the enemy 
light troops and militia were cheering 
and laughing as they ran after the poor 
ponderous-footed German cavalrymen 
staggering under carbine, sword and 
thick cloth uniforms. 

Stark’s riflemen had seized our can- 
non. Vermont militia were merrily 
hunting fugitives and camp plunder; 
Continentals herded batches of hated 
Tory neighbors taken in arms; and 
marched sullen files of bewildered 
German dragoons toward the bridge. 


‘A. THERE was an old logging road 
(V leading down across Little White 
Creek where, amid the damp stillness 
of mosses, dead leaves and primeval 
trees, only a deadened echo of battle 
penetrated the twilit silence. 

This soft wet road we followed; and 
I intended to continue on across it and 
down to the Waloomsac, but we heard 
poor Baum’s drums beating a parley 
and knew that the enemy must com- 
mand the Waloomsac. 

So we followed the lumber road 
southwest, until I could see the glim- 
mer of the Hoosic River on our left 
and the muddy Sancoix Road curving 
out between it and us. 

I don’t understand how it could be, 
but all sounds of battle had utterly 
died away in this solitude. 

But now we heard a distant racket 
of horses’ hoofs, wagons jolting, and 
men on the march along the Sancoix 
Road; and when we came out of the 
deeper woods the head of Breymann’s 
column appeared, drums beating a 
ponderous German march. 

And there rode Breymann himself 
and the impossible Skene with him. 

I remounted my nag at once and, 
followed by my piquant young baron 
ess, rode forward. 

“Herr Colonel,’’ said I, saluting 
Breymann who had pulled in his horse 
at sight of me, “I regret to report 
Colonel Baum dead and his troops 
destroyed.” 

“What!"’ cried Skene. ‘‘You sav 
Baum is dead and his dragoons ruined! 
Sir, give me leave to inform you that 
such a misfortune is absolutely im- 
possible. Why, sir, here is a Riedesel 
dragoon with favorable dispatches, 
all fresh arrived from Colonel Baum, 
who assures us that he is holding his 
own at Bennington, and master of the 
situation!” 

‘Colonel Baum,”’ I retorted stiffly, 
“is mortally wounded or dead; and 
his troops are dead or fleeing or pris- 
oners to the enemy.”’ And to Lieuten- 


ant Breymann, whose mouth sagged 
like a dying fish: ‘*If you continue the 
movement, you will merely verify at 
a bloody cost what Iam saying to you. 
And I have the honor to assure you, 
sir, that it would be the part of wisdom 
either to halt here and fortify Sancoix 
Mill and await reinforcement, or to 
fall back within supporting distance 
of the main army on the Batten Kill.” 

Breymann’s face became congested 
with anger and he glared at me: ‘“‘I 
have not heard a shot fired, so far!"’ 
he shouted in a ridiculous passion. 
“If there were a battle going on a 
few miles ahead I'd hear it. Sir, you 
are laboring under a silly mistake—"’ 

“Sir,” I interrupted in a cold 
passion, “‘your language is insolent 
and unwarranted!”’ 

“Place that officer under arrest!"’ 
he shouted in a German fury; but 
Skene suddenly pulled at his sleeve and 
pointed to the right; and we all turned 
our heads. Scores and scores of silent 
men dressed like farmers in their shirt 
sleeves, and all wearing white cock- 
ades, were moving swiftly out of the 
woods along the river and forming a 
long loose line behind a rail fence. 

“Look out!’’ I cried sharply. 
“Those fellows are rebel militia!” 

“Those men are our own Loyal- 
ists,"’ bawled Skene gleefully, ‘‘and 
they wear the very badge I have 
recommended!’’ He clapped spurs to 
his horse and galloped out across the 
clearing toward them, waving his 
laced hat like the fatuous fool he was. 

Instantly a thin sheet of flame fringed 
the rail fence and a white cloud edged 
it. Skene and his horse went down 
with a crash, but Skene scrambled to 
his feet and legged it for our lines. 

Forward!" roared Breymann, lift- 
ing his heavy sword; and the ponder- 
ous sweating German column tramped 
doggedly forward. The light troops, 
flanking, advanced firing by platoons; 
the artillery, galloping ahead to un- 
limber, fired, limbered up, galloped on 
—a brave dauntless stupid soldiery 
that courted destruction at every step. 
Their drums, cymbals and bugle-horns 
struck up, accompanied by the sin- 
ister cracking of rebel rifles; the 
grenadiers burst into heavy German 
song. 


(NA THE SETTING sun glimmered red 
VY on their flashing metal caps, bur- 
nished buckles and breastplates; tall 
grenadiers began to fall down in the 
muddy road; wounded and screaming 
horses reared, kicked or fell over dead, 
spreading confusion in the column. 
And above the horrid tumult sounded 
the high-pitched drums and the high 
nasal voices of German officers as the 
column was halted again and again 
to dress the ranks in the face of that mer- 
ciless storm of lead! And all the while 
that sturdy song of agony rolled forth 
from the ordered steady ranks of brave 
men about to die. 

In the gathering twilight Brey- 
mann, wounded, and Skene riding an 
artillery horse—his third—the traces 
of which he himself had cut—and all 
that was left of the grenadiers, came 
fighting and firing and singing on with 
stubborn courage. 

Let none tell me that Germans are 
cowards. I saw them march; I saw 
them die. 

Skene was an ass. I salute him, for 
he knew no fear. Or, if he did, he 


showed none, and led his men for- 
ward to the bitter end. 

I have no love for Breymann. For 
his swinish behavior to me before all 
his troops I had meant to call him to 
account and fight him if it cost me my 
commission. 

But I salute him also. I never saw a 
braver man under fire. Wounded, he 
kept his saddle and his coolness, and 
when the tide turned and Warner's 
green-coated Continentals enveloped 
his flanks, he would not abandon a 
single one of his grenadiers, but fought 
like a devil to defend every man. 

He was a real soldier—maybe a 
stupid one—but a stout one too. 

He said to me: ‘Herr Lieutenant, 
please excuse me for impoliteness’’— 
and the rebel bullets shrilling around 
us thick as whistling sleet; and Brey- 
mann’s wounded leg filling his boot 
full of blood. 

‘‘Herr Colonel,’’ said I, ‘‘at com- 
mand; and happy to serve so gallant a 
German.” 

‘‘A thousand thanks, sir,’’ said he 
quietly, ‘‘—and the Herr Lieutenant 
would oblige me by carrying the light 
infantry forward to continue the flank 
movement through the woods.” 


I LOOKED around desperately 
(V for my lady-love; she was riding 
with the wagons in the rear, as I had 
bidden her, and, looking back, I saw 
her standing up in her stirrups to 
watch me out of sight. 

Then Warner's green and white uni- 
forms suddenly enveloped us; and we 
tore our way out of those accursed 
woods. 

But they drove us back toward the 
grenadiers; and these, in turn, sagged 
and gave way slowly, in good order. 

The column was done for; the day 
ending—a bitter day for the British 
army. 

Back we marched along the dark 
and muddy road, now lighted to an 
infernal blaze by the flame of mus- 
ketry. 

As I rode I kept one hand on my 
lady-love's arm. 

Near San Coix Mill I saw Fraser 
ride up in the torchlight, just escaped 
from Baum’s disaster; and he told me 
that only thirty dragoons of the Regi- 
ment Riedesel had survived that aw- 
ful panic and slaughter called by the 
enemy the Battle of Bennington. 

‘*For God's sake, Lieutenant,’’ said 
he, ‘try to reach General Burgoyne 
tonight and tell him what has hap- 
pened to poor Baum, and what is hap- 
pening to Breymann and Skene!" 

I nodded; and presently I said to my 
lady-love: “*Can you ride all nighte”’ 

“You will noc let me go on to Al- 
banye” 

“If T did your scalp would be 
hooped, stretched and drying at an 
Indian fire within an hour.” 

After a silence she said cheerily: 
“In that case, Herr Lieutenant, | pre- 
fer to die of an all-night ride with 
you!” 

Together, her narrow hand clasped 
close in mine, I guided our horses 
through darkness toward the distant 
Batten Kill. 

And so we rode blindly on—carriers 
of as bad news as ever had been sent to 
astonish and trouble any general who 
ever led a cavalry charge or dandled a 
light-hearted lady upon his knee.

The tweedy ones invariably go in for grape and plum jam 


By GAYNOR MADDOX 
ILLUSTRATOR: ROBERT GC. GELLERT 


poe your men at breakfast. Jam eaters, 
you will find, have the most loving hearts. 
It’s just nature’s way. Coffee and cereal don’t 
seem enough. . It takes fruit suspended in its own 
loveliness to bring out the best side of the male. 

The tweedy ones who indulge in dogs and pipes 
invariably go in for something like Concord grape 
and plum jam. And stacks of toast agleam with 
butter. They are an agreeable breed and well 
worth the effort. 

Merely slip off the skins from about 114 pounds 
of fully rips grapes. Simmer this pulp in covered 
saucepan for 5 minutes. Put through a sieve to 
free from seeds for grape seeds have a way of re- 
tarding the heart’s expansion. Save the skins. 
Chop or grind them and add to pulp. Capture also 
14 pounds of fat ripe plums. You don’t have to 
peel them. Merely cut into pieces and crush 
relentlessly. Treat them to 4 cup of cold water. 
Bring to boil. Stir with faithfulness. Cover and 
simmer for 15 minutes. 

Then combine grapes and plums. Sigh with 
anticipation as you 
measure out 7 cups of 
sugar into the bottom 
of a man-size kettle. 
Now for the fruit’s 
seduction. Measure it 
into the sugar adding 
enough water to fill 
out the demand for 4 
full cups. Mingle with 
a wooden spoon. 
Bring to a full rolling 
nautical boil over the 
hottest fire. Be vigi- 
lant with your stirring 
both before and during 
boiling. Boil exactly 1 
minute. Swing off the 
stove and with the 
same light-heartedness 
stir in ¥% bottle of 
fruit pectin. Skim, 
pour quickly into 
glasses. Seal in its magic at once with paraffin. 
And get yourself a new breakfast robe. 


Pate you a cravat-conscious husband? Men 
who select their ties with care often fall into 
the strawberry-sun-preserve breakfast group. You 
mustn't call them fussy. No, they are merely 
quick to appreciate the nuances of life. In other 
words, they like their strawberries preserved 
whole. So select perfect berries, wash them with 


Keep him rational with blue- 


berry and rhubarb jelly 


care. Weigh them and weigh 
out an equal amount of sugar. 
Moisten the sugar with very 
little water and boil until clear 
and sirupy. Then with an eye 
to the future and with loving 
care, drop in your strawberries 
one by one. Bring sirup again 
to a boil and continue for 3 
minutes. 

Into a large shallow heat- 
resisting glass dish slip the 
berries and their clinging sirup, 
spreading over the top a fine wire netting. Insects 
never make the heart grow fonder. Place these 
preserves hard in the sun. As twilight falls carry 
them indoors. On the morrow again let them 
flirt all day with golden sunshine. Keep up this 
indoors and out business until the sirup is as thick 
with gold light as you want it. Then tuck them 
away in gleaming pint jars. 
Fresh graham toast is their true 
mate. Remember too that cravat- 
conscious husbands don’t like 
crusts on their toast. 

Young men who whisper about 
a vine-clad cottage in some quaint 
old village can be kept rational 
with blueberry and rhubarb jelly- 
True, they usually dote on dry 
cereals, but this sweet confusion 
of blueberries and rhubarb will 
steady their souls and add con- 
tent. If you married a lad from 
this set, send him out to the hills 
for 114 pounds of bursting blue- 
berries. While he is gone, tele- 
phone the grocer for 114 pounds 
of his freshest rhwbarb. Clean 
the fruits and put through 
grinder. Combine their crushed 
consistencies, place in jelly bag and squeeze 
out all the juice. Measure 4 cups of it. Pour 
into large old-fashioned kettle with 714 
cups of sugar. Snap to a boil over furious 
fire and then like a flash add 1 bottle of 
fruit pectin. Stir with devotion. Bring to 
turbulent boil and hold it for 1% minute. 
Swing off the stove, skim and pour into 
glasses. Cover with paraffin while hot. 
Better look up Grandmother's recipe for 
soda biscuit. Young husbands are so often 
sentimental. 


GOOSEBERRY and 

strawberry jam should be 
used for fathers of very young 
children. Because of the 
wealth of flavorous seeds the 
youngsters can’t have it. That 
makes it seem more like 
Father’s personal property, 
just like his shaving soap and 
big white handkerchiefs. 
Tones up his self-esteem. Certainly 
worth about a pound of ripe goose- 
berries and a quart of selected straw- 
berries. 

Crush mercilessly and combine. 
You need 4 cups of the fruit. If a 
little shy of it, add enough water to 
make this measurement. Scoop 7 
cups of sugar into the largest fam- 
ily kettle. Splash in the fruit. 


Mark gooseberry and strawberry 
jam “For Father Only” 


Fresh coffee, English muffins 


and tangerine marmalade 


Sweet Contusion 


Jams and preserves 
that men like 


Wield the mixing 
spoon and place kettle 
over hottest part of 
stove. Bring to stern 
boil. Go right on stir- 
ring. Boil in this vig- 
orous fashion for 1 full 
minute. Take from 
stove and stir in 4% 
bottle of fruit pectin. 
Skim tidily. Pour in- 
to 10 6-ounce glasses 
and cover atonce with 
paraffin. We suggest you paste little white strips 
on them marked boldly *‘ For Father Only.”’ 


Cracvat-conscious men. 


like strawberries pre- 
served whole 


peered you happen to marry an English type? 
The more difficult specimens demand black 
currant tea whenever they catch cold. Put a 
tablespoon of black currant jam in a tall glass and 
fill with boiling water. Hand it 
to him, leave the room quietly 
and be patient. However, if he 
merely likes English tailoring 
you have nothing to worry 
about. Tangerine marmalade or 
gooseberry jelly will make every- 
thing quite all right. Cover 12 
tangerines with warm, not hot, 
water. In 30 minutes remove 
and wipe kindly. Pour boiling 
water on 2 pungent fresh limes, 
stand same length of time and 
wipe. With a sharp knife slit 
the peel of the tangerines in four 
ways. Strip off. Cut into bur- 
nished gold shreds with scissors. 
Treat the limes as well. Boil these 
shreds until tender. Put the tan- 
gerine and lime pulp in a porce- 
lain saucepan with a little water. 
Cook just long enough to make a pulpy mass. 
Force through coarse wite strainer just to remove 
any little oddities. Next combine peel and pulp. 
Weigh mixture and weigh out an equal amount 
of sugar. Mix and start boiling. When this very 
English delicacy gets to the sirup-that-jells state, 
your stint of loving labor is over. Cover with 
greased paper and allow to cool slowly. Then seal. 


po Same antl yourself that the British sun 
never sets on gooseberry jam or jelly, crush 
about 3 pounds of fully ripe 
gooseberries. Put in porcelain 
saucepan with 1% cup of water, 
bring to boil, stirring constantly. 
Cover and simmer 10 minutes. 
Turn into jelly bag and squeeze 
out the juice. Then plunge 4% 
cups of the juice into a large 
kettle after 7 cups of sugar. 
Mix well even though the Brit- 
ish don’t. Over a roaring flame 
bring at once to a boil, add 4% 
bottle of fruit pectin and keep 
stirring. Then bring to a raging 
boil and hold it there for ex- 
actly % minute. Shunt from 
stove to the kitchen table, skim 
and pour at once. Paraffin while 
hot. Tea? No, no—clear fresh 
coffee, English muffins oozing 
with butter and the morning 


paper. 


Blackberry Jam Cake 


Recipe of the Month 


| Gg aacmrces is famous for its blackberry dishes, made 
from the berries which grow profusely in the mountain 
clearings and along the old roads of this historic state. 
Mrs. Nichols suggests an old-time blackberry jam cake, 
with a delicious spicy flavor, as a typical Kentucky recipe: 


% cup shortening 
1 cup brown sugar 
3 €8gss 


¥% teaspoon cloves 

1 teaspoon cinnamon 

1 teaspoon nutmeg 

134 cups flour 1 cup blackberry jam 

1 teaspoon soda 3, tablespoons sour milk 
¥% cup raisins, chopped 


Cream the shortening, add brown sugar gradually and 
beat until light and fluffy. Add beaten egg yolks. Sift flour, 
soda and spices together and add this mixture alternately 
with the jam and sour milk, which have been stirred to- 
gether. Add the raisins, floured. Fold in the beaten egg 
whites. Bake in two layers in a moderately hot oven, 375 
degrees F. 

For filling and frosting mix together 114 cups powdered 
sugar, 3 tablespoons cream and 6 tablespoons butter. Allow 
mixture to stand over hot water for 15 minutes. Add 1 
teaspoon vanilla just before spreading on cake.

Tooth decay 


reduced 5/% 


with fresh Orange ) 


Gum troubles decreased 83% —children’s rate 
of growth greatly improved—as scientists 
make “the most comprehensive study of 


child nutrition on record” 


AT MOOSEHEART, famous 
City of Childhood, 1400 chil- 
dren live complete and happy 
lives of play and study. Their 
diet and dental care are be- 
yond the usual. 


From these were chosen 
341 typical American boys 
and girls aged 10 to 17 for 
the 34-year study of child 
nutrition reported in the 
monograph and Free Booklet below. 


bi. 


The first year they received their 
usual diet. By all previous standards it 
was excellent: milk, butter, eggs, vege- 
tables, fruits, meats, cereals and sundries. 
But like other American diets étdid not pre- 
vent frequent gum troubles and new cavities. 


The second year two 8-oz. glasses 
of fresh Sunkist Orange juice with the 
juice of half a lemon in each were added 
to the daily diet. 


Less than half as many children had 
new cavities this year. Gum troubles les- 
sened 83%. And the rate of growth was 
vastly improved. 


The final eighteen months the 
amount of fresh orange juice was re- 
duced to three ounces daily. This amount 
aided growth, but gum troubles and decay 
returned. 


What this means to you 


Small amounts of fresh orange and 
lemon juice are valuable in the diet be- 
cause they supply the four protective food 
essentials which help to keep the body 
youthfully vigorous—vitamins A, B, C, 
and calcium. 


But much greater protection isafforded 


Hn 


Children who were under- 

weight made extra gains in 

height and weight during 

their first year on fresh orange 
and lemon juice. 


ind Lemon Juice 


AD eo 
341 Mooseheart children were chosen by 
scientists as typical American boys and girls 
—well-cared-for, well-fed. 


by two full-sized glasses a day. 
And since vitamin C, one of 
the essentials, is not stored in 
the body, it is recommended 
that one glass be taken at breakfast and 
the other later in the day.

Serve a slice of each omelet to every guest 


Breakfast Before Bridge 


By ALICE BRADLEY 


MEAL served at any time before 

twelve-thirty noon may be called 
a breakfast. The two established rules 
for a company breakfast that dis- 
tinguish it from luncheon are: first, 
that at breakfast eggs in some form 
are always served, and second, that a 
salad is served only at luncheon. 


Menu 


Chilled orange and pineapple juice or 
fresh strawberries and cream 
Melba toast 
French omelets with varied fillings 
and tomato mushroom sauce 
Corn meal waffles Rhubarb conserve 
Coffee 


French Omelets with Varied Fillings 


8 eggs 1 tablespoon 

¥% cup milk chopped parsley 

I teaspoon salt 1 tablespoon spring 

14 teaspoon pepper onions, chopped 

4 tablespoons fat % cup bread dice 

cup grated cheese 2. tablespoons butter 
¥% pound hors d’oeuvre sausages 


For each omelet beat 2 eggs slightly 
with 2 tablespoons milk, 14 teaspoon 
salt and few grains pepper. Melt 1 
tablespoon fat in omelet pan. When 
hot turn in egg mixture, place over 
medium heat. As it begins to cook on 
bottom and sides, lift with spatula and 
let uncooked portion run underneath, 
first in one place, then in another. 
Sprinkle one omelet with grated 
cheese, the second with chopped pars- 
ley and spring onions, the third with 
bread dice browned in butter and the 
last with cooked hors d’oeuvre sau- 
sages. When thick underneath turn 
heat on full and brown quickly. Turn 
off heat, fold omelet and place on hot 
platter. Pass tomato mushroom sauce 
separately. Serve the omelets in slices, 
giving a portion of the four kinds to 
each person. 


Tomato Mushroom Sauce 


5 tablespoons fat 

1 slice onion ¥% teaspoon salt 

3 tablespoons flour Few grains pepper 
¥% cup mushrooms 


1% cups tomato 


Mztr 3 tablespoons fat, add onion and 
stir and cook until brown. Add flour. 
When slightly brown add tomato, salt 
and pepper and stir until sauce boils. 
Cook 5 minutes. Slice mushrooms, 
pan-fry 3 minutes in 2 tablespoons 
fat, strain sauce over mushrooms. 


Corn Meal Waffles 


24 cup flour I teaspoon sugar 
¥% cup corn meal 2% cup milk 
2 teaspoons baking 2 eggs 
powder 2tablespoonsmelted 
¥% teaspoon salt shortening 


Stet flour with corn meal, baking 
powder, salt and sugar. Add milk, 
egg yolks beaten light and melted 
shortening. Fold in egg whites beaten 
stiff. Heat waffle iron, sprinkle with 
I teaspoon water and when iron stops 
steaming put in one fourth of the mix- 
ture. Cook until waffle stops steaming. 


Rhubarb Conserve 


2 Cups sugar 
2 cups water Few grains salt 
1 pound rhubarb % cup raisins 
4 cup walnut meats 


I Orange 


Cur orange in quarters, remove seeds 
and force through food chopper. Add 2 
cups water and cook 20 to 30 minutes 
or until skin is tender. Wash rhubarb 
and cut into half-inch pieces, add to 
orange and cook until soft. Add sugar, 
salt and raisins. Boil until mixture 
will mound up on spoon. Blanch wal- 
nuts, cut fine and add after conserve 
has cooled 5 minutes. Pour into glasses, 
cover with paraffin, partially seal and 
process in water bath 3 minutes.

HE ‘‘fact’’ this year is your 

tailored suit, whether it is a 

dark wool, a soft silk, a white 
or string-colored linen. Beginning 
with this you turn the inventive 
side of your mind to the accessories 
that change its appearance. Wear 
a cotton blouse one day—gingham, 
piqué, plaid cotton, bandanna, 
embroidered organdie. Try linen 
the next—handkerchief sheer or 
crisp colored butcher’s linen. And 
don’t overlook checked taffeta 
and the new nets, plain or printed. 
Alone or in a set with matching 
hat, belt, bag or gloves there is no 
end to the variety a blouse may 
give your suit, as you see in these 
Paris sketches. 

Then again the hat, gloves and 
buttons may do the furbishing. 
Picture your navy blue suit with 
bamboo buttons instead of navy 
and a burnt straw sailor and dark 
chamois gloves as a change from 
white. This is a Molyneux idea. 

As for your simple one-piece 
dress that too may have its varia- 
tions on a single theme. Robert 
Piguet shows you what he does 
with the same black frock to make 
it look like three different dresses.

Knit It for Fall 


E KNOW that you knitters are 

particular people. Any bit of 
clothing that comes from your needles 
must be smart, whether it is a dress, a 
blouse or a pair of baby’s pants. So we 
went to a young woman whose name 
is a motto in Paris when it comes to 
knitted fashions—and asked her to de- 
sign a special dress for you. 

Here it is—an original Anny Blatt. 
And if you think we weren't in a 
prouder than Punch frame of mind 
when this design slipped out of its 
French envelope, just look at the dress 
and judge for yourself. It is typical of 
the hand-knit styles that have given 
Anny Blatt such a tremendous vogue 
on both sides of the Atlantic. It is 
simple, wearable, yet with a real dis- 
tinction in its turnover button collar 
and its effective diagonal pattern. 


This diagonal stitch which you see 
enlarged is one of Anny Blatt’s favor- 
ites. It is not only easy to do, she says, 
but very effective to wear. Whether 
you are slight or of a more important 
size you will find it becoming. The 
raglan shoulders too, she points out, 
are designed to fit any arm, big or 
little, fat or thin. 

Because the chic of a knitted dress 
depends to a large extent on the wool 
you use, we asked for and received 
Anny Blatt’s approval of the yarn we 
recommend in the knitting directions. 
It has a dull rough tweed-like surface, 
just the thing for a dress that will be 
perfect for the street this fall. As for 
color you may have your choice of a 
number of shades all appropriate for 
fall. Soft green, seal brown, navy or 
Spanish tile would all be attractive.

How to Dress 
Your Husband 


By STUART ROSE 


IHAT most husbands would be the better for a 

little guidance in choosing their clothes is well 

known to all observant women. That thousands 

of husbands receive such guidance from their 
wives—and with unfortunate results—is equally true if 
not quite so well known. Those wives who bother 
about it at all seem to insist upon employing women’s 
standards in selecting men’s clothes which, considering 
that the wives are women, is natural enough, even 
though unhappy in its practical workings. Dressing 
your husband does not consist of making certain that his 
socks match his tie which in turn matches his handker- 
chief. The problem presents greater complexities than 
these; and therefore is one to be approached cautiously 
and with prayer. 

The first trick, and one of the most difficult, is to 
catch your husband. At the outset his acquiescence may 
be so tentative as to suggest a balky yearling being 
dragged to the branding; but without it, I insist, you 
are nowhere. Husbands of course vary, so that your 
technique must be adapted to the peculiar requirements 
of the individual involved. If for example your par- 
ticular husband is a man of decided opinions, it would be 
fatal to approach him with: ‘‘Adelbert, you need a new 
suit and I'm going along to pick it out for you. The one 
you're wearing now makes you look like a race track 
tout.’” This method is known as the frontal attack and 
is not to be recommended for the average man. Even 
though your motives are most praiseworthy, even 
though Adelbert may recognize the sweet reasonable- 
ness of what you have said, you are foredoomed to 
failure. Are we mice or are we men? 

It will be seen, then, that the employment of tactics, 
minor and major, will be necessary if you are to bring 
your grand strategy to the desired conclusion. While I 
am not in a position to know the requirements of each 
individual husband, I suggest that in many average 
cases the necktie opening has been used with a certain 
measure of success. 


[ YOU can persuade your husband to wear one good 
necktie, a fine rich necktie, it is but a step to have him 
acquire another and another and another. Once he has 
built up a modest collection of these handsome cravats 
he will begin to take pride in them. Then you have him! 
It is but a second logical step to convince him that he 
ought (a) to dress up to his neckties and (b) that as you 
have been so successful in selecting these neckties for 
him, your advice in making further choices will be 
equally valuable. 

Now comes the hard part. Having mastered the tech- 
nique of the approach, it becomes essential for you to 
learn something about the articles of apparel under con- 
sideration, for the only neckties worse than those 
selected by the average man are those bought for him 
and forced upon him by the average wife. Every comic 
artist knows that the Christmas necktie gag is good for 
one drawing a year; and every husband, seeing such a 
drawing, shudders in dread anticipation. 

Setting out in quest of a necktie, it is useful to know 
that only the expensive ones are cheap and, conversely, 
that all cheap neckties are expensive. It is possible 
nowadays to buy a cheap motor car and find it excellent; 
cheap houses may upon occasion turn out all right; and 
some of the less expensive varieties of men’s clothes are 
of a surprising excellence. But there has never been 
found under the sun a cheap necktie that was worth 
carrying out of the shop, let alone paying real money for. 

Once this axiom is fixed in your mind, try to recall the 
colors of your husband’s suits. Most men in this coun- 
try run to grays or blues, which simplifies your problem. 
If your husband goes in for browns your task is still not 
difficult, but if his taste has carried him into greens or 
heathers, you'll have to walk carefully lest you stumble. 
Save in summer, when foulards are pleasant, it is safest 
to stick to the heavier silks such as the English spital- 
fields. These tie up better and last longer than light ma- 
terials and will stand an extraordinary amount of abuse. 


Small repeated patterns or any 
one of the many varieties of 
checks—not plaids!—are apt to 
be the best choice for the inex- 
perienced buyer. They are al- 
ways in good taste and may be 
worn anywhere. Stripes are 
risky, although occasionally 
good. If you must buy them, 
look out that you don’t select a 
pattern that will turn your hus- 
band into a spurious Old Etonian 
or a member of the Royal Gar- 
rison Artillery. On the whole, 
it is the way of discretion to 
leave them alone. Never buy one 


of the large splashy patterns so dear to the hearts of 
misguided maiden aunts and never under any circum- 
stances select a necktie of watered silk of the variety 
known to women, I believe, as moire. 

Within these limits, then, you may make your choice 
and you will find it surprisingly wide. Any combina- 
tion of black and white, blue and white, black or blue 
and gray, or gray and white, will do for black, blue or 
gray suits. Ties of wool, similar as to pattern and color, 
go well with the shaggier tweeds and with country 
clothes in general. With brown clothes, red or brown 
and white, red and blue, or brown and blue, or any of 
these general combinations will do nicely. Green is 
dangerous and should be avoided. Solid colors in heavy 
grained silks are usually good, but with these keep away 
from self-figures or jacquard patterns. 

Now we arrive at the second phase; persuading the in- 
nocent victim of your wiles to dress up to his neckties. 
You have found a chink in the husbandly armor, but be- 
fore applying the can-opener be certain that you com- 
mand a basic knowledge sufficiently sound to carry you 
through the operation. 


"LL ASSUME that you entertain no burning desire to 

make your husband over into a bad approximation ofa 
movie star, of a belted earl or of a character out of Wode- 
house. If you do, you’ve come to the wrong shop and 
may turn the page in search of more sprightly matter. 
Anyone who spends five thousand a year on his clothes 
can make some sort of impression with them, but we are 
here concerned with the man who spends five hundred 
dollars or less. 

The first thing to get out of your mind is the idea that 
there are ‘‘styles’’ for men in the sense that the word 
style is generally understood by women. Fashions there 
are, but these change so gradually that a suit or overcoat 
purchased this year, provided the selection is originally 
sound, will be perfectly good three or four years from 
now so far as cut and pattern are concerned. (Of course 
any suit may wear out, but I can’t help that.) 

The best clothes for men still originate in London and 
not on Broadway, but this does not mean that your hus- 
band is obliged to patronize a Bond Street or Savile Row 
tailor in order to be well dressed. The large American 
manufacturers of ready-to-wear clothes can afford to em- 
ploy the most expert designers in the world, and do. 


59 


In many cases the necktie opening has 
heen used ewith a measure of success 


American woolens, I am told, are the equals both in ap- 
pearance and wearing qualities of any in the world, and 
a great deal less expensive than the imported variety. 
Your main problem is to avoid either the obvious or the 
eccentric, remembering always that tricky design is 
usually employed to distract the buyer’s attention from 
careless workmanship or shoddy materials. 

In selecting clothes for business wear, you’d best ad- 
vise your husband to stick to solid colors, quiet strip- 
ings or to weaves that do not assault the naked eye at 
twenty paces. The harder materials ordinarily give 
more wear and hold their shape better than the rougher 
tweeds, homespuns and shetlands, though many men 
wear the latter in town or country with a fine impar- 
tiality. Real country clothes, because of their greater 
variety in pattern and color, are fun; but these more 
exuberant materials when worn in town become merely 
funny. 


MAN does not need an extensive wardrobe in order 

to make a good appearance on most ordinary occa- 
sions but it is cheaper in the long run to have several 
suits at a time. If a suit is worn for one day and hung 
carefully in the closet for the following two, it will 
not require pressing very often. 

Having settled on a material, we come to cut. There 
are two types of lounge or business suit, single and 
double-breasted. Double-breasted suits are ordinarily 
considered to be slightly dressier than the single- 
breasted variety, but this is largely a matter of opinion 
and personal preference. The main point to bear in mind 
is that single-breasted suits have a tendency to accentu- 
ate slimness. If your husband is short or inclined to 
stoutness, this is the suit for him. Striped patterns also 
give the illusion of increased height. Although coats 
are slightly fitted at the waist and over the hips, this 
effect is gained by tailoring. No suit should be tight. 
Shoulders are padded a trifle, but the extreme peaked 
effect should be avoided like the plague, else your hus- 
band is apt to resemble an adagio dancer. Single- 
breasted coats have either two or three buttons, only the 
middle one of which is used. Double-breasted coats 
have six buttons, the upper two are spaced slightly 
farther apart than the other four. Again, only the 
middle button is used in fastening the coat. 

Overcoats offer a slightly wider latitude in design, but 
there are at least two general utility coats which may be 
worn either in town or country and, if necessary, over 
evening clothes. These are the guards coat, usually 
made up in dark blue, double-breasted, with a half-belt 
in the back; and the town ulster, also double-breasted 
and made up in dark blue or oxford gray, slightly 
longer than the guards coat and with a straight back. 
Either of these coats, inmoderately rough materials, may 
be worn anywhere and at any time. They solve the prob- 
lem of the man who gets along with only one overcoat.

MORE NEARLY 
PERFECT 


V VEN mother nature was 


designing our food for us, she didn’t know that 
we'd ever live in smoky, dusty cities and towns, 
work in offices or factories, and wear clothes all 
the year ’round. So she didn’t feel the need to put 
in our food—not even in milk, our most nearly 
perfect food—an adequate supply of the very im- 
portant vitamin D—the sunshine vitamin which 
the ultra-violet rays from the sun created in our 
bodies when we lived out in the clear sunshine. 


We haven't had enough of this precious 
vitamin D, since smoke and dust and buildings 
and clothes have kept these ultra-violet rays from 
reaching us. Without it the disease called rickets 
(faulty bone development) has become widely 
prevalent among children. Physicians have dis- 
covered that it often exists even where it is not 
suspected, and results in faulty bones and bad 
teeth in later life. ; 


We can now prevent it. The ultra-violet 
rays have been brought to earth again—not to 
create the vitamin by shining on our bodies, but 
by shining on our most important food—milk. 
In the Pet Milk plants the ultra-violet rays are 
created, allowed to shine on the milk, and thus to 
create the precious vitamin in the milk, just as 
the ultra-violet rays of the sun would create it in 
our bodies if we still lived out in the clear sun- 
shine. What we don’t get from the sunshine we 


can now get from our food. 


All Pet Milk is now enriched with 
vitamin D by irradiation with these ultra- 
violet rays. Uniformly rich in the calcium and 
phosphorus of which bones and teeth are built, 
Pet Milk now enables your baby and your 
children to use these bone and teeth- building 
materials more efficiently—to build sounder bones

Four-Hour Pattern 


OUR midsummer search for an 
extra dress or two to carry you 
through the hot weather can very 
well end here. From pattern No. 5555 
you can make two youthfully becom- 
ing dresses—one for sports, the other 
for dancing—in less time than it takes 
to fly from Philadelphia to Chicago. 
Approximately four hours are all 
you need to make either version of 
this pattern and this in- 
cludes the cutting, the hand- 
finishing and the pressing. 
The dance version may take 
a little longer because of the 
hand-rolled hem on the col- 
lar frill, but if you like our 
idea of using ribbon for the 
sash and bows your sewing 
time will be cut down con- 
siderably. 
One of the features that 
make this dress so simple is 


the attractive tie effect on the shoulder 
which takes the place of a sleeve. An- 
other is the detailed dressmaking 
lesson which comes to you along with 
your pattern. Armed with this even 
a beginner can cope with No. 5555 
successfully. 

For the sports version we suggest a 
soft yellow linen trimmed with white 
piqué or navy piqué with white. For 
the evening dress light blue 
dotted swiss would be lovely 
trimmed with navy taffeta 
or plaid mousseline with 
satin ribbon to match your 
favorite color in the plaid. 


Pattern No. 5555. Sizes, 14 to

35-inch material for the short 
skirt style and 5\/4 yards 35- 
inch material for the evening 
version. The price, 25 cents.

[CONTINUED FROM PAGE I r] 


to raise Bill up the ladder—up and up 
to the top. And that was how it came 
about that, defying some of the rules 
of the well-known book of etiquette, 
she buckled on her armor and arranged 
her forces for what would be magnifi- 
cent victory or ignominious defeat. 

For Bill. Some day they would take 
him off on fishing trips and golf 
tournaments, jaunts to French Lick, 
big league games in Chicago. 

Perhaps Bill thought that these 
other men were really of better clay, 
because they belonged socially. The 
note of wistfulness in his voice was a 
knife that found her heart with true 
aim. She wanted the best for him. 
Nothing had ever really mattered but 
Bill—and little Bill of course. 

That last morning had decided it. 
She would break the ice. She would 
give a party, a very grand and proper 
party. Somehow she had to send Bill 
rocketing toward that dream. 


bang CAUGHT a glimpse of her- 
self now in the mirror beyond. 
That lovely dress. Sheer and billow- 
ing and lacy. She brushed away a 
silly tear and the envelopes in her 
hand touched her cheek. She glanced 
at them thoughtfully. 

One, on monogrammed paper, read: 


Mrs. Eliot Spencer 
accepts with pleasure 
Mrs. William Jeremiah Walton’s 
kind invitation to luncheon 
on August twelfth at one o'clock. 


Of course she had answered. You 
knew she would always do the right 
thing. The social arbiter of Cotting- 
ham. The richest citizen by far; she 
owned the big porcelain factory where 
Bill was employed. 

The next envelope held paper of a 
poisonous green which said: 


Mrs. Katherine Seabury 
accepts with pleasure. 


Kitty Seabury. Nobody ever called 
her Katherine. Even the clerks in the 
shops, Tonia’s maid, the boy in the 
paper store, all referred to her as Kitty. 
They spoke of her in that light fash- 
ion, even though she went everywhere 
with Joseph Falconer, one of the 
wealthiest men in town. She had an 
abundance of red hair and dark, dark 
eyes that seemed too weary ever to 
meet one’s squarely. And her clothes 
were sumptuous miracles of taste. 
Tonia regarded the cheap blue enve- 
lope in her hand. The third and last. 
She drew out the sheet on which had 
been written in a staggering hand: 


Dear Mrs. Walton, 
Thanks for inviting me. 
I will come to your party. 
Very truly yours, 
Mrs. Jor Miter. 


Well—maybe Mrs. Miller didn’t 
live in a nice house and have lots of 
money and run out to the country 
club every day, but she was an aw- 
fully nice person. Wouldn't little Bill 
have been run over and killed but for 
Mrs. Miller’s oldest boy, John? Would 
Tonia ever forget that horrible mo- 
ment when she had seen her little son, 
and Bill’s, toddling across the street 
in the path of a truck? 

The maid came in now from the 
kitchen. 

“‘Everything’s ready, Mis’ Wal- 
ton,’’ Thelma said. 

Tonia smiled happily. Then the 
bell rang and Thelma hurried to the 


door. Tonia could hear her direct the 
guest toa room above. Tonia waited, 
rehearsed her greeting, her smile. 
And then in a moment she was facing 
the glamorous person beyond. 

“What a perfectly heavenly day 
you have for your party!"’ Kitty Sea- 
bury came in with outstretched hand. 

““How do you do!’’ Tonia greeted 
her shakily. “‘I’m so glad you could 
come. 

‘And I’m first?’’ demanded the 
guest with a puzzled smile. 

“They'll all be here any moment 
now,” Tonia assured her. 

“What a charming house! You're 
expecting quite a crowd, aren’t you?”’ 

“Forty,’’ Tonia told her promptly. 

““Aren’t you brave!’’ sighed the 
other. ‘That's a real undertaking.”’ 

“Oh, but your parties are much 
bigger, I know!”’ Tonia said. ** Bill 
says that those duplex apartments 
where you live are immense.” 

Kitty Seabury colored uncomfort- 
ably. ‘‘Tell me, is that a picture of 
your baby?”’ 

Tonia placed the glass-framed photo- 
graph of little Bill in her hands. ‘‘ He’s 
the cutest thing you ever saw,”’ she 
began. “‘Only—of course I had to 
send him out with the nurse-girl to- 
day. Company frightens him.” 

Again the doorbell. From where 
she stood Tonia could see Mrs. Eliot 
Spencer’s smart coupé parked at the 
edge of the drive. Pride flowed 
through her. Bill darling! I’m going 
to help you. I’m going to give you the 
friends you want. 

“Good afternoon, Mrs. Spencer! 
It’s so nice to see you.’” The book had 
suggested that greeting. Tonia spun 
it off word for word. There was sin- 
cerity in her tone and happiness in her 
smile. No one could have resisted her. 

““Ah, Mrs. Walton!’’ The older 
woman accepted the proffered hand. 
““Tt was sweet of you to think of me.”’ 

Not a detail of the elegantly simple 
costume escaped Tonia’s guarded 
glance. Chalk-white crépe, a small 
striking hat against her snowy hair. 
She made a picture of graciousness. 

As her glance wandered beyond 
Tonia her smile remained warm and 
pleasant, but Tonia saw her stiffen. 

“This is Mrs. Seabury, Mrs. Spen- 
cer,” Tonia said through the sharp 
agitation of the moment. 


THE CLOCK struck one-thirty. 
bs A dart of fear flew through 
Tonia’s heart. 

Again the bell. And in a moment 
red-cheeked perspiring stout little 
Mrs. Miller puffed into the living- 
room, fanning herself with a cardboard 
advertisement from a confectioner’s. 

“I'm awful sorry, Mis’ Walton, if 
I've held up your party,’’ she began 
breathlessly. ‘‘But I couldn’t get 
started a minute sooner. Those young 
ones of mine—oh,’’ she paused as 
Tonia offered her hand and turned to- 
ward the other guests, murmuring in- 
troductions. ‘‘Oh, pleased to meet 
you, Mis’ Spencer—and you too, Mis’ 
Seabury. I’ve heard a lot about you 
both. My, ain’t it hot?” 

‘‘How do you like Cottingham?”’ 
Mrs. Spencer asked her hostess easily. 

“IT am crazy about it,’’ Tonia con- 
fessed. ‘‘It’s such a beautiful place— 
and clean and quiet—and Bill likes it 
too.”’ Her nervous glance went fur- 
tively to the clock. Her smile was 
forced. Twenty minutes to two.

[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 66] 


“No place like it,’’ put in Mrs. 
Miller. ‘‘I was born and raised here, 
met my man and had my children 
here, and I suppose I'll die here too.” 
She was smiling happily. 

“Have you many children?’’ asked 
Mrs. Spencer with a friendly smile. 

“*Seven,’’ returned the other. ‘‘Four 
girls and three boys.”’ 

Tonia heard them vaguely. Pres- 
ently the clock said ten minutes to 
two. She couldn't wait much longer. 
It came to her then, as if a door had 
been opened abruptly in her mind, 
that there was no need of waiting. 
There were no others coming. The 
thirty-seven who had failed to answer 
had done so intentionally. 

Mrs. Miller was saying, ‘My, 
ain't you havin’ a crowd, though! 
All them tables!"’ 

The clock struck two. 


{AK TONIA got to her feet uncer- 

tainly. She was smiling. She 
said simply, ‘‘We can’t wait any 
longer for the others. Let’s—let’s sit 
at the dining-room table. It’s—room- 
iest.’’ She led the way. 

Mrs. Spencer followed along as 
casually as if the rooms buzzed with 
the expected guests. “‘What a lovely 
view you have of the meadow!”’ she 
remarked pausing for a moment beside 
the window. ‘‘And your garden—my 
dear, it’s too lovely!”’ 

Tonia smiled at her gratefully 
through eves that ached with unshed 
tears. ‘‘ Will you sit there, at the head 
of the table, Mrs. Spencer? And you 
there, Mrs. Seabury. And over here, 
Mrs. Miller. Thelma, take away the 
extra doilies, and everything. We'll 
have our lunch now.” 

Mrs. Spencer’s casual manner te- 
flected nothing but graciousness and 
serenity. Kitty Seabury took her cue 
expertly from the older woman. Mrs. 
Miller looked from one to the other in 
open surprise. It would be difficult 
for her to resume the breezy natural- 
ness that was her charm. 

Jellied bouillon. Nasturtium petals 
scattered on the saucer. 

Tonia thought to herself, ‘‘It isn’t 
so bad as death—or sickness. And 
I'd have to live through them. I 
can live through this. I’ve got to.”’ 

“T’'m so glad that you like our 
view, Mrs. Spencer,’’ she heard her- 
self say. ‘* Bill and I have been sort of 
planning to take away the stockade 
fence at the foot of our land. It spoils 
the sweep of it, don’t you think so?”’ 

She saw a sudden spark in the white- 
haired woman's eyes. Was it admira- 
tion, approval? *‘Of course!’’ agreed 
Mrs. Spencer briskly. ‘‘Fences are so 
limiting, my dear. I don’t think they 
were ever God's idea.”’ 

“There were gates to paradise,”’ 
murmured Kitty Seabury. 

“That’s so,’’ agreed Mrs. Spencer 
slowly. ‘‘But don’t you think they 
were figurative, as they sometimes are 
today?”’ 

“They’re never figurative or real, 
to those inside.’’ And the dark eyes 
met Mrs. Eliot Spencer’s for a brief 
moment. 

Breast of chicken with mushrooms. 
Asparagus Hollandaise. Rolls like 
snowflakes. Miniature tulips of butter. 

‘My, your dishes are beautiful, Mis’ 
Walton!"" Mrs. Miller shook her 
head in awed delight. ‘‘Once in a 
while I buy myself a cup and saucer 
that’s pretty and I plan on keepin’ it 
to have my tea in when I’m alone. 


And then them divils of kids come 
along and smash it or chip it.”’ 

“But you wouldn’t be without 
them, divils or not, would you?"’ Mrs. 
Spencer put to her with a comradely 
laugh. ‘I know how it is, Mrs. Mil- 
ler. When I was a bride, Eliot bought 
me some odd pieces of Haviland china 
at a sale. I adored them. And then 
Thomas came along, and Arthur and 
Juliet and Marie. It used to break my 
heart when I'd see a piece of it chipped 
or cracked—or lying in pieces on 
the floor. And then they grew up and 
went away and I had lots of lovely 
china without chips or cracks—’’ 
Her gaze drifted toward the window. 

Kitty Seabury broke her asparagus 
with a pensive fork. ‘‘I was sixteen 
before I knew that dishes were ever 
made of anything but crockery. They 
were so heavy—’’ she remembered 
with an absent smile. ‘*Once I stum- 
bled when I was getting up on the 
little stool in front of the sink. I can 
still remember the crash and roar of 
those plates as they went tumbling 
down to the floor around me. They're 
just as fussy about cracks and chips in 
orphan asylums, you know.”’ 

““And they made you stand on a 
stool—a bit of a kid like that, handlin’ 
and washin’ dishes?’’ demanded the 
outraged Mrs. Miller. ‘* Well, glory 
be to God, it ain’t right!’’ 

“Oh, you get used to it. Until you 
get out in the world and see that there 
are more beautiful things. And then,”’ 
she shrugged, ‘‘well, maybe you try to 
make up for lost time.”’ 

Avocado on a nest of watercress. 
Paper thin bread rolled with cheese 
and toasted to a delicate brown. 


“MY JULIA loves pretty things,”’ 

sighed Mrs. Miller, ‘‘and divil a 
thing has she got to her name but an 
old kitchen dress or two—workin’ 
and slavin’ away, helpin’ me with the 
others. Yesterday at sunset she said, 
“Look, Mamma! See that little red 


cloud over there? That’s me—all 
dressed up and goin’ to the dance at 
the country club. That blue is the 
sash and the bright silver part is my 
jewelry. Don’t you think I’m kind of 
grand?’ And I thought to myself, 
“Sure, you'll never wear silks and 
satins all your life—if you're like the 
rest of us; you with your brown hair 
like a thrush’s wing and the soft white 
skin of you. And it’s breakin’ your 
heart you'll be, you poor darlin’!’”’ 
She glanced around, her cheeks a bit 
flushed. ‘‘But listen to me goin’ on,” 
she added helplessly. ‘‘I don’t know 
how I got talkin’ like that.”’ 

“She’s a pretty little thing,’’ put 
in Kitty Seabury gently. ‘‘I’ve seen 
her going by on errands. I wouldn’t 
worry about beautiful china if I had 
a daughter like that.’’ There was 
something wistful in her tone, some- 
thing lonely and haunting. 

Bombe glacé. Minute miracles of 
iced cakes. Coffee. Rose petals float- 
ing in crystal finger bowls. 

““We’ve planned little Bill’s life 
years ahead,”’ Tonia put in eagerly. 
““He’s a rowdy, though. We want 
him to be a doctor, but I’m terribly 
afraid he'll turn out to be a prize- 
fighter or—or something.” 

A lull. Tonia remembered the ad- 
vice in the book of etiquette, and rose. 

The rooms beyond still bore mute 
testimony to her humiliation. She 
summoned Thelma.

O COOL you off—to quench your thirst 
—there is nothing so fine as good, old- 
fashioned fresh lemonade. Its tangy flavor 
gives real refreshment. It has none of the 
cloying sweetness of drinks that are cold but 
fail to cool. 
Favorite Way to Make 
“My favorite recipe? Simplicity itself. 
Allow the juice of one lemon for each large 
glass of ade. Add cracked ice and water to 
fill—-and sugar to taste. Slip a slice of lemon 
over the rim, drop in a maraschino cherry 
—and you can’t improve upon it! 


Many Delicious Variations 


“But you can vary it in countless ways. 
Mix orange juice, grape juice, gingerale, tea 
—even the left-over juice of canned fruits 
(lemon juice ‘cuts’ the syrup and makes a 
delicious combination). You can’t go wrong 
as long as you remember this first principle: 


“Ice makes it cold, but lemon. makes it 
cooling !” 


Health Authorities State: 


Lemons are an important “protective” 
food, promoting bodily vigor. They are a good 
source of vitamin B—an excellent source of 
vitamin C. They aid digestion by stimulat- 
ing appetite and help prevent acidosis. By 
their liberal use you tend to arrest forms of 
tooth decay and gum troubles caused by lack 
of protective foods. 


Because they add to the joy of eating and 
the joy of living, be liberal in your use of 
lemons. Those with “Sunkist” on the tissue 
wrapper are California’s juiciest and finest.

““We won't need the extra tables,”’ 
she said steadily. Then with her 
guests she went on to the living-room. 

Kitty Seabury paused beside the 
piano, ran her fingers over the keys. 

“Please play, won’t you?’’ asked 
Tonia. 

Kitty Seabury seated herself, played 
a lullaby, a delicate lilting thing with 
irresistible melody. At Tonia’s urging 
she sang. The three women gave her 
the compliment of rapt attention. 

The telephone rang. ‘‘Mrs. Wal- 
ton? This is Mrs. Horace Mott. My 
dear, I’m perfectly appalled! Your 
card was mislaid with some other 
letters. I didn’t find it until today. 
I'm terribly sorry! You will forgive 
me, won't you? I should have adored 
it. So sweet of you to think of me. 
May I call tomorrow?” 


TONIA heard herself murmur 
words. She knew that Mrs. Eliot 
Spencer's coupé had been seen before 
the Walton 2 she knew that the 
smart set in Cottingham moved to the 
social baton of that impeccable leader. 
That car today was like a sterling mark 
on silver. She was made. And yet— 
“Don’t stop, please!’’ Mrs. Spencer 
was begging Kitty Seabury. 

The telephone again. 

“Mrs. Walton? This is Mrs. Lucien 
Perry. I’ve just returned from a busy 
week in Chicago and I found your 
card. I’m desolate over it. It would 
have-been glorious to come—but—I'm 
going to call very soon. We must get 
together!”’ 

Mrs. Lucien Perry had returned yes- 
terday. Tonia had seen her pass! 

Mrs. Spencer said, as Kitty Seabury 
finished, ‘‘ Will you sing that lullaby 
at the church fair next week, Mrs. 
Seabury? I’m on the committee and 
it’s exactly what I need to balance the 
program.” 

“Why—I—if I can do it well 
enough,”’ came a faltering response. 

“You do it beautifully!’’ The 
praise was warm. Her eyes met the 
dark eyes beyond, and something in 
her gracious smile spelled benediction 
and understanding—and pity. 

“T£ you'll excuse me,’’ Kitty Sea- 
bury said to Tonia, ‘I think I'll run 
along. It’s nearly five.’’ Tonia went 
with her to the door. 

“T loved your music,’’ she said. 

“Thank you for everything.’’ Kitty 
Seabury clasped her hand and went out 
with confidence in her step. 

And then Mrs. Spencer found that 
she too must leave. She bade Mrs. 
Miller a warm farewell and paused at 
the door with Tonia. 

““You’re such a little thing. Why, 
my dear, you’re just a child, aren’t 
you? Marie would have been your 
age if—’’ She broke off rather sharply. 
The admirable poise was shaken for a 
moment. ‘‘I came to your luncheon 
party today because once I wasa lonely 
bride in a strange town, and I remem- 
bered. I shall come again, if you ask 
me, and see your baby and sit with you 
under the trees in your garden. Be- 
cause I like you, my dear. You bring 
back memories. And tell Bill for me, 
I think he’s a very lucky man.”’ 

“I got the bargain,’ Tonia said. 

But Mrs. Spencer only smiled. *‘I 
gather that you're fond of music. 
Won't you and your husband have tea 
with me on Sunday? There’s a cellist 
I’d particularly like you to hear.”’ 
And before Tonia could recover from 
her astonishment, ‘*‘ Would you, too,”’ 


Mrs. Spencer continued, “‘be good 
enough to get little Julia Miller’s size 
for me? I’m going to Chicago next 
week and I might be able to find a 
flame-colored gown with a blue sash.”’ 

The telephone again. 

“Hello, Tonia!”’ 

“Bill!’’ Tears rushed to her eyes, 
crowded her throat. She fought them 
back. ‘‘Darling!’’ came clearly. ‘But 
you weren't due until Saturday!”’ 

‘Oh, got a date for tonight?”’ 

“T have now!” 

“Glade” 

“What do you think? Come home!”’ 

“Half an hour,’’ he promised. 
“Got in on the four-ten, galloped 
right up here to the office. I’ve got 
big news for you, Missus.”’ 

“Do you love me, Bill?”’ 

“It’s been on the front page for 
years!’’ he howled. ‘‘Don’t you ever 
read the papers?’’ He was gone, with 
his haunting precious laughter. 

“T’'ll be runnin’ along now myself,”’ 
Mrs. Miller was saying as she came 
into the hall. 

Tonia a her hand into the strong 
padded clasp. ‘I’m glad you came.” 

“I'm glad too, bless you. Sure it’s 
the first time in all my years I ever 
heard of goin’ to a party and feelin’ as 
if you'd been to a confessional.”’ 

“What do you mean?’ begged 
Tonia, puzzled. 

“‘T mean I feel good in the heart of 
me, darlin’. I'll be wanderin’ along 
now and thinkin’ about you in front 
of my own fire. May God bless you, 
Tonia Walton, and them that sat with 
us at your table today!’ 

Tonia went out to the terraced lawn 
that led to the meadow. 

She was seeing a cluttered kitchen 
with a young wife who was Mrs. 
Eliot Spencer, distracted at the antics 
of her children. Dishes to wash, floors 
to clean, beds to make. Mrs. Eliot 
Spencer! And she was seeing her alone 
today in that palace on the river. 

She was seeing a little sober-eyed 
child standing ona stool before a sink, 
washing heavy plates, longing for a 
mother’s love, and beauty, and whole- 
some play. A child whose soul was 
filled with music. 

She was seeing the mother love that 
shone in a mother’s eyes, thwarted in 
her dream for Julia yet brave and 
philosophical and indomitable. 


THERE was Bill now, racing 
down to greet her, to sweep her 
in his arms. 

‘*Was the trip a success?’’ she asked 
through his embrace. 

‘“Must have been more than I 
thought.’’ Something about his eyes. 
Something about his voice. ‘Tonia, 
Lindley Stevens wants us to drive over 
to Madison with him and his wife on 
Sunday. Buttonholed me the minute I 
struck the office.”’ 

‘Oh, Bill! I’m so happy for you!” 

‘‘For me?’’ came the blank echo. 
“Why, you little fool kid, I’m just a 
big clown; but you—’’ gently, with 
his heart in the words, ‘“‘you could 
knock ’em dead, the whole outfit, if 
you had half a chance. Maybe it 
hasn’t been burning me up that I was 
holding you back from the kind of 
times you could have had with a 
regular educated guy. Say, what are 
you crying about? Aren’t you glad?” 

‘*Let me be proud of you—”’ 

“Aw, Tonia, a big clown like me!”’ 

‘*Let me be proud of you my way.” 
The rest was lost against his heart.

... Besides Greatly Increasing His Appetite for Milk 


Now —A Remarkable Swiss Food Development, 
by Actually Creating the Sensation of Hunger, 
Combats Loss of Appetite in Summer... 
Frequently Adds Weight at the Rate of A Pound

F summertime makes a fussy eater of your child—if he 

tends to lose weight in warm weather—try this new- 
found way to make “‘poor eaters”’ eat! A remarkable, scien- 
tific development that can make him actually relish even 
vegetables and milk! 


This new way does not merely ‘“‘tempt”’ the taste—for that 
alone is too often ineffective. But it actually creates the sen- 
sation of hunger—makes a child want to eat, without coax- 
ing or commanding. 

Almost immediately, you’ll note a change commencing. 
Not only will you find your child more eager to eat (even 
such healthful foods as spinach, carrots and lettuce)—but 
you'll usually find him willing to drink a far greater amount 
of milk each day. 


A remarkable food concentrate (originally developed in 
Switzerland and called Ovaltine) should achieve this result 
for your child. In summer, you simply give it shaken up 
with cold milk and children love its taste. 


Literally Creates Hunger

Second: Ovaltine is not only extremely nourishing in itself,

Third: It is common knowledge that milk turns to solid

not easily digest these milk curds. 


However, when Ovaltine is added to milk, it has the power 
to break up these curds into small granules. Thus increas- 
ing digestibility to a marked degree. 


Milk is also taken much more readily when mixed with 
Ovaltine. And children love its delicious taste. 


These three features of Ovaltine are important. And spe- 
cialists will tell you that the child with poor or ‘‘slow”’ 
digestion is usually the one who won’t eat—who shuns 
vegetables and objects to drinking milk. 


But once appetite is stimulated in a scientific way—and the 
sensation of hunger is produced—a definite change takes 
place. The old objection to every-day foods gradually dis- 
appears. And even a “‘vegetable-hunger”’ is built up natu- 
rally day by day. 


Thus, Ovaltine importantly aids the growing child by pro- 
viding and helping him to secure those nourishment factors 
which play such a vital part in normal development and 
the maintenance of robust health.

dries out the skin—may |

it, make it Jookold... 
Don’t take chances! 
Protect your skinfrom — 
burning ... Restore 
moisture quickly once 
sunburn has started.

Sunburn_ 


restores moisture to sun-dried skin 


Applied before exposure 
...it helps protect your 
skin from burning 


Here’s more good news about your 
favorite lotion! 


You know how soothed your hands 
feel after you’ve put Jergens Lotion 
on them. How tired, drawn skin re- 
laxes, roughness goes away and the 
skin becomes smoother, softer. 

That’s because of the remarkable 

ability this lotion has of yielding 

up its moisture to the skin... Tests 
show that Jergens goes into the 
skin more quickly and more com- 
pletely thanany other lotion tested. 
That explains why it’s so deliciously 
cooling and comforting to sunburned 
skin. Over-exposure to bright sun- 
light dries the skin. The sun’s rays 
draw moisture out from the inner 
skin cells... Painful irritation results. 


When you apply this fra- 
grant lotion, the dried-out skin 
gratefully drinks it up—the 
stinging discomfort of any 


ordinary sunburn is quickly relieved. 


And there’s another remarkable 
thing about the way Jergens is com- 
pounded. Specialists find that a film 
of Jergens Lotion on the skin actu- 
ally excludes the most irritating rays 
of the sun. 


Before and after exposure 


So tuck a bottle into your beach kit 
or the pocket of the car. Before and 
after going out into the sun, use it 
freely on your arms, neck, shoulders 
—any part of your skin directly ex- 
posed. You will love its soothing, 
comforting coolness—its wonderful 
protection! 


For week-ends and traveling, you 
may want to get the handy, smaller 
bottle at the ten-cent store. But for 
regular use, you'll want the 50¢ bottle. 
Or even more economical is the 
large size at $1.00. You can get both 
at drug stores or department stores. 


This Lotion goes into Skin Cells 
Laboratory experiments show that Jergens quickly re- 
leases its moisture to the skin cells, thus effectively 
relieving sunburn discomfort. Send coupon. 


When You Face the Light 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 74] 


grayish-brown eye paste is used with 
discretion and the eyelash cosmetic is 
also brown to harmonize with the 
generally warm sunny effect of the 
make-up ensemble. 

For evening (see illustration lower 
right page 74) a very pearly skin ef- 
fect is created by the make-up foun- 
dation. The powder is a pinkish 
lilac which lights up marvelously 
under electricity. The rouge and lip- 
stick are of a richer deeper tone with 
a slight violet cast—also very success- 
ful under evening lighting. A silvery 
blue eye paste is used and a dark 
blue eyelash cosmetic. If the lady had 
had hands she could have done right 
by them by using a violet fingernail 
polish. 

There is of course a whole theory of 
color and light behind these make-up 
effects; but I am sure that you do not 
want to have your brains frazzled as 
mine have been trying to understand 
what light takes out or puts into your 
skin. Practically speaking all you 
have to do is to experiment with your 
own face under’ natural or artificial 
light to see what happens. A little 
double-faced pocket mirror is now de- 
signed to show you how you really do 
look under these two different light- 
ings and it’s a great revelation and a 
real face-saver sometimes. 

The make-up shown on page 74 is of 
course for the summer complexion, 
which ordinarily takes on a little 
browner and a little rosier tone than at 
other times of year. Sun-kissed is the 
popular term, I believe. The cosmetics 
chosen are all on this warm ruddy 
tone. They harmonize with the skin 
and with each other. In other words, 
they are right. They glow and make 
the skin look vibrant and young. If 
you saw them under ordinary artificial 
light, however, the skin would look 
muddier and the lips darker and duller. 
Which is the effect artificial light has 
on the yellow reds. 


HE most successful evening make- 
up features pinkish tones and reds 
with a suggestion of violet in them. 
Under ordinary electricity some of 


the blue or violet goes out of these 


shades and they look clear and lovely. 
The skin is pearly and the lips rightly 
red. 

More make-up is permissible in the 
evening, not only because the assump- 
tion is that the occasion is more 
decorative and social, but also be- 
cause you have to use a larger quantity 
of make-up under artificial light to get 
your effect. Many women use eye 
make-up only in the evening. Many 
women who do not need or care for 
rouge in the daytime find that they 
look much better in the evening if 
they wear a little. And many women 
who skimp on their make-up founda- 
tions in the daytime feel that they are 
necessary for their dressed-up face. 

I cannot say that there seems to be 
much difference in the use of lipstick 
by day or evening. The maximum 


amount seems to prevail at all times. 
But I do know this: Many smart 
women use different types of lipstick 
with their sports clothes and daytime 
costumes from what they use with 
their evening frocks. With simple 
clothes they like a mildly colored 
lubricating type of stick which makes 
their lips look shiny and rosy and 
natural. While in the evening they 
want to go the limit on indelibility. 

To return to the eyes for a moment, 
I might just say here that there is a 
great fashion for shining eyes and 
that many women who do not care to 
use color on their lids in the daytime 
do put on a shiny cream which gives a 
brilliant look to the eyes. Oily prepa- 
rations for the lashes and brows are 
also very popular. 


F COURSE I need hardly remind 

you here that the make-up shown 
on this lovely sculptured head which 
Georgi has created is only one typical 
iliustration of what can be done. This 
young woman fortunately is quite 
young, and her—can we say ‘‘natu- 
ral’’—coloring is as perfect as the 
artist could make it. Hair, eyes, skin, 
age, contour of face and choice of cos- 
tume colors are taken into account by 
the clever make-up consultant when 
she tells you and me what to wear on 
our faces and how to put it on. 

The yellow reds, it’s my own pri- 
vate opinion, are young and robust 
colors, and should be handled care- 
fully on the older or more fragile 
skin. Older women should try to find 
rouges and lipsticks which are softer 
and more miotlater in tone and they 
should also wear less per face. Their 
powder foundation should be of very 
light weight and their face powders 
fine and delicate. Everything which 
is applied to a lined skin shows up far 
more readily than it would on a 
smooth young one. So the simple fact 
which we must face as we grow older 
is that the more we need cosmetics, 
the less we can afford to put on. 
Quality must take the place of quan- 


_tity in make-up; and the suave and 


subtle touch must be substituted for 
the bold poster technique. Which, 
after all, seems to me quite as logical 
as that sub-deb clothes should be re- 
served for sub-debs—and not urged on 
mé when I go to buy a tasty little 
costume adapted to my years. 

I should like most awfully to im- 
press on any of you who may be read- 
ing these words that in my opinion 
make-up is grand. There’s nothing 
like a little powder on the face to give 
you self-possession, or a little color on 
the cheeks to make you brave. But 
make-up is no set formula. It can’t be 
for there are so many different con- 
siderations for each individual face. 
To my mind, however, this only makes 
the personal make-up problem more 
fascinating. For as you wield your 
lipstick well, you may have the satis- 
faction of knowing that you are your 
own best masterpiece.

TO WOMEN... 


Doctors put strong emphasis on this 
warning: ‘‘ Make sure that toilet tissue 
is soft before you use it!”’ 


And women understand at once 
why such*advice is especially aimed 
at them. For they have an extra use for 
toilet tissue. Too intimately personal 
to be stated openly ... but far, far 
too important to be ignored! 


With this special need of women in 
mind—Northern Paper Mills has 
adopted a special process. A process 
that results in a tissue that is 100% 
pure cellulose—similar to cotton! So 
each and every sheet of Northern 


IT’S IMPORTANT 


Tissue is supremely soft. 


Moreover—Northern Tissue is guar- 
anteed free from any trace of splintery 
groundwood. It’s highly absorbent — 
as only pure cellulose can be. And for 
absolute cleanliness, Northern Tissue 
is sterilized the hospital way—under 
steam heat. 


A tissue so unquestionably safe for 
women is naturally safest for the chil- 
dren—for the whole family. Order 
three rolls of Northern Tissue —or 
Gauze (a companion product)... and 
let their softness convince you. North- 
ern Paper Mills, Green Bay, Wisconsin. 


NORTHERN TISSUE 


and GAUZE 


Both Northern Tissue and Gauze have been 
especially designed for women. Northern 
Tissue is pure white. Gauze is for women 
who prefer a lighter-weight, cream-colored 
paper, at a slightly lower cost. Both North- 
ern Tissue and Gauze are linen-ized for even 
greater softness.

ASSEMBLED BY KATHARINE RIGGS

cure is being protected and one’s 
hands beautified by soft white 
lambskin gloves cream-treated 
with lanolin. (Yes, they're ap- 
proved by the Good Looks de- 
partment.) The gloves are so 
nice-looking that you'll be proud 
to wear them. We suggest them for 
light gardening and for driving. 


COOKS who like to work in 
fV the grand manner—that is, 
without too many measuring 
cups and other impedimenta— 
will like one feature of a new line 
of enamelware. This feature is 
the graduated measure in cups 
and pints baked into the inside of 
every saucepan, kettle and double 
boiler. Nor is this all. Shapes 
are modern and interesting, bot- 
toms are black for efficient heat ab- 
sorption, double rims seal in flavor. 


A FINE demonstration of a 
(VY new cleaner for painted sur- 
faces is going on at the left. Per- 
haps you think your kitchen 
walls aren't so dirty as this one— 
but we dare you to wash a few 
square feet and see! The cleaner 
is a flour-like substance which is 
added to water, sloshed on with a 
big brush, allowed to set for a 
few minutes, then rinsed off care- 
fully. The last step is a rub-down 
with a clean sponge. Professional 
painters swear by this product 
(we've heard one of them at it). 


WHEN entertaining in-laws 

at contract, do please make 
sure that the cards are properly 
illuminated. Such sad things can 
happen! There will be no excuse 
for trumping a partner's ace with 
the new indirect pin-up wall lamp 
sketched. It throws no shadows 
and can’t shine in anyone's eyes. 
The lamp, pleasantly © modern 
in design, is made of aluminum 
with a translucent bowl that 
looks like opal glass when lighted.

panion booklet Frocks for

new slip cover. The material,

printed mohair in a charming 
magnolia pattern on a_ back- 
ground of navy blue, turquoise, 
gold or tobacco brown. Come to 
think of it, mohair is an ideal 
slip-cover fabric—it doesn't 
wrinkle, it sheds dust, it clings to 
the upholstery beneath instead of 
shifting around when sat upon.

"How I hate that 
Sneaker Smell!" 


Pek years mothers all over the United States were 
troubled about the disagreeable perspiration odor 
which clings to ordinary sneakers. Then came the 
Hygeen Insole and put a stop to all this! For, as en- 
thusiastic reports from thousands of parents prove, 
this amazing new insole prevents offensive “sneaker 
smell” from developing! 

How does Hygeen Insole “work’’? Instead of soak- 
ing up perspiration, it permits it to evaporate quickly, 
leaving Hood Canvas Shoes free from offensive odor! 

Then there are Hood Shoes—made by the patented 
Xtrulock process—that are molded in one smooth 
unit. No stitches to break—no seams to chafe feet or 
wear holes in socks. The scientifically ventilated* uppers 
provide tiny air spaces which let cool air shoot right 
through the canvas. Besides, Hood Shoes **""™"? ending; 
are washable. No artificial stiffening to 
wash out and leave the canvas limp! 

Xtrulock Shoes give extra wear with- 
out extra weight.
