Facin g the World 


A Tower Room Talk 


By ANNE BRYAN MCCALL 


EAR ANNE Mc- 
CALL: Ihavetwo 
children, a boy of six 
and a little girl of five. 
I already worry about 
their future. It fills me 
with dread to think 
that in the next twelve 
or fourteen years they 
will be facing the world 
of dangers and temptations, the kind 
of world I had to face at eighteen. And 
maybe they will have the same hard 
time I had, and that would be pretty 
bad; and maybe they will take to that 
world as I didn't—I mean maybe they 
will take to those crude behaviors and 
like them. I hated the cheap be- 
haviors—the drinking and the petting 
and all that—yet I craved popularity 
terribly. I’ve always craved it. If 
only there was something to do to 
guard them against the later years! 
Jupirx M— 


EAR ANNE McCALL: My 
daughter is a beautiful girl. She 
is eighteen. I brought her up strictly 
and I never let her do any of the things 
that I thought were unladylike. Well, 
much good all that care of mine did. 
She does as she likes now and the 
things she likes, I regret to say, are 
these cheap and tawdry behaviors. 
Matitpa G— 


As Solomon Said 


Y DEAR MRS. M—: The answer 

to that sentence in your letter, 
“Tf only there was something to do to 
guard them against the later years!" 
seems to me to be quite simply, 
‘There is.’’ I believe the reason a 
good many mothers nowadays take 
the ‘‘after-all-what-can-you-do-about- 
it’’ attitude is that they do not really 
see the problem until it is rather late to 
do much about it. But when anyone 
begins to think of it early, as you are 
doing, there seems to me a good deal 
that can be done—and a good deal of 
necessity too for doing the right and 
not the wrong thing. 

The main thing after all is to do it 
early. Solomon said a good many 
ages ago that as the twig is bent the 
tree inclines—and a good many peo- 
ple in one way or another have made 
similar observations ever since; but 
now we know scientifically and by 
way of psychology that our later lives 
are pretty well determined by our 
childhood years. So we know that if 
a child is brought up wisely its later 
years will almost inevitably show 
wisdom. 

Someone will say, ‘‘ But how about 
the second letter on this page—the 
letter of Matilda G—? There is some- 
one who did her best to train her child 
wisely, and look at the result!”’ 

But fundamental laws are not likely 
to fail. Generally when we look into 
it, it is we who have failed. You 
notice Mrs. G— prepared her daugh- 
ter for the future by never letting her 
do things that she (Mrs. G—) thought 
unladylike. In other words she seems 
to have had the habit of making her 


child’s decisions for her 
—and I know of no bet- 
ter way of fitting a child 
not to know how to 
make wise decisions of 
her own than just that. 
Since she was brought 
up to depend on some- 
body else’s judgment, 
not her own, it seems 
not surprising at all that when she 
was grown she should continue to 
follow other people's tastes—in this 
case the tastes of the pretty crude 
young set she was thrown with. 


The Greatest Safeguard 


HERE I believe is the whole heart 

of the problem of how to bring up 
children so that they will not be a 
prey to anybody’s and everybody’s 
tastes, but will have from the begin- 
ning definite tastes of their own. In 
other words, the greatest safeguard for 
the youth of tomorrow is that the 
child of today should be allowed to 
develop good taste of its own. 

Just how to do this would fill 
volumes—but the general principle is 
simple. Let the child learn early that 
choosing is one of the most important 
things in life and one of its greatest 
privileges and then give it the chance 
to choose what is worth while; what 
is lovely instead of what is not 
lovely; what has value instead of 
what is valueless. A few simple 
phrases used often will help; such as, 
“Which do you think is the lovelier?”’ 
“Which would you choose?’’ And 
phrases to avoid are such as, ‘‘ You do 
that because I tell you to,’’ ‘‘ Because 
I say so,’ “‘ You are going to do this 
because I want you to.”’ 


The Craving for Popularity 


Fs eee thing to beware of if 
you want your children in their 
older years to have good taste, is en- 
couraging them to care too much what 
people think of them. You say that 
you yourself craved popularity. To 
like and enjoy popularity—yes; but to 
crave it so that one is willing to 
sacrifice one’s good taste and ideals 
to it is precisely the sort of thing 
that sends so many of our young peo- 
ple to compromising with their own 
standards rather than not be what 
they call popular. 

The truth seems to be that they 
simply haven't good or cultivated 
taste. They are not even able to 
chocse between the kind of popularity 
that is worth having and the kind that 
is not. It all comes down at last I be 
lieve to the ability to choose wisely; 
and that ability, like most other 
things of value in our later lives, be- 
gins in our early years. 


Nore: Anne Bryan McCall is glad to 
help you solve your human relations 
problems. To anyone interested she 
will send a selected list of books on 
child psychology. Address her in care 
of the Woman's Home Companion, 
250 Park Avenue, New York City.

Let’s Eat Out of Doors 


HERE is something about food and drink 

served in the open, combined with a view, 

that smacks of ambrosia and nectar on 
Mount Olympus. By this I do not mean 
the common or garden variety of picnic, but the 
transference of all the comfort and luxury of 
indoor eating to some cool and airy retreat out 
of doors, where one may enjoy the ease of sitting in a 
chair and putting one’s knees under a real table, with 
the kitchen and the refrigerator still near enough to 
have the hot dishes hot and the cold drinks cold. 

We, in our own family, eat anywhere we want to 
when the moment arrives. If it happens to be sunny or 
mosquito-y we lunch on our screened porch which is 
thickly shaded with vines. If the air is a little chill 
there 1s a sheltered corner of the garden with a marble 
table and comfortable chairs where there is no wind and 
lots of sun. If the day has been unspeakably hot and 
sticky, we choose a sunken flagstone terrace shaded by a 
great tree and overlooking a lovely wooded glade. We 
carry our trays a little farther, to be sure, but we escape 
from all that reminds us of hot offices and the routine of 
housekeeping. Or again we choose the open lawn at the 


By HELEN PERRY CuRTIS 


ILLUSTRATOR: RAY PROHASKA 


back of the house where we catch the slightest breeze 
and where the kitchen window sill serves as a conven- 
ient pantry shelf. At this point too we hear the radio 
distinctly, but scftened by distance; and if we happen 
to find Neapolitan serenades or Viennese waltzes on the 
program, the ensemble is perfect. 

In other words, sometimes we go where tables and 
chairs invite, sometimes we invite the tables and chairs 
to go along with us. 

The outdoor table may be set in as many different 
ways as the indoor one. Your fine glass and china and 
silver will be greatly enhanced by the outdoor setting, 
or modern glass and pottery in enchanting colors may 
be bought for a song. Colorful linens of every kind, 
much gayer than those you can use in a conventional 
dining-room, fit perfectly into a background of greenery 


and bright flowers, and the many fascinating 
and practical paper table accessories to be found 
in the shops need no special pleading. Wire 
clips may even be had to fit over the edge of the 
table and hold your covers in place, or little 
pockets (shaped like flowers or fruit) may be 
appliquéd to the corners of an open-air table- 
cloth, into which three or four clean pebples or other 
weights are slipped to keep the cloth from blowing up 
on the table. 

Now for a few pertinent details about the food. I 
make it a rule that no food shall appear until the 
family or guests have assembled, ready to be served or to 
carry out their own. Hot dishes are taken from the 
stove only at the last moment and served in warm 
covered dishes. Platters with hot-water containers be- 
neath them, and a metal lid which keeps the meat hot 
until the moment of carving, are ideal for this pur- 
pose. Hot biscuits should be wrapped in a napkin and 
sandwiches or slices of bread should be covered with a 
damp cloth until brought to the table, so that the air 
will not dry them up. Cold drinks may be kept cold 
with ice cubes or thermos [conTINUED ON PAGE 84]

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


Let’s Eat Out of Doors 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 83] 


pitchers. Butter, celery and radishes 
may be served in bowls of chipped ice. 
Salads may be arranged beforehand on 
plates or in a salad bow] and brought 
forth crisply at the last moment from 
the icebox. Frozen desserts served on 
dishes already chilled in the refrigera- 
tor will keep cool for some time. 

For myself, I plan meals that require 
a simple table setting and only two or 
three courses, with each course, hot or 
cold, depending upon the weather, 
arranged on a single serving dish or 
tray. If your family happens to like 
salads and cheeses and fruits your task 
is a simpler one than it is if there must 
always be roast beef and boiled vege- 
tables, but every housekeeper will 
want to work out her own ideas. I 
mention a few of our favorites merely 
because they contain suggestions for 
simple cooking and ease of service. A 
screen door, by the way, which may 
be pushed open with the foot but 
which will close itself noiselessly by 
means of a strong spring, and with 
felt pads along the door casings, will 
add much to the convenience of serv- 
ing. Or if the kitchen window is a 
quicker means of access than the 
kitchen door, it may be made doubly 
convenient by wide folding shelves 
built one inside and one outside of 
the window sill. 


A Continental Breakfast 


F THERE is a French or Italian 

bakery available, try café au lait 
with croissants, or chocolate with 
brioches or any one of the other delec- 
table breakfast rolls. Your center- 
piece may be a bowl of tangerines 
arranged on shiny leaves, or a flat 
basket lined with grape leaves and 
filled with several varieties of grapes 
and purple and white figs. Fruit puts 
the finishing touch on this kind of 
breakfast. 

If, however, your hard-working 
husband and your ravenous children 
want a bigger send-off for the day you 
may have your electric toaster, waffle 
iron, egg boiler, or chafing-dish con- 
nected with a long-distance plug, and 
have a comfortable breakfast on the 
porch with no running back and forth 
to the kitchen. 


Luncheon and Tea 


UNCHEONS in the heat of the day 
may be extremely simple but 
deliciously cool and appetizing—such 
as a cold fruit cocktail or fruit cup, a 
crisp salad of crunchy cabbage, celery, 
gtated raw carrot and chopped salted 
peanuts, cold sliced liver-loaf or as- 
sorted cold cuts from the delicatessen, 
iced tea with lemon and mint, and a 
wine gelatine with thin cookies for 
dessert. On a cool day a hot meat 
broth in a tureen, a vegetable cas- 
serole with hot corn bread wrapped 
in a napkin, and a cottage pudding 
with a hot lemon and raisin sauce, 
warms the ‘‘cockles of the heart’’ as 
well as neighboring portions of the 
anatomy. 

Tea is perhaps the easiest meal to 
serve out of doors. The temperature 
of the drink may depend somewhat 
on the temperature of the day, but hot 
tea is invariably refreshing even on a 
warm day. Then there is hot coffee, 
maté—a growing favorite for the tea 
hour—iced tea with lemon or mint or 
fruit juice added, iced coffee, iced 


chocolate with whipped cream, or any 
one of a dozen delectable cold drinks. 

A plate of dainty sandwiches gar- 
nished with sprigs of parsley, canapés 
made of cream cheese with a touch of 
black currant jelly in the center, small 
cream puffs stuffed with chicken salad, 
hot baking-powder biscuit made like 
small Parker House rolls with grated 
cheese folded in, or just plain crisp 
Devonshire toast, cookies, dainty cup 
cakes, thin slices of fruit cake—any or 
many of these help to make it a party. 

The most marvelous tea I can re- 
member was served me on an English 
lawn, high on a hilltop overlooking 
the chimney-pots of Ventnor and the 
sea beyond. We had hot tea of course, 
thin slices of bread and butter into 
which we folded crisp sprigs of water- 
cress, broken sponge cake and enor- 
mous strawberries served on the hull 
on pink Staffordshire plates with a 
pile of sugar in the center. 


The Hearty Meal 


OR dinner a baked ham, around 

which sweet potatoes and portions 
of cauliflower have been browned, 
served in the deep pottery dish in 
which it has all been baked, is deli- 
cious. (And the ham may be used 
cold for luncheon the next day.) This 
meal may be started off with peas and 
carrot cubes molded in lime jelly 
served on a lettuce leaf, and finished 
with a shortcake of hot buttered bak- 
ing-powder biscuit and crushed straw- 
berries or raspberries topped with 
whipped cream. 

A baked fish stuffed with seasoned 
mashed potato, arranged on a platter 
with strips of bacon and broiled half- 
tomatoes au gratin makes a meal in 
itself. Hors d'oeuvres, canapés, shrimp 
cocktail, chilled grapefruit baskets 
filled with fruit cup, hot or jellied 
soup, start the outdoor dinner off well; 
and fruit shortcakes, sherbets, 
whipped gelatine or icebox desserts 
give it a delicious wind-up. 

A real Italian dinner seems particu- 
larly fitting for outdoor consumption. 
Several of the usual courses may be 
eliminated, but I will suggest them 
here. A vegetable soup with grated 
Parmesan cheese, fillet of sole with 
lemons, a dish of spaghetti or noodles 
with any one of a dozen sauces, an 
entrée of asparagus with more grated 
Parmesan cheese and Italian bread 
sticks, a main course of crisp lettuce 
salad with roasted fowl, and a dessert 
of assorted cheeses on a wooden cheese 
tray, a great bowl of mixed fruit, and 
coffee. Having coffee in a different 
nook in the garden adds to the charm 
of the outdoor meal. 

These are only snapshots, taken at 
random, of some of the settings and 
menus we find specially enjoyable. If 
you choose your food combinations 
with care and take pains not to have 
them monotonous, your outdoor meals 
will be extremely popular with your 
family and friends. The children will 
be glad to set the table and carry out 
the trays just because anything that is 
different is fun, and if you are giving a 
party you won't need to worry about 
its being a success. By the time your 
guests have learned to manipulate the 
kitchen window, and roll out the tea 
wagon without jiggling it, and bal- 
ance a tray while going through a 
screen door, they will feel perfectly at 
home and quite hilarious as well.

‘* Eh? Oh—’’ He paused. ‘* Maybe,” 
he suggested, ‘‘if you dropped a—er—

suggest something—"’ 

**Salt,’’ said Binks wearily. ‘‘ Other-

tests proved that it’s the best canned 
soup there is. I wouldn’t know be- 
cause I lost my taste for it. Just 
now I think it’s the most unappetizing 
soup in the world.”’ 

She paused. Fred and Laura Gerard 
were ambling toward her. Their faces 
lighted up with surprise. 

“Why, Tyler—when did you ar- 
rive? Why didn’t you come to the 
housee”’ 


BINKS’ companion got to his feet. 

“Tt was hot in town and I couldn’t 
resist the beach the minute I got off 
the boat—"’ 

He stopped at a brief strangled cry. 

““Tyler—not Tyler Howard?”’ 

Fred Gerard made instant amends. 

““Oh—I beg your pardon, Binks. 
May I present Mr. Howard, Mrs.—’’ 

But Binks was gone. 

She fled to the swimming platform 
and Matthew was there, sitting with 
feet dangling, watching Sylvia swim. 

‘Binks, darling,’ he began getting 
up, ‘‘I’ve been looking for you to give 
you a swimming lesson. See, I brought 
down your life-preserver—’’ 

‘* Matthew, I’ve ruined everything. 
Tyler Howard. The m-memorandum.”’ 

‘What on earth—’’ said Matthew. 

“‘T said his soup was awful,’’ gasped 
Binks. ‘‘I said he was an old frozen- 
face. I’ve ruined everything—”’ 

‘Now take it easy and tell me just 
what happened—’’ 

‘*Matthew—you'll never forgive

M-maybe you n-never did really. 
M-maybe you wish you’d m-married

‘‘For heaven’s sake,’’ said Matthew 
alarmed. ‘‘Who?’’ Then he laughed 
as he caught her quick glance toward 
the water. ‘‘Her? Sylvia? You must be 
crazy. I couldn't love anybody in the 
world of women,’’ he concluded

Binks became pale. ‘‘Oh, isn’t that 
awful?”’ she wailed. *‘ After what I've 
done to you. I’ve ruined everything— 
I even called him names—’’ 

“Who? Tyler Howard?’’ An odd

about. They both saw Sylvia with 
upflung arm, struggling— 

‘* Help—Matt—cramp—”’ 

Matthew turned to Binks wildly. 

‘‘Where is it? The memorandum?” 

Her hand flew panic-struck to the 
bosom of her dress. 

“T don’t know—I don’t know—”’ 

Matthew jumped into the water. 
It was a leap taken more in the man- 
ner, thought Binks with a queer for- 
lorn satisfaction, of a man in suicidal 
despair than of one gone to the rescue 
of beauty in distress. She saw Mat- 
thew bring up alongside. She saw 
Sylvia’s arm close around his neck. 
Matthew tried at once to loose her 
grip but she clung, and Binks saw 
something she knew the poor fighting 
frantic Matthew did not see. She saw 
Sylvia smile and close her arm still 
more closely around his neck. 

Just a joke, this was. In another 
second or so she'd let go. 

But Binks decided suddenly not to 
wait. She had read somewhere that 


the way to break a drowning person’s 
grip was to strike him—or even her—a 
smart stunning blow. No one, to be 
sure, was drowning, yet the ancient 
theory lured her irresistibly. 

Off came her dress and her shoes and 
stockings. She slipped the inner tube 
up over her absurd little shorts and 
dropped without a qualm over the 
side of the platform. As she fell a 
nail scratched her but she gave no 
heed. Evenly, rhythmically, she pro- 
gressed toward the two in the water. 
Smartly she brought up a small fist 
and poked it between the wide 
astounded eyes of Miss Karnan. 

“What are you—ugh—doing—”’ 
sputtered Miss Karnan treading water. 

‘“Drowning people—have to— 
splush!—sock ‘em when—glub—they 
grip—gloop!"’ said Binks and turned 
about for the return journey, feeling, 
despite all the horrors of the day, 
somehow strong and purged. 

‘‘Binks,’’ shouted Matthew fol- 
lowing rapidly. ‘‘Great heavens— 
wait—you'll sink—deep water—”’ 

“Can't,’’ gasped Binks. ‘‘Can’t 
sink. Life belt on—’’ 

“But it’s busted,’’ yelled Matthew. 

Binks gulped. She swallowed water 
and felt a sickening anticipation of the 
sinking of leaden legs. In panic she 
kicked them; in blind obedience to 
automatic rhythm her arms moved as 
arms should move, and her legs—her 
legs did not sink! She was swimming— 
swimming, raced Binks’ flighty 
thoughts, when it didn’t matter any 
more . . . she'd spoiled Matthew's 
chance with Tyler Howard. 


AT THE dock Fred and Laura Ge- 
rard and Tyler Howard awaited 
them. 

Miss Karnan was the first to land. 
With no word or backward glance she 
swept, cold, dripping and outraged, 
past the others toward the cottage. 

Binks, staying waist-deep in water 
on account of an acute shortage of 
clothing, did not even see her go. 

Matthew turned about. ‘* Binks, for 
heaven's sake come out of there.”’ 

“Tc-can’t. I haven't anything on— 
m-much. Anyway I’ve ruined every- 
thing. I—I think I won't come out.”’ 

Matthew said, ‘‘Nonsense!’’ and 
said a word to the others who hastily 
turned the other way. Binks dressed 
and met presently the amused eyes of 
Tyler Howard. 

“I picked this up from the beach,”’ 
he said, ‘‘after you rushed away.’’ He 
held the memorandum in his hand. 
‘Mrs. Farrell,’’ he told Matt, ‘‘says 
our soups are too concentrated. She 
knows because she has tried them all.’”’ 

Tyler Howard continued gravely, 
“I read your memorandum; it’s a 
very thorough job, Mr. Farrell. Come 
to my office Monday morning—at 
ten, say?” 

Matt nodded dumbly. His eyes flew 
to Binks. Something she’d been miss- 
ing for a long time shone in them. 

“T swam, Matt,’’ she burst out 
wanting to clinch things. 

“I saw you. It was marvelous, 
Binks,’’ said Matthew fervently. 

“You know,’ Mr. Howard told 
them as they strolled toward the cot- 
tage, ‘‘I didn’t learn to swim until I 
was quite grown. Couldn’t seem to 
get over a fear of the water. What a 
person really needs is—’ 

‘*Confidence,’’ said Binks thought- 
fully.

Add a Door Hood 


To an uninviting entrance 


HE entrance of a house always conveys something of its 

character. This attention to the doorway goes back to 

the days when man lived in a cave and hung up animal 
skins to protect the opening. The wooden door came later 
and as the owner decorated it and sat before it in his leisure 
it became a symbol to him of home and protection and it re- 
mains that to this day. A need for further shelter from sun 
and rain brought the projecting covering. The charm that 
has grown around this is especially exemplified in the early 
Colonial style throughout the country. 

The over-door hood shown above is in this tradition. 
Besides its decorative quality it has very evident practical 
uses. It gives protection from the weather to anyone wait- 
ing on the doorstep; it affords a direct cover from house door 
to automobile if the driveway is adjacent; it makes the 
kitchen entrance seem a little like an entry. The hood 
projects three feet six inches from the wall and is four feet 
six inches wide. 

The metal hood can be painted in any desired color;

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


IHIN fruits from apple and 

peach trees that have set 
fruits heavily by removing all 
those now undersized and all 
that can possibly touch each 
other as they advance in size. 
Use clippers to take off apples. 
Clip the dead flower heads from 
lilacs, rhododendrons and azaleas 
for better bloom next season. 
Sow seeds of perennials now for 
next year’s bloom. Destroy poi- 


son ivy now before it has a 
chance to go to seed. Often all 
that is necessary is to chop off 
the trunk of the vines at or just 
below the surface of the ground 
without the risk of tearing them 
down from their support. Never 
let anyone work around this 
plant who is not known to be im- 
mune. Results of its poison are 
sometimes very serious and con- 
tinue for years. 


All on a Summert’s Day 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 21] 


Burns manuscripts in the Kingston 
library and could they make an ap- 
pointment. A photostat of a title- 
page somewhat dubiously that of a 
first edition. Would the librarian of 
the Kingston copy compare their 
title-pages and advise of any varia- 
tions? A library on the west coast 
writing that they were planning an 
exhibition of early books on the 
Spanish explorations south and west 
and asking if they could include any 
of the Kingston Florida collection. 

Such mail and similar tasks had 
been a familiar routine in her life for 
ten years. But only in the past twelve 
months had she pursued them in these 
surroundings and she could still feel 
delight and amazement that for tyr- 
anny and occasional censure she had 
exchanged praise and even a small 
share of renown. 


HER FATHER had been in his 

youth a casual collector of books. 
A naturally sharp and fairly idle intel- 
ligence had increasingly fastened his 
attention on his hobby until it became 
the core of his existence. Stephen Ware 
had brought back his first acquisitions 
from his university days to the old 
square white house in Bangor that 
was itself a rarity. And there on 
mounting shelves he had steadily laid 
his increasing rows of books year atter 
year. He had married, Evelyn had 
been born, her mother had died, he 
had lost his health, the world had 
moved out of one century and into an- 
other, but his attention had never 
wavered from his collection. 

Inevitably that collection had be- 
come famous. All these years he had 
steadfastly refused even fabulous offers 
for this volume or that. So the collec- 
tion was intact when he finally offered 
it at auction. Almost as if his life had 
actually been in his books, as exter- 
nally it seemed to be, he died a month 
later. Evelyn, dazed by these events, 
was left, like Othello, without occu- 
pation. 

While she was still in school she had 
begun opening her father’s mail and 
reading him extracts from catalogues. 
As she grew up she did more of his 
correspondence and under his tutelage 
gradually made expert arrangements 
and annotated lists. Without knowing 
it she became herself an expert. 

But if she was not aware of this, 
others were and when Mr. Kingston's 
buyer and librarian left him after years 
of ministration (to accept quite ap- 
propriately a diplomatic post) that 
great man bethought himself of Ware's 


daughter. It was his letter that shook . 


Evelyn out of her lethargy and his 
plan that she accepted. With no more 
than a look around she had the furni- 
ture shrouded, the shutters drawn and 
the heavy doors locked and went off 
to New York and the first freedom she 
had ever known. 

Something gay and light-hearted 
that had survived her years with an 
ill-tempered and crotchety old man 
came bubbling up to the surface now. 
Everything pleased her, seemed in- 
deed to exist expressly to give her 
pleasure—James, Mr. Kingston, these 
newer richer gaudier book rooms with 
their newer richer gaudier bindings, 
the tiny apartment that looked on a 
great river, the clothes in the shop 
windows and the people on the 
streets, the colored postman who 
bowed when she met him in the hall— 


it was all for her and she was endlessly 
glad of it. 

So when late afternoon came she 
locked up the treasures she had re- 
moved from their grilles, washed her 
face and painted her lips and went off 
to meet Mr. Brandigo, as joyful as a 
child ona picnic. Like the lady in the 
song she had promised to meet him in 
the park. It seemed as if James always 
insisted on meeting her somewhere if 
she wanted him to call for her; and 
would call for her if it was her idea to 
meet outside. Well, thought Evelyn, 
I suppose men are like that; he’s just 
as bossy, in his way, as Father was. 

Young Brandigo beamed when she 
atrived. Whenever he waited for 
Evelyn it seemed impossible that she 
would ever come, or that he had ever 
seen her, or that they had ever by 
some divine chance met, in a city so 
encrusted with other and unimportant 
millions. It seemed so totally un- 
likely that out of an old but indifferent 
tie to Tom Ware he should have 
eventually met this flower, incredibly 
his cousin, Evelyn. 

“You've got a new hat on! You 
look as pretty as a picture.”’ 

Evelyn beamed in turn and they 
strolled along the edges of the sheep 
meadow. Late afternoon sun gilded 
the close-cropped grass and the narrow 
stretches of the park took on a glory 
of spaciousness and fair extent. 

But alas, ‘‘picture’’ reminded James 
of his aunt and he told Evelyn the 
episode of the fireside print and the 
wildwood lady. His audience smiled 
with him and chuckled at all the 
respectabilities thus gently mocked. 
Then she said: 

“But of course it’s not true. Your 
aunt can’t know much about the 
things she buys, James. She’s all 
mixed up somehow. The artist she 
meant never painted babies and Mary 
Cassatt never had any connection with 
Monet. She’s just like all these people 
who buy things without any real 
knowledge.”’ 


Nees looked indignant. 
“Look here,”’ he said firmly, ‘‘do 
you know who my aunt is?” 

Miss Ware looked at him coolly. 
‘Very likely not,’’ she observed with 
an edge. 

James blushed. ‘‘I didn’t mean that 
the way it sounds. Only, you know 
she lived in France when she was a 
girl and studied painting with one of 
those birds.”’ 

“Oh,” said Evelyn eager and pleased 
again. ‘‘She paints? How wonderful! 
Fancy being able to look at something 
lovely and then put it down in all its 
colors and shapes.”’ 

“N-no, she doesn’t really paint. She 
just studied.’’ James hesitated. ‘‘She 
wants to see you. She’s going to write 
you a note.” 

‘““You gave her my address?”’ 

“*Yes—I wanted her to know you.” 

‘*James Brandigo, what did you tell 
her? Did you tell her I was lonely? 
You know I’m not. You know I think 
it’s wonderful to do what I want and 
heavenly not knowing too many peo- 
ple. I haven’t half time enough to do 
what I want to do—without someone 
coming around to be kind to me.”’ 

She looked wild, the New Eng- 
lander’s passion against being patron- 
ized rising like a banner. 

James, unconsciously tactful, made

All on a Summer’s Day 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 8g] 


the perfect answer. “‘I thought it 
would be nice for her,’’ he said, ‘‘and 
for me. She brought me up pretty 
much; we lived in her house when I 
was a child and my mother was young 
and gay and Aunt Jenny was the one 
who planned things for us and got us 
to the circus and gave us candles for 
our birthday cakes.”’ 

Evelyn melted again, ‘‘James, you 
are sweet,’’ and inconsequently, ‘‘I 
wish that merry-go-round was bigger. 
I love to ride them. Only these horses 
are too small.”’ 


“We could go to Coney Island,” 
said James. 


EVELYN looked pleased, then 
stern. ‘‘You don’t want to; 
you're just saying that to please me.”’ 
James smiled. ‘‘That doesn’t seem 
so unreasonable. And I do want to— 
at least I want to be with you and 


‘“‘But I wouldn’t want to go to the 
law courts with you and I wouldn’t 
pretend I did.” 

James felt a little confused. ‘‘If it 
comes to that, I’m not fascinated by 
those moldy old books you mull 
over all day, but I can’t see that’s got 


anything to do with merry-go- 
rounds.” 
“Maybe not,” she said doubtfully, 


“but Idon’t think it’s right, trying to 
please people all the time.” 

You please me all the time,”’ said 
James firmly. ‘*‘What does your New 
England conscience make of that?’ 

Evelyn’s earnestness vanished in a 
chuckle on the soft contagious warm 
note that would have melted a harder 
heart than Mr. Brandigo’s. 

“Oh, well, if you don’t try—’ 

And they both laughed. At noth- 
ing, at late spring bursting around 
them in city dress, at riders going by 
on horseback and younger riders going 
by on scooters, at wisteria thick over 
arbors and baby carriages crowding 
pigeons beneath them. 

The late long light lasted them out 
their walk; as it grew dark they ate in 
arestaurant already agreeably familiar, 
idly found a Mickey Mouse for a 
pleasant quarter-hour and then walked 
back to Evelyn’s apartment. Ele- 
ments as simple as could be found; but 
out of them they made content, con- 
tent such as Mr. Brandigo—with his 
tempestuous marriage and subsequent 
disaster, his dogged and difficult re- 
turn to an interrupted profession—and 
such as Miss Ware—with her severe 
taskmaster and her own delicate ver- 
sion of her father’s pride—had never 
touched before. 

Before Evelyn’s apartment house 
they paused. She yawned childishly. 
“T feel cll full of air and sleep,"’ she 
said comfortably. James, sensing the 
right moment, brought up the un- 
finished business of his Aunt Jenny and 
Evelyn promised sweetly to go to see 
her. 


BUT AS things turned out it was 
two months before the meeting 


came about. James followed up 
Evelyn’s promise too sharply which 
led her to say crossly, ‘‘I do wish you 
wouldn’t hound me ao *  Whereat 
James replied firmly, ‘* You said you'd 
go, and I told Aunt Jenny you would, 
and you'll have to.’’ All that this 
produced was an argument which 
came out as such conversations usually 
come out—with nothing settled. Only, 


in the interval Mrs. Taylor had gone 
back to the country. 

When Mrs. Taylor came to town 
again early in August James, walking 
more delicately, contrived to win 
Evelyn to action, Evelyn feeling a 
little shy and a little stern as she ar- 
rived was nevertheless fascinated by 
Mrs. Taylor’s apartment. Lofty ceil- 
ings, long corridors and huge rooms 
took it back to an earlier day; bulky 
covers of Holland linen concealed fur- 
niture behind the floor-length folds and 
pictures and mirrors were swathed 
inches deep in tarlatan. At the win- 
dows tall shutters steeped the rooms 
in gloom. 

Mrs. Taylor appeared and _ inter- 
rupted Evelyn's fascinated absorption. 
She shad a quick glance oddly like 
James’ own but with an added sharp- 
ness. She had too a quick light voice 
that Evelyn liked at once. It was 
plain that her impulse was to like her 
first look at Evelyn. Evelyn found in 
her that rarest of the sympathies, a 
woman of another day who can live 
in this, the worldly person whose 
worldliness has added only to the 
head without taking from the heart. 


SO PRESENTLY Mrs. Taylor, 

having covered a good deal of 
ground, was saying, ‘What do you 
and James do to amuse yourselves, to 
forget it’s hot, to pass the time, 
to—’’ She waved her hands, still 
plump and pretty and expressive. 

“‘Oh, we go and look at the rivers 
and the people and Sunday nights we 
go to the Stadium concerts—’’ 

Mrs. Taylor nodded. ‘‘ Always 
something up that way for lovers. I 
don’t know what it is about uptown 
but when I was a girl we used to go up 
to the speedway Sunday mornings— 
in the winter though—and watch the 
horses. They would go at a good clip. 
I always liked it better than any rac- 
ing I ever saw afterward. The first 
cutter race always brought the winner 
a magnum of champagne from the old 
roadhouse on Jerome Avenue where 
they ended. I don’t suppose there are 
any prizes at your Stadium.” 

Evelyn shook her head. ‘'Except 
that it’s pretty beautiful. With cars 
going by very quietly mostly so that 
you're only conscious of their small 
lights. It’s all lights. Stars and 
matches where people are smoking 
and red tail-lights on cars. Oh, it’s 
lovely. And James and I have such a 
good time.” 

‘So I gather from James,”’ said his 
aunt; and then briskly, ‘‘Have you 
made up your minds yet to get mar- 
ried?”’ 

This question which might have 
been cruel or impertinent or super- 
ficial was none of these and Evelyn 
gave it the dignity of a considered 
reply. 

“T don’t know exactly why we 
don’t,”’ she said slowly. ‘‘I do think 
James is a dear, and I love being with 
him, and the way he looks and talks 
and smiles, and I'd really rather be 
with him than with anyone—”’ 

Mrs. Taylor smiled. ‘‘It sounds 
authentic so far.”’ 

Evelyn's gravity broke into the soft 
laugh that was so unexpected from her 
earnestness. 

“It’s just that he’s so bossy,’’ she 
added then, gravely, looking at Mrs. 
Taylor very straight and rather wist- 
fully. [CONTINUED ON PAGE 91]

THE EASY WAY TO STOP PERSPIRATION 


All on a Summer’s Day 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 90] 


Mrs. Taylor tried to think of James 
as masterful. She failed. ‘‘In what 
way, my dear?”’ 

“In every way, it seems to me,”’ said 
Evelyn mournfully. ‘‘We were tulk- 
ing about places to live—just talking, 
you know, not about us—and he said 
the only place he really would like to 
live would be in the country in a white 
house with green shutters. He was so 
firm about it too. So I didn’t say any- 
thing. But you know, Mrs. Taylor, 
I’ve lived all my life in a white house 
with green shutters. And I like the 
city. I like the people and being left 
alone and the look of a city and the 
feeling of being part of a great city. 
So you see—’’ 

““Hm-m,’’ said Mrs. Taylor, ‘‘I 
don’t know that I’d be so down- 
hearted. Perhaps James’ tastes aren’t 
so fixed as you think.”’ 


&’_\ EVELYN continued her mournful 

recital: ‘‘And then he’s not only 
always so decided about things but he 
decides things right away. I like to 
think things over awhile and make 
my mind up afterward.” 

Mrs. Taylor again marveled at James 
the decisive. Still, perhaps he was 
like that. 

‘‘And another thing,’’ went on the 
soft insistent monologue, ‘‘I don’t be- 
lieve he likes my job and I love it. I 
couldn’t bear to stop what I’m doing.”’ 

“Oh, I think you’re wrong about 
that,’’ said Mrs. Taylor welcoming an 
opportunity for opposing one ob- 
stacle. ‘‘Ithink James is very proud of 
your work. He told me, bragging, you 
knew all about Mary Cassatt,’’ and she 
twinkled companionably at her guest. 

Evelyn, remembering her strictures 
to James on this point, blushed. But 
Mrs. Taylor did not notice, for she 
went on with considerable eagerness, 
‘That's more than James does, I must 
say. I repeated to him some scandal of 
the past the other day, attributing it 
to that blameless lady and he took it 
all as truth and news. I laughed to 
myself when I realized afterward what 
I’d done. But the poor boy won’t 
ever know the difference.”’ 

Evelyn forbore to tell her of her own 
attempts to disillusion James. 

With the skill of sincere good will 
that nothing can simulate Mrs. Tay- 
lor, returning from her own excursion, 
led the talk further into the differences 
that seemed to remain unresolved be- 
tween this otherwise congenial pair and 
presently an odd fish rose to her bait. 

“James is always trying to please 
me,’’ said Evelyn speaking with more 
violence, ‘‘and I hate that.’’ 

“T think it’s rather nice to be 
pleased,’’ said Mrs. Taylor mildly. 

‘I do too,’’ Evelyn agreed, ‘‘to be 
pleased, but I don’t like anyone—any- 
one you really care about I mean— 
trying to please. Don’t you see, peo- 
ple should be themselves and then if 
they please, all right. But—’’ 

“You feel James isn’t himself? Of 
course I’m his aunt, almost his foster- 
mother, but I do think he’s singularly 
unaffected.’” 

“Tt isn’t that,’’ said Evelyn, un- 
offended, and seemed to give up trying 
to explain what it was. Mrs. Taylor 
felt baffled. 

But presently Evelyn, easy in a 
childlike awareness of complete and 
uncriticizing attention, disclosed the 
shadows of her hesitations. 

““You know, I think the reason I 
like being in Mr. Kingston’s library 


so much is like Mary’s lamb—they 
seem to like me to be there, and to 
think the work I do is, well, is fairly 
good. It’s no different really—except 
there’s more of it to do—from what I 
used to do in my father’s library. But 
he never gave any sign of liking it ex- 
cept by not scolding when I didn’t 
make a mistake. 

‘He was quite old, you know, and 
he’d been an invalid for years and been 
used to having everything done for him 
and everything done to please him. 

‘“My mother’s whole life must 
have been spent in pleasing him, but 
he never acted as if she did. And 
my father always decided everything, 
even about me. He traveled a great 
deal before they married but afterward 
he never went away from Bangor, 
even before he became ill. Then of 
course he couldn’t go anywhere, not 
even upstairs. He had a bedroom on 
the ground floor and he went all 
around the downstairs rooms and out 
into the garden in his wheel-chair.”’ 

Evelyn stopped and blushed again. 
This time Mrs. Taylor saw it and ap- 
proved. A pretty trick most of these 
modern young girls have forgotten, 
she said to herself. 

“Oh, Mrs. Taylor, how dreadful of 
me to talk so much and all about my- 
self.”’ 

“Just what I liked to hear, my 
dear,’’ said Mrs. Taylor comfortingly. 
“Tt doesn’t sound like a gay life fora 
young girl.” 

“It wasn’t bad. It sounds worse to 
hear about than it was to live in. I 
had lots of cousins and I used to go to 
camps in summer when I was in school 
and then later to England to stay with 
some friends of Father’s whom he used 
to visit before I was born. They were 
a large family too, like the Wares. 
It’s just that it was very strict and 
severe at home and here it’s all so 
different. Sometimes I think it can’t 
be true. Sometimes I am looking at a 
book I have looked at before and I'll 
think I’m back in Father’s library and 
I'll feel all still and stiff. And then 
the telephone will ring and it will be 
James. Saying he’s coming to take me 
to see his Aunt Jenny,’’ she ended ona 
note of mischief and with that melting 
of soberness into fun that Mrs. Taylor 
found very endearing. 

His Aunt Jenny got up from her 
Holland-linen chair. ‘‘ Well, my dear 
child, if James can break evil spells, 
you might do worse than take him 
on,” she said lightly. ‘‘It’s a gift, in 
a husband.”’ 


MRS. TAYLOR never seemed to 

do anything about the confidences 

the young people had severally made 
her, though before she went back to 
the country for the rest of the summer 
she asked them to dinner and the three 
found no difficulty in passing a merry 
evening together. But perhaps in the 
sunniness of Aunt Jenny’s common 
sense their doubts had insensibly dis- 
solved. Or perhaps the best purpose 
of any oracle is that those who con- 
sult it must perforce clear their own 
minds in explaining their difficulties. 
At any rate, on another August day, 
Evelyn found herself at the latter end 
of an afternoon again surveying herself 
in the mirror. It had been a bad day 
in the Kingston library; hot and air- 
less, and there had been an extraordi- 
nary number of telephone calls which 
had demanded patience and skill.

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


All on a Summer’s Day 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 91] 


Nevertheless her spirits rose when 
James came for her and after an early 
dinner in an air-cooled restaurant they 
went to the park. To their uncritical 
eyes people strolling in thin bright 
summer clothes seemed delightful and 
they added themselves to their number 
with pleasure. The clear light was 
still in the sky when they settled 
themselves on a bench near one of the 
refreshment stands. Evelyn looked 
with her usual satisfaction at the 
varied customers of the stand and 
James looked at Evelyn. 


&.\. THEY TALKED of the agreeable 
Y/Y items of their day; they sat in com- 
panionable silence and watched the 
passers-by. They were aware of that 
trivial and yet vivid happiness that 
sometimes comes of a passing moment 
when existence seems at once peaceful 
and lively, and they smiled at each 
other contentedly. James, not given 
to literary allusions, was struck with 
Evelyn’s look of a kind of undis- 
turbed innocence, ‘“‘child of the 
pure unclouded brow,’’ he thought, 
vaguely conscious of having read it 
somewhere and suddenly thinking 
it apt to her beyond anyone he had 
ever seen. He thought of his life 
with Louisa and how little she had 
ever suggested any such ideas to 
anyone who looked at her and then 
his train of thought led him further. 
What was there of the past in this 
girl and this moment? What non- 
sense to think that with her he need 
ever consider such expediencies as be- 
ing master of his household. Here in 
this hot and sultry park with her was 
more peace than any house deep in 


the most lush country, could bring. 

Evelyn feeling his glance upon her 
turned toward him and smiled. As she 
did so she was conscious of a warm 
rush of feeling. Dear James, why did 
she resist his ideas and argue with him 
so? It was not the white paint and the 
green shutters that had made the house 
at Bangor a prison but the uneasy 
spirit that inhabited it. With James 
no house, however far from human 
habitation it might lie, however it 
might repeat remembered patterns of 
unhappy things, would ever be any- 
thing but a place to be happy in. Her 
mother had tried to please her father 
because she feared his tempers and his 
arbitrary ways; James had the aim of a 
child who wants others to have a 
good time because he is having one. 

She leaned forward and uttered her 
discovery. 

“James, I’d love a house in the 
country,’’ she said softly, but James 
who never interrupted had spoken 
simultaneously, ‘‘ Evelyndarling, can't 
we get married this very summer and 
live here where we found each other?”’ 

They laughed. 


AS IT was James’ plan that they 

finally adopted, it was to a city 
address that Mrs. Taylor a few weeks 
later dispatched a package. ‘‘The 
check I sent you,’’ she wrote, ‘‘was 
just one of those auntly gifts but this 
is for fun.”’ 

It was the tinted etching, the lovely 
Mary Cassatt that James and Evelyn 
agreed—for reasons not altogether 
plain—had marked the turning point 
in their lives, the thing. that really 
brought them together.

wearing a set of antlers, but the 
artist did not dare to tamper with 
the feelings of his public. The tragic 
note was quickly silenced. The dog 
opened an eye to reassure the audience 
as Mickey rushed into a close-up and 
cried, ‘Is there a doctor in the house?”’ 

Whea Charlie Chaplins became too 
common in Kansas City Disney took 
a partner and appeared on amateur 
nights in a singing and patter act. At 
fifteen years, he got a job as peanut 
butcher on trains running out of 
Kansas City. Later in that year, 1917, 
the family moved to Chicago where 
Walt attended high school for a year. 


t.\ IN CHICAGO an acute conflict 
PO aeiilooe between his ambition 
to be an actor and his ambition to be 
an artist. He did illustrations for the 
high school magazine and studied car- 
tooning at night at the Chicago Acad- 
emy of Fine Arts; at the same time he 
and a friend worked up an imitation of 
Weber and Fields and sought unsuccess- 
fully to go onthe amateur stage. He 
also tried to break into two other fields 
of public entertainment. He sent for 
all kinds of books on magic and sleight- 
of-hand but could not find the time for 
practice in these arts. He paid the first 
installment on a small cinema outfit 
and planned to produce pictures writ- 
ten and acted by juveniles for juve- 
niles, but he could not raise the money 
to buy films or to pay the second in- 
stallment on the camera, which was 
repossessed by the company. 

In the summer of 1918, Walt, then 


sixteen years old, needed a job and ap- 
plied for a position as letter carrier. He 
said that he was seventeen years old. 
His conscience would not let him lie 
by more than one year, and he was re- 
jected as being too young. He went 
home, put on his father’s clothes, 
deepened his voice, hardened his con- 
science and reapplied for the position, 
saying he was eighteen years old. This 
time he was hired. He was rejected on 
account of his youth at army and navy 
recruiting stations and at a Canadian 
enlistment office, but in September, 
1918, he joined the Red Cross as a 
chauffeur. He arrived in France after 
the armistice but served for a year as 
chauffeur for Red Cross officials. 

Returning from France he worked 
as a commercial artist in Kansas City, 
drawing illustrations for advertise- 
ments in farm journals . . . pictures of 
happy cows eating patent salt blocks, 
of triumphant hens hatching out dol- 
lars after eating patent hen foods, and 
oil wells spouting seas of money. 

It was pure accident that started 
Disney at work on animated drawings. 
By answering an advertisement in a 
newspaper he got a position with the 
Kansas City Slide Company, which 
formerly made stereopticon slides and 
had recently been making advertising 
cattoons for motion picture projec- 
tions. Disney’s first animated cartoon 
pictured a steamboat propelled by a 
series of nervous twitches. Human 
figures were activated at that time like 
stop-go signals. Paper arms and legs

Mickey Mouse 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 92] 


were pinned to a paper torso. The 
figure was photographed in one atti- 
tude, the arms and legs were then 
moved, the second photograph taken 
and so on, the animation being very 
jittery. Walt induced his company to 
get away from the Mexican jumping 
bean effect by drawing a fresh cartoon 
for each photograph. 


DISNEY was only nineteen years 

old when he made his first indepen- 
dent production, but the haphazard ex- 
periences and enthusiasms of his early 
years had furnished him with an 
ideal equipment for his life work. He 
owed very little to schooling. His im- 
pressions were all first hand; he had 
never peered at life through the spec- 
tacles of books. Recently, when Dis- 
ney was discussing his rules for keeping 
Mickey Mouse true to his own charac- 
ter and for avoiding any violations of 
the laws of nature as they exist in 
Cartoonland, an interviewer said, 
“That's a paraphrase of Hamlet’s ad- 
vice to the players.’’ Disney said that 
he had never seen or read Hamlet, but 
that he had had a vague idea that it 
was a part ham actors liked to play. 
He had never heard of holding the 
mirror up to nature. Disney is as free 
as Al Smith from the taint of book- 
learning; no man could be clearer of 
the curse of sophistication. 

Disney's first independent effort was 
an animated weekly saga of the news 
events of Kansas City. This ran for six 
months. Its success inspired him to 
attempt Little Red Riding-hood. He 
worked on it for six months, using his 
father’s garage as a studio. Goldi- 
locks, The Town Musicians of Bremen 
and then children’s tales followed. 
Walt, who had just come of age, then 
formed a corporation, hired a staff of 
artists and started to work on a large 
scale; but his distributing agency went 
into bankruptcy and failed to pay him 
for his films. This gave him his first 
introduction to business trouble, which 
has since become an old friend. The 
crash in Kansas City made him decide 
to goto Hollywood. To get the money 
for the trip he wrote a song film for a 
theater organist and made motion pic- 
tures of the offspring of his friends. 

He arrived in Hollywood in August, 
1923, with one suit of clothes, very 
ancient and greatly patched. Walt and 
his brother Roy, who furnished capi- 
tal of two hundred and fifty dollars, 
set up a studio in Hollywood. They 
lived for months on a starvation diet 
with the sheriff at their heels. Finally 
an eastern film distributor became in- 
terested in him and Walt started his 
series called Alice in Cartoonland 
which was a combination of a flesh- 
and-blood heroine with a supporting 
company of pen-and-ink characters, 
the photographs of the girl being 
superimposed on the photographs of 
the cartoons. After making sixty 
Alice films Disney started to produce 
Oswald the Rabbit. Oswald was a 
great success and Disney ventured to 
ask a higher price for his work. The 
distributor’s reply was to raid Dis- 
ney’s staff and start making his own 
Oswalds. 

This was the first of three raids 
which have been made on Disney's 
studio. Many film producers had the 
theory that Walt Disney was not par- 
ticularly important to his cartoons. 
Their idea was that to duplicate Dis- 
ney’s success it was only necessary to 
capture some of his able workers. This 


theory has not justified itself. Disney 
has survived three almost complete 
changes in the personnel of his organi- 
zation and his pictures have steadily 
increased in popular favor. 

Disney was in New York when he 
had the trouble over Oswald the 
Rabbit. He then decided to make a 
new series and distribute it through 
some other company. The idea of 
Mickey Mouse was forced on him by 
process of elimination because the cat, 
dog, rabbit and other feasible animals 
were already cartoon heroes. Disney 
also had a sentimental attachment to 
mice because of the friendship which 
had sprung up between him and a 
mouse which used to drop around and 
play on his desk in the advertising 
office in Kansas City. It occurred to 
Disney that if he used a mouse hero he 
had a magnificent made-to-order vil- 
lain in the cat, with unlimited possi- 
bilities of dramatic conflict. Of course 
Mickey is a mouse in name only; he is 
a biped that wears shoes and has a 
human soul. Walt, with the help of 
his wife, the former Lillian Bounds, 
wrote the first Mickey scenario on the 
train from New York to Hollywood. 
For a long time Mickey was closely 
guarded against the kidnapers in which 
Hollywood abounded; any new brain 
child was then likely to be abducted 
from its rightful parents. It was con- 
sidered dangerous to let the mouse idea 
become known even at the studio, and 
the first Mickey picture was made ina 
garage used by Walt and his brother. 


AS THE new cartoon character 

was coming into existence, Al Jol- 
son was revolutionizing motion pic- 
tures by the success of his singing in 
The Jazz Singer. Walt decided to gam- 
ble with the new medium and took his 
picture, which was entitled Steam- 
boat Willie, to New York to be fitted 
up with music and sound effects. 

The synchronization of Steamboat 
Willie was one of the major ordeals of 
Disney’s life. The art of matching 
noise to action was then in great con- 
fusion. Effects came before causes, 
thunder preceded lightning, dull 
thuds resounded before objects fell. 
A musical director was engaged who 
had had experience in recording music 
for feature films, but this experience 
was a hindrance rather than a help. 
He knew how to set the pace for his 
musicians in an ordinary screen play, 
but the action in Steamboat Willie was 
so rapid that the orchestra had played 
only half of the musical accompani- 
ment before the film was finished. 

Disney got a metronome for the 
guidance of the musicians, but the 
director scorned to use it. ‘* What am 
I here for?’’ he demanded. ‘‘Am Ia 
machine?’’ Again the orchestra tried 
to keep its music abreast the high 
speed action of Steamboat Willie. It 
was like a group of nervous amateurs 
shooting at quail; the musicians blazed 
away too early and too late. This 
wild performance was repeated again 
and again. The weather was hot, the 
musical director was fat and he melted 
away in his effort to get the sound and 
action on speaking terms. Finally 
Disney induced him to let the orches- 
tra keep time by watching a film on 
which a bounding ball acted as a 
metronome. Even then the musicians 
were distracted by watching Steam- 
boat Willie and finally they were 
turned with their backs to the picture.

Woman’s Home Companion July 1934 


Mickey 


Mouse 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 93] 


The results at last were pretty satis- 
factory. Disney found it necessary, 
however, to spend a whole night 
clipping out bits of film here and there 
in order to bring the music and noises 
into their true relation to the action. 

Mickey Mouse made his début in 
Steamboat Willie at the Colony 
Theatre on September 28, 1928, and 
was an enormous success. The audi- 
ence went wild when he played a 
xylophone solo on a cow's teeth and 
gave a concert with a set of dishpans. 
There was a great variety of sound ef- 
fects all pretty well timed. Disney's 
next picture was The Skeleton’s Dance, 
his first Silly Symphony. This was re- 
jected by many motion picture ex- 
hibitors as being gruesome, but 
audiences loved the jolly skeletons as 
they loved the jaunty mouse. After 
breaking all precedent by playing an 
encore at Roxy's, The Skeleton’s 
Dance became a cartoon classic. After 
five years it is still playing and has 
earned more money than any other 
“short,’’ unless the lead has recently 
been taken by Three Little Pigs. 

When making the first Mickey 
Mouse pictures Disney threw in all the 
wild gags he could think of. Later, 
however, Mickey began to assume a 
definite personality, and Disney would 
reject good comedy ideas, saying, 
‘Mickey isn’t like that.’’ Today the 
mouse has matured, as Mr. Pickwick 
did, from a madly farcical comic to a 
being with a consistent though dizzy 
psychology. Disney will not for ex- 
ample permit what he calls ‘‘camera 
puns’’ in connection with his cele- 
brated creation. A rival used this gag: 
a lady character in an animated car- 
toon presses a handkerchief to her 
mouth and her lips are printed on the 
handkerchief in lipstick; the impres- 
sion of the lips then delivers a mono- 
logue. This was a ‘‘camera pun”’ 
which was highly rewarded with 
laughter, but such devices are held to 
be unworthy of the dignity of Mickey 
Mouse. He is not permitted to be 
cruel or arrogant except in his dealings 
with villains. If he is vainglorious one 
moment he is deflated in the next. 
Extreme vigilance is exercised to keep 
him from becoming a smart Alec. Too 
much whimsey and fantasy are objec- 
tionable. In Disney’s own words 
there should be ‘‘just enough exagger- 
ation to take the characters out of the 
real and into the imaginary; it must 
be natural to be effective.” 


&.\ ONE OF Mickey's comparative 

failures was as a violinist. He 
fiddled divinely, but he fiddled too 
much to suit the audience. Since then it 
has been a rule that Mickey must never 
be allowed to be arty. The hands of 
Mickey Mouse and the rest of his 
physique are the work of other artists, 
but the voice is the voice of Disney. 
Walt started squeaking out the Mickey 
Mouse dialogues in the early days and 
has stuck to it for a good reason: if an 
actor or announcer were hired to speak 
Mickey's lines, the man might be 
hired away and it would disturb 
fans to have Mickey's voice change 
from time to time. 

There are seventy-five artists em- 
ployed at the Disney studios, most of 
them working on animated cartoons. 
A few others turn out comic strips and 
designs for Mickey Mouse novelties. 
Nearly fifteen thousand separate draw- 
ings are made for each film. The back- 
grounds are drawn separately and 


remain on paper. Mickey and the 
other animated characters are drawn 
on sheets seven by nine inches and 
then traced on celluloid. The cellu- 
loid is superimposed on the paper back- 
grounds and then photographed. After 
one picture is taken, the celluloid 
drawing is removed, a new celluloid 
action picture is superimposed on the 
background and the camera clicks 
again. When Technicolor is used, the 
color is painted on thousands of draw- 
ings and transferred to film by the 
color cameras. 


EVEN AFTER the success of 

Steamboat Willie and The Skele- 
ton’s Dance the Disneys had a difficult 
time. It was a slow process to equip 
theaters with talking picture appara- 
tus. Many exhibitors doubted whether 
a mouse had the stuff of which popular 
heroes are made, but audiences never 
objected to Mickey on that account. 
For some reason or other the mouse, 
though unfavorably regarded in real 
life, has been kindly treated in art and 
letters from the time of A/sop to that of 
Robert Burns and later. Even after 
Mickey had been established as a 
world character, the Disneys were 
still working their way through peril- 
ous financial shoals. Only four years 
ago Walt and Roy sold their jointly 
owned automobile to meet their pay- 
roll and for six months thereafter they 
walked to work. Their claim is that 
the value of the animated cartoon has 
never been adequately recognized. 
When it was reported a few months 
ago that the profits of the Three Little 
Pigs had passed the million-dollar 
mark, the Disneys examined their 
books and reported that the profits 
from that record-breaking film were 
then less than four thousand dollars. 
It will have a long run here and abroad 
and they estimate that its net earn- 
ings will eventually be about forty- 
five thousand dollars. Technicolor has 
recently proved an important factor in 
winning recognition for the Silly Sym- 
phonies. Walt and his brother have 
been afraid of what they call *‘commer- 
cializing the business.’ Both of them 
have turned deaf ears to promoters who 
have approached them with dazzling 
schemes for selling stock, refinancing 
and expanding, but the idea of having 
Mickey Mouse bossed by Wall Street 
is horrifying to the Disneys. 

Because of the prejudice of the 
theater chains against paying hand- 
some sums for short films, Walt is 
eager to make a long feature in ani- 
mated color cartoons. Fan mail runs 
strongly in favor of a full-length Alice 
in Wonderland in this medium. Others 
think The Wizard of Oz is made to 
order for the animated cartoon. James 
Thurber has demanded in stern accents 
that Disney, putting aside business 
and excuses, proceed to make the 
Odyssey. 

The difficulties in handling the ani- 
mated cartoon increase geometrically 
with the length of the film, and the 
experiments suggested would all be 
heavy gambles. Walt Disney is con- 
vinced that tragedy and triangles and 
other heavy stuff cannot be conveyed 
by his medium. The ability of the 
animator to express varieties of emo- 
tion has, however, increased enor- 
mously in the Jast three or four years. 
Disney is eager to attempt an ambi- 
tious opus in the comic adventure field. 
He believes that the animated cartoon 
is only at the beginning of its career.

whose discovery ten years

NTELLIGENCE is a pretty good thing 

to have in this world, and a good 
many people who use it find it makes 
their lives much easier. One fine ex-

George F. L. 
Bryant, a well- 
known school 
teacher, of 24 
Elm Street, 
Fairfield, 
Maine. Mr. 
Bryant writes 
us that ten 
years ago he 
had what the 
doctor said was 
colitis. Being a 
school teacher 
he was not get- 
ting the proper 
amount of exercise, and he tried va- 
rious kinds of medicines, which re- 
lieved him for a time, but nothing 
gave him permanent results until he 
discovered Nujol. 


“T have tried several other brands,” 
writes Mr. Bryant, “but there is noth- 
ing I have found equal to Nujol in 
effectiveness. The density of it ap- 
pears to be just right, and Nujol has 
no unpleasant taste. It is the only 
thing my youngster of thirteen will 
take. He has used Nujol since he was 
three years old. He is strong, and well 
—and I think Nujol has a great deal 
to do with his excellent health. 


“Fact is, the whole family uses 
Nujol. When we go on a camping trip 
our appetites are enormous, and then 
is the time when Nujol becomes the 
most important thing in our medicine 
chest. 


“You can publish this letter and 
this picture of me if you want to, if 
you think it may do somebody else 
some good.” 


Indeed we do think it may do some- 
body else some good. This ten-year 
record of father and son certainly 
proves that Nujol is harmless used 
over a long period of years, and that 
it can’t do you anything but good! 
Like most worthwhile discoveries, this 
is so simple it seems too good to be 
true. 


Follow Mr. Bryant’s example. Use 
Nujol yourself—bring up your chil- 
dren on it to be regular as clockwork. 
Nujol now comes in two forms, plain, 
and Cream of Nujol, the latter fla- 
vored and often preferred by chil- 
dren. You can get Nujol at any drug 
store. 


What is your Nujol story? If you 
have been using Nujol for ten years 
or more, if you are bringing up your 
children on it, tell us. Address Stanco 
Incorporated, Dept. M-14, 2 Park 
Avenue, New York City.

Muss Piper Passes 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 16] 


And even as she spoke the car 
slowed down, then stopped quite dead 
in the middle of what was later (much 
later) found to be an abandoned road 
leading to an abandoned quarry. 


WHEN SHE returned to the Hall 

in a taxi at one-thirty in the 
morning, sitting next to the driver for 
the sake of company, Miss Piper was 
greeted by an agitated host and hos- 
tess. ‘‘Really, Miss Piper—’’ Mrs. 
Mandeville began. 

But Miss Piper, thumping her re- 
assuringly on the shoulder, said, 
‘There, there, Charlotte! Cheer up! 
The car’s somewhere near a place 
called Higgleston, but the farmer said 
he'd notify the nearest garage in the 
morning, so they'll soon get it back to 
you. It isn’t wrecked; just seems to 
have been tampered with, or some- 
thing. Would you mind paying Her- 
bert? I’m cleaned out. You ought to 
go to Higgleston sometime, Charlotte. 
Charming country. Quite unspoiled. 
It’s so heartening, isn’t it, in this 
civilized age to come on a primitive 
patch, miles from anywhere?” 

The next morning Miss Piper came 
into breakfast and assiduously read 
the paper from porridge to marmalade. 
Especially did she read the left-hand 
side of the second page: 

“Last night at the Ambassador 
Hermione Nettle’s place in Treasure 
Trove was taken by a hitherto un- 
known actress, Rosalie Ross. Young 
Miss Ross, after a somewhat nervous 
start before an audience frankly dis- 
appointed by the absence of the star, 
soon got into her stride and gave an 
extraordinarily vivacious and com- 
petent performance. Outstandingly 
successful was her singing of ‘Will 
you, won't you’ which she was called 
upon to repeat three times.” 


And later on in the morning Cyril 
received from town a special-delivery 
letter which was promptly relayed to 
Mr. Mandeville, then to Mrs. Mande- 
ville, and after some hesitation, to 
Miss Piper—prowling about inter- 
estedly on the outskirts of the group. 


Dear Cyril: 

I have been thinking everything 
over and have decided that it would 
be a mistake for us to get married. 
We live such different kinds of lives 
and I don’t think I should ever be able 
to fit into yours. I mean although I’ve 
got to work so terribly hard and never 
know if I'm going to get anywhere 
anyway, I guess I'd feel pretty flat if 
I didn’t do anything. So let’s say 
good-by. I think it was lovely of you 
to ask me to marry you, and I'll al- 
ways remember it. My best wishes. 

RosaLig. 


Miss Piper said, ‘‘Well, well.” 
Then she went pelting upstairs to her 
own room; and an hour or so after- 
ward she came ee down again 
into their midst followed by Hunter 
with all her bags. 

“Just time to make the next train,” 
she shouted cheerily. ‘‘Good-by, 
Charlotte! Good-by, John!’’ She 
embraced them all vigorously; then 
tore out to the car, where the dogs were 
waiting in melancholy silence, their 
tails between their legs. 

Mr. and Mrs. Mandeville hurried 
after her. ‘‘But, Miss Piper—’’ 

‘““Good-by, good-by,’’ Miss Piper 
shouted from the front seat. ‘‘Don’t 
get into any more pickles. Good-by, 
old boy! Good-by, old girl! Cheer 
up! I'll come back again soon!” 

Then Hunter started the car and off 
she went leaving behind in the autumn 
sunshine a small motionless group of 
moping dogs. 


A Recipe for Swimming

surface and driving the swimmer for- 
ward. He should repeat this kick- 
ing practice at intervals until step 14. 

Step 9. Sranpinc Up Arter Front 
Froat. There are four distinct parts 
to getting to the feet after a front 
float: 1. Make the body into a ball 
by drawing the knees to the chest. 
2. Pull downward with the hands, 
rolling the body to a squatting posi- 
tion. 3. Put the feet on the pool bot- 
tom. 4. Raise the head. If the knees 
are not drawn up first or if the head is 
not kept down until the last, there 
will be difficulty. The pupil floats 
holding the rail, then goes through 
the four steps in order, without taking 
his hands from the rail. 

Step to. Rerease Ram Berore 
Sranpinc Up. The pupil floats hold- 
ing the rail, then releases the rail and 
goes through the four parts of getting 
to his feet. It is now he reaps the bene- 
fit of practice in gripping the water. 

Step 11. Push AND FLoat To THE 
Ratz. The pupil stands in waist-deep 
water facing the pool wall and about 
four feet from it. He bends forward, 
takes a breath, puts his face in the 
water, reaches for the rail and falls 
forward. His hands reach the rail al- 
most before his feet leave the bottom. 
He then stands five feet from the rail 
at the start; soon he will be starting 
ten or fifteen feet out. 


Step 12. Stanp Up Berore ReacninG 
Ratt. The pupil pushes toward the 
rail as in step 11, but gets to his feet 
before he reaches it. If he feels unable 
to stand up in open water he can 
simply float to the rail and get up 
there. If he does have trouble he 
should practice further on step to. 

Step 13. Front Pusn-orr. The pupil 
stands in, waist-deep water about 
eighteen tnches from the pool wall 
with his back to it. He stands on his 
right foot and places the sole of his 
left foot against the pool wall behind 
his right knee. He then bends for- 
ward, takes a breath, puts his face in 
the water, reaches his hands out in 
front and pushes with his left leg. He 
should not kick the wall, but give ita 
quick firm push. He goes into the 
front-float position and after a little 
practice will be able to coast fifteen or 
twenty feet before standing up. 

Step 14. Pusu-orr anv Kicx. The 
pupil does a front push-off, then starts 
the crawl kick he has been practicing. 
The kicking should increase his coast- 
ing distance by ten or fifteen feet. Of 
course he must not coast into deep 
water.

The pupil does step 14, then, after his 
kicking is well started, pulls his left 
arm, elbow straight, directly ‘below

A Recipe for Swimming 


[CONTINUED FROM PAGE 95 | 


him and back until it reaches his left 
hip. Then he continues kicking with 
his right arm in the front-float posi- 
tion and his left by his side. 

It is very important that he start 
kicking before he takes the stroke and 
that he continue kicking after he takes 
it. If the kick doesn’t become auto- 
matic now he will forget it later when 
he has more to think about, and that 
is bad. A crawl swimmer never drowns 
without first letting his feet sink. He 
does not go under water in a prone 
position; his feet sink, he raises his 
arms instinctively toward the light 
and their weight forces his body under 
water. Therefore the pupil must prac- 
tice the kick until it is automatic, 
which will mean that he can never 
be drowned until he is either uncon- 
scious or too exhausted to kick. 

Step 16. Kick anp Boru Arms. The 
pupil kicks, pulls with his left arm as 
in step 15, then immediately pulls 
with his right arm, at the same time 
swinging his left arm above the water 
back to the front-float position. Then 
he continues kicking with his left 
arm in the float position and his right 
arm by his side. He must kick be- 
fore, during and after the strokes. 

Step 17. Kick, One Arm anv Ex- 
HALE. The pupil does step 15. As he 
makes the last half of his arm-pull, he 
exhales vigorously through his nose. 
He takes only one arm-pull, and he 
doesn't inhale until he stands up. 

Step 18. Kick, Born Arms AND 
Breatue. The pupil does step 17. 
Then he continues by taking a right 
arm-pull as in step 16 and as his left 
hand leaves the water by his hip he 
rolls his head to the left until he is 
looking over his left shoulder. In 
this position he inhales through his 
mouth. As his left hand reénters the 
water he turns his face back toward 
the bottom and holds his breath until 
his left arm-pull is half finished, when 
he again exhales. Thus he continues. 
He must not raise his head to the front 
in inhaling; he must merely turn it 
and look over his left shoulder. He 
must not inhale through his nose, for 
a few drops of water will clog his nose 
whereas he can inhale through his 
mouth even if it’s half full of water. 


> THE pupil is able to swim 


crawl; he’s probably pretty 
clumsy at it, but he knows it well 
enough so that he will improve with 
practice. 

He should not try swimming in deep 
water—particularly not without the 
presence of a competent life guard— 
until he knows a little more about 
handling himself in the water. Here's 
a safety hint: If any accident should 
occur, if the pupil should get out of 
his depth, remember that the surest 
way of helping him is by throwing 
something buoyant—a life-preserver, 
a board, an air-cushion, or even a 
wooden chair—rather than by trying 
to swim and rescue him. Unless you're 
a truly expert swimmer, don’t try to 
make a swimming rescue if any other 
kind can be made. But there won’t be 
any emergency. 

The pupil can either be left to play 
and swim in shallow water until he 
unconsciously acquires some degree of 
skill or he can be taught it. What he 
needs to know before he tries swim- 
ming in deep water is how to stop.and 
rest; so far he knows how to swim, 
but if he became tired or panicky he 


wouldn't know what to do. When he 
has absorbed the substance of the fol- 
lowing five steps he will be able to 
stop and rest any time he wants to. 
Step 19. Back Froatr. The pupil sits 
down in water that is knee-deep. He 
lays his arms straight out on either 
side like the top of a T, lets his head 
drop back, fills his lungs and slowly 
straightens his body. He will come to 
a horizontal position at the surface. 
If his feet do not float, he can raise 
them and keep them up by doing the 
crawl kick gently. He tilts his head 
back so that his nose and mouth will 
be highest; not all his head can be 
kept above water, and these are the 
important parts. If he tries to hold 
his entire head high, instead of merely 
tilting the top of it back and down, 
he will only succeed in forcing his en- 
tire face under water. He must simply 
relax and let the water hold him. 
Step 20. Sranpinc Up Arter Back 
Froat. The action of getting to the 
feet after a back float is this: 1. Drop 
the hips; sit down in the water. 2. 
Scoop back and down with the hands, 
bringing the body to a true sitting 
position. 3. Stand up. The pupil 
floats on his back in waist-deep water 
close to the pool wall, then gets to his 
feet in the manner already described. 
If he is unable to do it, he can take 
hold of the pool rail and help himself. 
When he has got up successfully several 
times near the wall, he tries it in the 


middle of the pool. 


4_\ THE MOST natural way to try to 

float is by lowering the hips and 
raising the head: it can’t be done that 
way. Conversely, the most natural 
way to try to stand up after floating is 
by lowering the feet and raising the 
hips and torso: it won't work. To 
float, keep hips and chin high; to 
stand up, sit down. 

Step 21. Roxt~inG From Craw To 
Back Fioat. The pupil is swimming. 
When he wants to stop and rest, he 
merely continues a breathing-roll until 
he is on his back. He looks over his left 
shoulder as in inhaling, drops his right 
shoulder completely under his left, 
and rolls onto his back. Once there, 
he can rest as long as he likes. 

Step 22. RoiitinG From Back Froar 
TO SwIMMING Position. The pupil is 
floating on his back and wants to start 
swimming. First he turns his left 
hand palm-down and his right palm- 
up. Next he starts kicking, so his feet 
won't sink. Last, he pushes down 
with his left hand and pulls up with his 
right; his left shoulder rolls over his 
right, and he is in swimming position. 
He must be sure to start his kick before 
he rolls, so that his feet won’t sink. 

Step 23. Swim, Froat, Swim. The 
pupil pushes off from the pool wall, 
swims a few strokes, rolls over and 
floats on his back, rolls again into 
swimming position and swims on. 

Now your pupil is ready for deep 
water. There must absolutely be a 
competent life guard at hand the first 
time he goes out of his depth; the 
guard will not only guard him but will 
give him encouragement and keep him 
from panic. 

Through your efforts, now, there is 
one more person in the world who 
knows how to exercise and play in 
water; one person who has added a 
new and useful and fascinating achieve- 
ment to his list. 

Good for him! Good for you!
